Preface 


А dream of my childhood came true when І got an opportunity 
of visiting the snout of Gangotri glacier and deep snows of Hima- 
layan heights in 1978. This was my first mountaineering course 
from Nehru Institute of Mountaineering (NIM), Uttarkashi and the 
first exposure to Himalayan wilderness and solitude. At that time it 
was rare to find any trekker on the track. Today it is one of the 
busiest tracks and mountain climbing areas of India. This first ad- 
venture experience attracted and motivated me for many mountain 
climbing challenges in Himalaya. Later in 1981 I accepted a chal- 
lenge to climb Nanda Devi peak along with other eleven members. 
With the success of this expedition my ambition arose to participate 
in Everest climb in Solu-Khumbu district in Nepal. Although I was 
unable to climb the peak but region's economy and depleting ecol- 
ogy made a deep impression on my mind. 

Study of adventure tourism was more or less untouched by 
earlier scholars. Therefore, I desperately felt like going once again 
for Himalayan challenge for academic purpose. This study is the 
outcome of my extensive field surveys and interviews that I con- 
ducted in Uttarkashi and Chamoli districts of Garhwal. Due to non- 
availability of latest data, study is based on 1981 census records. 

I very much owe more than ceremonious words of thanks to 
Mr. Rattan Singh Chauhan, Senior Instructor, NIM, Uttarkashi, 
without whose Cooperation, encouragement and constructive sug- 
gestions at various stages, it would not have been possible to com- 
plete the work. I express my sincere thanks to Prof. R.C. Joshi, 
Head of the Department of Electronic & Computer Engineering, 
University of Roorkee for guiding me in the later stages of work. I 
also express my sincere thanks to Dr. M.C. Sanwal, Head of the

Introduction 


1.1 HistoricAL REVIEW OF Tourism 


Since time immemorial man had a thrust for wandering. The 
ancient Greeks had a remarkable wander lust. The Roman empire 
had the history of pilgrimages. The great navigators of the 15th and 
16th centuries expanded the horizon of the world, as compulsive 
voyagers and adventurers, they were rich people and had the mo- 
nopoly of travel in Europe. í 

Prior to the middle of the 18th century, nature and its beautiful 
but rugged objects had little appeal and mountains in particular 
were disliked and avoided. Until 1763 Switzerland was avoided. 
Climbing glaciers and snow peaks had not been introduced. Later 
on, few daring innovators had taken an interest in mountain climb- 
ing and tried to explore the high mountains of Alps. Gradually 
Chamonix became the target of adventures. This unique change of 
attitude and taste transformed Switzerland into the first play ground 
of Europe. Goethe declared, “Switzerland at first made so deep 
impression on me that I was bewildered and restless.”! The con- 
cept towards mountains changed and people began to feel an irre- 
sistible urge to travel to the mountains. 

In the beginning of the 10th century rich people started visiting 
Switzerland for acquiring health and knowledge and gradually that 
attitude changed and they felt an inner urge to visit and enjoy na- 
ture’s bounty. In 1811, with the ascent of the Jungfrau, sport of 
alpinism began and grew rapidly. Alpine societies were founded in 
Austria, Germany and Switzerland. Mountain chalets and footpaths 


- СС-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


HE Co 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai апа eGangotri_ 


18 Tourism in Garhwal Himalaya 


encouraged Alpine adventures. The first mountain hut was con- 
structed in 1865 in Kaprun, Salzburg as per the recorded evidence. 
The end of the 19th century and the early 20th century were the 
periods of strong growth in mountain tourism. Alps became the 
centre for summer as well as winter tourism. 

Walking on snow and crossing the glaciers was not unknown to 
Indians. The 3500 kms long majestic Himalaya on the northern bor- 
der has always held a strong fascination for men. The Himalaya 
offers challenges, inspiration, sublimity, relaxation and satisfaction. 
The Vedas (written some 5000 years ago), the ancient literary mas- 
terpieces are full of praises to Himalaya, which reverentially was 
and is believed to have proximity to the Gods. The Hindu religious 
books—Purans, Bhagvada Gita and Ramayana—all are full of 
praises to Himalaya, the *Abode of Gods", where Gods lived and 
mortals meditated for salvation. According to Hindu mythology the 
liquid essence of life, Ganga, descended from heaven to earth in 
Himalaya. Therefore, our holy men took the journey to Himalaya, 
hence it had become the place of pilgrimage for the devout among 
Hindus, Sikhs and Buddhists since centuries. 

Since the dawn of the civilisation Indians have been attracted 
by the hazardous mountains and established places of pilgrimage 
there, like Badrinath, Kedarnath, Gangotri, Yamunotri, Hemkund- 
Lokpal, Vaishnu Devi and Amarnath etc. Perhaps most notable 
among the pilgrims was Adi-Guru Shankaracharya, who about a 
thousand years ago came from the south to revive Hinduism and 
established the shrine of Badrinath. 

Economic necessities drove the hillmen to travel in the moun- 
tains as a way of life as hunters, traders, shepherds, and porters. 
They had to cross high passes (5538-5846 m). In this process they 
invented indigenous equipments, techniques and clothing for such 
adverse conditions and difficult climbs. Among local explorers in 
the Survey of India (1865-85) were Pandit brothers—Nain Singh, 
Kishan Singh and Ata Mohammed. They were sent by the British to 
the cold desert of Tibet and adjacent countries for survey. 

Adventure activities in the Himalaya were first tried by the 
Europeans, mainly Britishers. For this, the region was explored by 
Trail, Tillman, Huge Ruttledge, Shipton and Smythe. This started 


with the crossing of 16,500 ft pass by Trail in 1830, which is | 


СС-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Sw 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


Introduction 19 


known as the “Trail pass”. Later on it was crossed by Adolf Sch- 
lagintweit in 1855, by Edmund Smythe in 1861, by T.S. Kennedy 
in 1883, and later by others. But the awakening of interest in moun- 
taineering as a sport in India came with the extensive trek of W.W. 
Graham in 1883 with two Swiss guides Emill Boss, Ulrich 
Kauffmann who tried a route up the Rishi gorge to the western base 
of Nanda Devi. Later Graham attempted Dunagiri (7134 m) and 
made it. In 1855 Schlagintweit brothers attempted Kamet. Their 
approach was from Tibet. 

In the 19th century three areas of Himalaya namely the Kara- 
koram, Garhwal-Kumaon and Sikkim attracted westem mountain- 
eers and explorers very much. In 1905 came the wizard of Nanda 
Devi story, Dr. T.G. Longstaff with Alexi and Henri, two Alpine 
guides. They reached a col (5875 m), which is known as Longstaff 
col. Again in 1907 Longstaff came with G.C. Bruce, A.L. Mumm 
and three Alpine guides and reached the Bagini pass (6060 m). 
Later Longstaff made the ascent of Trisul (7188 m) in 1907 with a 
local inhabitant Karbir Singh from the north, the highest point 
reached till then. С.Е. Meade, another mountaineer attempted the 
Kamet thrice (in 1910, 1912 and 1913) but couldn’t climb higher 
than a pass (7203 m), which was later named as Meade’s col. 

Then came a flood of explorers and mountaineers towards 
Nanda Devi. In 1926 Hugh Ruttledge came with colonel D.C. 
Wilson and Howard Somervell, but couldn’t succeed in penetrating 
the sanctuary. In 1931, with the ascent of Kamet (7755 m) by a 
British team led by Smythe, mountaineers started concentrating on 
Nanda Devi. This beautiful ice-sheathed abode of Goddess Nanda 
Started taking test of endurance and ability of mountaineers. But, 
till 1933, its base surrounded by the double-walled amphitheater of 
peaks remained impenetrable. 

Finally in 1934 came the gate crashers, Eric Shipton and Bill 
Tillman, the first mortals to wander at will in the sanctuary of 
Goddess Nanda. Ultimately on 24th Aug, 1936 from the bivouac at 
7385 m, Tillman and Odell got success in reaching the summit of 
Nanda Devi (7817 m), after a nine-hour climb. 

The Indians used to go as helpers, porters and agents, but they 
achieved only credibility. Smythe wrote much about Kesar Singh, a 
local porter, “there аге more of his type in the village of northern

Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai апа eGangotri 


20 Tourism in Garhwal Himalaya 


Garhwal, and given the same opportunities as the Darjeeling men, 
they would, I am convinced, develop into even finer mountaineers, 
finer at all events in that they would not be cursed on mountain 
with unnerving superstitions.’’? In between 1935-36, larger sections 
of Kumaon and Garhwal were completely surveyed by G.H. 
Osmaston assisted by R.A. Gardiner and R.G. Edge of Survey of 
India. In 1938 six Germans climbed Bhagirathi II, Chandra Parbat, 
Swachanda Parbat and Sri Kailash. The decade of fifties witnessed 
the golden era of mountaineering in Himalaya. In 1950 the French 
climbed the first 8000 m Annapuma I. The British got success in 
1953. In the same year Nanga Parbat was climbed by the Germans, 
Cho-oyu by Austrians, K2 by Italians, Kanchanjunga and Makalu 
were climbed іп 1955, Gasherbrum I & IV were climbed іп 1958. 

The success of Edmund Hillary and Tenzing on Everest 
changed the scenario of Indian mountaineering. Indian mountain- 
eering Foundation came into existence in 1961. The beginning of 
Indian mountaineering was on its threshold. The credit of creating 
interest of mountaineering among Indians goes to the Doon School, 
Dehradun. Eminent mountaineers like J.A.K. Martyn, J.T.M. 
Gibson and R.L. Holdsworth were teachers there. In 1940, 
Holdsworth took an exploratory trip of outer Himalaya with ten 
boys of Doon School. Nandu Jayal was one of them. The boys got a 
good exposure to Himalaya. In 1942 Holdsworth and Martyn took 
three teenagers to Badrinath, Mana village and Arwa valley. In 
1946 Nandu Jaya! Nandu Chengappa and others attempted 
Banderpunch peak. Due to bad weather they had to abandon their 
attempt of Banderpunch, but they climbed Hanuman (5610 m). 
Independence gave a new dimension to mountaineering in India. 

In 1950 Gibson led a successful expedition to Banderpunch. 
1951 brought laurels to Indian mountaineering with the successful 
expedition to Kamet, led by Gurudial Singh. The same year Nandu 
Jayal and Tenzing joined French Nanda Devi expedition. In 1953 
Nandu Jayal led an Indian Sappers expedition to Kamet and Abi 
Gamin. The team missed Kamet but climbed Abi Gamin. 

Real mountaineering started in India soon after the establish- 
ment of Himalayan Mountaineering Institute at Darjeeling in 1954. 
Nandu Jayal was the first principal and Tenzing was the first direc- 
tor of field training there. In 1956, Kamet expedition was organised

successfully with the Bengal Sappers. There were successful 
attempts on Saser Kangri and Nanda Devi too. In 1958 Indians 
attempted Cho-oyu with success, but unfortunately lost Nandu 
Jayal on the mountain. mare 

From successful sponsoring committee of Cho-oyu, Indian 
Mountaineering Foundation (IMF) became a permanent organisa- 
tion in 1961. The main objectives of IMF were to organise, support 
and provide a base for expeditions of mountaineering, skiing, rock- 
climbing, trekking at high altitudes as well as tO promote, encour- 
age, Support and execute schemes for all Himalayan adventures, 

In 1960 the first ladies basic mountaineering course was con- 
ducted at HMI, Darjeeling. With this an era of women mountain- 
eering began in India. In later years many expeditions to small and 
big peaks were successfully undertaken. The women’s expedition 
to Trisul in 1970 became a comer stone in the history of women 


mountaineering in India, when Dr. Meena Aggarwal 

peak with two sherpas. This was first 7000 m p beg by im 

Indian woman. In 1976 there was a joint Ind ЖАҚЫН 
expedition to Kamet and Abi Gamin. They/got success on Abi ^ ә 
Gamin. Thrity and Chander Prabha made tht it ЯН \ P 
Sherpas. Next year there was an all-India iex edition tov 7 a 
Kamet. Thrity Birdly, Chander Prabha and Bh е теа 
cess in reaching to the top. In 1979 there was joint d- 

New Zealand expedition, in which Chander Prabha with others 
climbed Rataban and experienced river-rafting on return journey 
upto Hardwar. 

In the mean time in 1981 it was decided to widen the horizons 
of IMF by including other kinds of adventures like—desert and 
jungle exploration, sea-ferrying, sailing, Sky-diving, hang-gliding, 
canoeing, cycle and motor-cycle tours, even exploration of polar 
regions, in short promotion and support of all kinds of adventure. 

In 1981 Indian girls with some men got a chance to go for the 
killer mountain’, Nanda Devi. Chander Prabha Aitwal, Harshvanti 
Bisht and Rekha Sharma climbed the peak. The ultimate in moun- 
taineering, the Everest, came in 1984 and Bachendri Pal climbed 
the peak. This infused the spirit of adventure among Indian women. 

IMF functions as a clearing house for foreign expeditions. It 
Tegisters the peaks for foreign expeditions and provides the service

of a liaison officer to them. Today there are many full-fledged 
training institutes of mountaineering in India viz, the Nehru Insti- 
tute of Mountaineering, Uttarkashi; Himalayan Mountaineering 
Institute, Darjeeling; Western Himalayan Mountaineering Institute, 
Manali; Jawahar Institute of Mountaineering and Winter Sports 
near Pahalgam; and Gujarat Rock Climbing Institute at Mount Abu. 

The number of Indian as well as foreign expeditions during 
these years has been growing. There are more than hundred moun- 
taineering clubs in almost all parts of India. In 1986 there were 
over 250 mountaineering expeditions and over 50,000 trekkers.? 

Though much has been achieved during these years, yet there 
is still much more to be done. There are hundreds of virgin sum- 
mits, miles and miles of unexplored forests, more and more diffi- 
cult ridges and unclimbed faces of peaks already climbed. The 
horizon of mountain tourism, specially mountaineering and trek- 
king is very wide, though most of the challenging peaks are already 
climbed, But many more thrilling challenges on the same peaks are 
inviting the adventurers today. New routes, new climbing styles 
and new techniques are to be tried. There are hundreds of new 
things yet to be done. The fascination to face the challenges of 
nature can never die because nature transforms its challenges every 
now and then. So Garhwal, the training ground of early mountain- 
eers, Can welcome many more mountaineers for many more new 
climbs, excitement, recreation, thrills and explorations. As pre- 
dicted by H. Kahn, the post-industrial society of tomorrow will be 
largely pleasure oriented. We have to make well planned prepara- 
tions, particularly in the Garhwal region right now to face the 
future when the problem will not be that of labour but of leisure. 

For this the whole of Garhwal has to get dressed up (infrastruc- 
ture building) to welcome new enthusiasts and mountain lovers for 
“social tourism’ and eco-tourism. 

Ever since the dawn of the civilisation man had a quest for 
wandering, but much of it was- unconscious in the beginning or it 
was for the fulfilment of necessities. Later on travelling for fun 
became the luxury of few rich people. But industrialisation, moder- 
nisation, spread of education, speedy communication system, in- 
creased disposable income, socialisation of leisure, monotonous 
characters of so many jobs and paid holidays changed the human

Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


Introduction 23 


life. This forced human beings for periodical refreshment of mind 
and body in wilderness areas for active Participation in exotic ac- 
tivities. This phenomenal change in tourism has been experienced 
world wide. Presently, tourism has been declared as an industry and 
its socio-economic and environmental impacts are visualising 
clearly. The greatest and the most Significant development in 
modern tourism is the demand for outdoor in mass recreation. To- 
day climbing, mountaineering, wildlife watching, snow-skiing, 
hale-skiing, water-skiing, river rafting and Canoeing have become 
popular. Most suitable for all these activities, Garhwal Himalaya, 
Started attracting tourists of different tastes and fashions. 


1.2 GARHWAL: AN INTRODUCTION 


The Garhwal Himalaya lies between the latitudes 29926'- 
31928” N and longitudes 77°49’-80°6’ E , with a total area of about 
30,000 sq. kms. In the east, the district boundaries of Nainital, 
Almora and Pithoragarh separate it from Kumaon in the west, river 
Tons separates it from the Himachal Pradesh. Starting from the 
foot-hills in the south, the region extends upto snow-clad peaks, 
making the Indo-Tibetan boundary. Politically the region consists 
the districts of Uttarkashi, Chamoli, Tehri, Pauri and Dehradun. 
First two being border districts and altitudinally higher than others, 
are most suitable for mountaineering and high altitude trekking. 
Therefore, in this book, two border districts have been studied. 

This new tourism has leaped into the Himalayan height of 
Uttarkashi and Chamoli districts of Garhwal with the establishment 
of Hill Area Development Board, U.P. in 1972. The natural beauty 
of the region—majestic Himalaya, snow-covered peaks, gushing 
Tivers, hanging glaciers, wildlife and pollution-free zone Started 
attracting tourists in the region. Gradually few restricted areas were 
Opened in Indo-Tibetan border for adventure activities. Holiday 
with pay, for adventure activities and subsidised mountaineering 
and Snow-skiing courses gave a chance to the countrymen to 
experience the thrills of adventure in the Himalaya. Char Dhams— 
Badrinath, Kedarnath, Gangotri and Yamunotri were the life-long 
ambition of every religious Hindu since the dawn of Hindu civilisa- 
Поп. Therefore іп the past, tourism trade had a tendency to be 


СС-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


EE 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai апа eGangotri 


24 Tourism in Garhwal Himalaya 


located in the area of religious or historical importance. But, today 
an amalgamation of adventure tourism and pilgrimage is taking 
place in the region. Uttarkashi and Chamoli districts of Garhwal, 
with deficient mineral resources, fragile eco-system and difficult 
accessibility started welcoming the domestic and foreign tourists 
for economic gains. This has been realized that the age-old pilgrim- 
age and recently introduced mountain tourism can put the economy 
of the region in good shape. 

This religio-adventure tourism industry is generating economic 
activities, socio-cultural exchanges, technological and environ- 
mental awareness among the people of the region. But, simultane- 
ously the negative impacts of this industry too are visualizing in the 
region in the shape of deforestation, garbage dumps, soil erosion, 
landslides and cultural pollution. 

According to J.D. Ives,* about ten percent of the population is 
directly dependent on mountain resources whereas more than forty 
percent is indirectly dependent on it for water, hydro electricity, 
timber, mineral resources, recreation and flood control. A proper 
resource management will give new directions to the economy of 
this region. 

The objective of this work is to study the economics of Hima- 
layan tourism (mountaineering and trekking) and its impacts on the 
region. For this study the objectives have been divided into the 
following: 


1. To explore the horizon of tourist attractions of Uttarkashi 
and Chamoli districts of Garhwal. 

2. Dissect the existing infrastructure of tourism and pilgrim- 
age of the region. 

3. To study the economy generated by mountaineering and 
trekking. 

4. Analyse the impacts of Himalayan tourism. 

5. To study the problems of Himalayan tourism for friendly 
and sustainable development of the region. 


Physical Setting 

The study area comprises two border districts of Garhwal 
mandal—Uttarkashi and Chamoli, geographically, northern 
Garhwal. It lies between 29° 31' N to 36° 6' 6" N and 77° 56' E to 


CC-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai апа eGangotri 


Introduction 25 


80° 6' E, covering an area of 17,141 sq kms, including Char Dhams 
of Badri, Kedar, Gangotri and Yamunotri. Badri-Kedar is the cher- 
ished goal of the Hindus, since time immemorial. To a Hindu this 
area is as important as Mecca to the Muslims, or Palestine to a 
Christian. In the north it is bounded by Tibet and Tehri, and Pauri 
in the south, on the east by Almora and Pithoragarh and on the west 
by Dehradun and Himachal Pradesh. Figure 1 depicts the study 
region. ; 

The area covers the catchment of the Ganga, Yamuna and 
Tons, their drainage pattern criss-crossing the region. Garhwal 
Himalaya is a distinct cultural unit, veritable store house of varied 
resources—hydrological, botanical, zoological, cultural and human. 
The varied morphology and great altitude variation differentiate 
one piece of land from another, climate as well as vegetation wise. 
The northern most part is rugged and snow-clad, but awe inspiring. 
The total reported area for land use is 17,06,602 hectares and real 
cultivable area is 74,319 hectares, the rest is either barren or under 
the cover of trees, bushes and pastures.’ 

In the Bhagvada Gita, Lord Krishna describes himself as 
"Sthavama" that is, “among the mountains I am Himalaya, noth- 
ing else.’’ Geographers say that in prehistoric times what was a sea 
bed named ‘‘Sea of Tethys,” suddenly came out on the surface as 
the longest and highest mountain range of the surface. 

Though the configuration of the Garhwal is being studied into 
three divisions from north to south longitudinally three parallel 
zones— 


1. The Himadri (The greater or higher Himalaya) 
2. The Himachal (The lesser or central Himalaya) 
3. The Siwalik (The outer or sub Himalaya) 


But as the study is concerned only with Uttarkashi and Cha- 
moli districts, hence, instead of studying all the three zones, only 
two the Himadri and the Himachal will be studied here. 


The Himadri 

The topography of the greater Himalaya is highly difficult. 
With precipitous slopes, snow-covered peaks, serrated crests of 
high ridges, glaciers, changing valley, bubbling waters coming

Detailed description of both the districts is given in Chapter 2. 
But it will not be out of point to state here that physical elements,

important factor, which influences the composition, distribution 
and social structure of resident population and tourist arrivals.

Introduction 29 


15th century king Ajai Pal began the unification of fifty two garhs 
(fortresses) into one kingdom. He shifted his capital from 
Chandpurgarhi to Dewalgarhi (present day Joshimath). The capital 
got shifted many times with different kings, but finally it was 
shifted to Srinagar. Many aggressions mauled Garhwal greatly, but 
the Maratha invasion of 1801 wrought much destruction. 

In 1803, Garhwal was invaded by Gorkhas. They ruled 
Garhwal for twelve years. They not only ruled, but ruined Garhwal 
in this period. Later with the help of British, Gorkhas were defeated 
on 15th May 1815. But the king of Garhwal was forced to cede 
Pauri (today's Pauri 4 Chamoli districts) to the British as payment 
for the help they rendered. The capital was shifted to Tehri. 

The first British Commissioner was Mr. George William Trail 
(1815-35). Other names in the administration of Uttarakhand are 
Battan and Sir Henry Ramsay. Long after Independence of India in 
1949, the merger of Tehri raj made way for the reconstitution of the 
five hill districts of U.P. into the present Garhwal division. 


Demographic Pattern 

The total population of Uttarkashi and Chamoli districts is 
5,55,294 with 2,79,876 males and 2,75,418 females. Both the dis- 
tricts have higher rural population in comparison to urban.'Chamoli 
has 3,35,172 and Uttarkashi has 1,77,676 people living in rural 
areas, whereas the number of people living in urban areas of 
Chamoli and Uttarkashi is 29,174 and 13,272 respectively. Sex 
ratio in Chamoli is 1026 females per thousand males, in Uttarkashi 
881 females per thousand males. Literacy percentage in Chamoli 
and Uttarkashi is 37.68 and 28.67 respectively which is very low in 
comparison to national rate. The main occupation of the people is 
agriculture. Nearly 81.26 percent population of Chamoli district 
and 79.23 of Uttarkashi is either agriculturist or agriculture labour. 
The percentage for small household industries is respectively 1.95 
and 1.08 percent. Rest are engaged in other occupations.’ The 
demographic structure is shown in Table 1.2. 


1.3 LITERATURE ON THE AREA 


Garhwal, which has got Char Dhams—Badri, Kedar, Gangotri 
and Yamunotri and the sacred rivers the Ganga and the Yamuna in 


CC-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


p 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


30 Tourism in Garhwal Himalaya 


its lap, is treated as the holiest among the holy places of Hindu pil- 
grimage. It is widely believed that this abode of gods and god- 
desses sanctified many saints who meditated here. Therefore there 
are plenty of mythological stories about the region from early 
times. 

Later in the 19th century many foreign religious missionaries, 
explorers and mountaineers started pouring into the region. They 
prepared their journey accounts with great care incorporating 
therein geomorphological, historical, cultural, religious and eco- 
nomic descriptions. In the 20th century researchers found the re- 
gion interesting for their studies. Development of mountaineering 
as sport paved the way to informative mountaineering journals. 
How the Alpine tourism came into existence and its development 
was also a matter of great curiosity for researchers. Therefore lit- 
erature on Alpine tourism development is also important. 

Altogether the research references can be divided into the fol- 
lowing heads— 


1. Mythological accounts 

2. Mountaineering and exploration details and gazetteers. 

3. Sociological and historical descriptions and regional re- 
searches. 

4. Mountaineering journals, expedition accounts, Alpine tour- 
ism etc. 


Hindu scriptures such as the Vedas, the Puranas, the Brahmans, 
Srutis and Smritis have the detailed descriptions of the region. Lack 
of information and maps of the region is one of the reasons of 
greater dependency on mythological accounts even today. Kedar- 
khand and Manaskhand of Skand Puran are full of regional infor- 
mation. Each and every religious detail is given. there. The 
Mahabharata underlines the religious importance of the region and 
provides an inventory of sacred places. The Puranas describe the 
glory of pilgrimage to different places. The Kedarkhand and 
Manaskhand of Skand Puran are almost entirely devoted to the de- 
scription of pilgrimage to Badri-Kedar Kshetra and Kumaon. 

Early exploration accounts of Skinner, ‘Excursions in India 
including the walk over the Himalaya Mountains to the source of 
the Jamuna and the Ganga' (1832), provide important geographical 
account of the unexplored regions. Atkinson's “The Himalayan

Introduction 31 


Districts of the North Western Province of India’ (1882-84) and 
vol. XI of the gazetteer reprinted in 1973 under the revised title, 
‘The Himalayan Gazetteer,’ (six vols) give historical, geographical, 
botanical, economic and all sorts of information of that time. 
Mountaineering accounts of yester years are of great importance. 
Among them, Smythe's ‘Kamet Conquered’ (1932) and ‘Valley of 
Flowers’ (1947), Tillman’s “The Ascent of Nanda Devi’ (1937), 
Hedin’s ‘Trans Himalaya’ (1931), Marco Pallis’ ‘Peaks and La- 
mas’ (1946), Bruce’s “The Assault of Mount Everest’ (1923), etc 
are the famous few, full of factual accounts and sometimes provide 
comparison of Himalayas with Alps. Apart from early 20th century 
accounts there are hundreds of present day first hand expedition 
records. 

Sociological and historical details of sociologists and historians 
too were of great help. Among them the prominent are Patiram’s 
‘Garhwal Ancient & Modern’ (1916), Painuli’s ‘A Tourist View of 
the Valley of Gods’ (1959), O’akley’s ‘The Holy Himalaya’ 
(1906), Dabral’s ‘Uttarkhand ka Itihas’ 8 vols (1965-77), and 
‘Uttarkhand Yatra Darshan’ (1960), Sankratyayan’s ‘Himalaya 
Parichaya’ part I (1953), Fonia’s ‘Uttarkhand: Garhwal Himalaya’ 
(1977), Singh's ‘Tourism and Tourist Industry in U.P.’ (1975), 
Kandari (1984) and Adhikari & Rawat (1990). 

Apart from sociological, historical and early exploratory details 
few mountaineering journals also provide useful information. 
Prominent among them are Indian Mountaineer, New Delhi, Hima- 
layan Journal, Calcutta and Abhiyatri, New Delhi. For Alpine tour- 
ism development quite a lot of references were consulted in depth. 
Lloyd’s ‘The Economic Development of Tourist Industry in New 
Zealand’ (1964), Mcintosh’s ‘Tourism Principles, Practices and 
Philosophy’ (1972), Nigel Hunt’s ‘Camping’ (1975), Peter 
Murphy’s ‘Tourism: A Community Approach’ (1985), Douglas 
Pearce’s ‘Tourism Today’ (1987) and Robinson’s ‘A Geography of 
Tourism’ (1981) are of great importance. 

For Himalayan tourism or say mountain sports activities in the 
region no specialised study has been done so far, but few refer- 
ences are of help. Among them Kaur’s ‘Himalayan Pilgrimage and 
the New Tourism’ (1985), Singh’s ‘The Kulu Valley Impact of 
Tourism Development in the Mountain Area’ (1989), Gary

Weare’s ‘Trekking in the Indian Himalaya’ (1986), Gupta’s “The

1.4 ConcLUDING REMARKS 


Uttarkashi and Chamoli districts with meagre mineral re- 
sources and fragile eco-system have got no scope for any big 
manufacturing industry, agriculture, economic development and 
growth of the region. Mountain tourism is the only silver lining on 
the dark industrial horizon of northern Garhwal (U.P. government 
declared Uttarkashi and Chamoli as ‘no’ industry districts). Hima- 
Тауап tourism in such regions can play a positive role in breaking 
through the vicious cycle of unemployment and checking the man- 
power drainage to plains. As a catalyst, it can bring speedy socio- 
economic transformation of the society and can create an aware- 
ness about the eco-conservation among the local people. But short- 
sighted tourism planning and negligent attitude towards resource 
inventory of tourism can lead towards ad hocism, ecological pro- 
blems and utter frustration among local people. Far-sighted moun- 
tain tourism development can provide alternative and sustainable 
development of the region.

Elements of Adventure Tourism 


2.1 INTRODUCTION 


Beauty can be a panacea for mankind’s fret and fever. Our 
Scriptures say, “all that’s beautiful is sacred, all that is sacred is 
beautiful”. Mountains are sacred because they possess beauty and 
they inspire mankind to meditate and grow spiritually. Beauty can 
be natural or man made. For attracting tourists it has to exist in 
either form. There should be some thing very unique in an area to 
attract the tourists. Basically there are three elements of tourism— 
locale, transport and accommodation. If the place is rich in natural 
beauty, amenities can be provided later on to make it a tourist desti- 
nation. Hence scenic beauty is the primary plus point for a place to 
become a tourist destination. 

Tourists like to spend their long holidays away from their usual 
residence, where they can enjoy nature and avoid urban agglomera- 
tion with least interference. General attractiveness of an area Or its 
Scenic quality is today a much sought after commodity. More often, 
the physical characteristics of natural features determine scenic 
quality of a landscape. Identification of the existing attractions is 
the first priority of tourism development. Later comes the infra- 
Structure, market analysis and profit-loss etc while all the compo- 
nents of tourism are essential and important for its growth, attrac- 
tions provide the energizing power. It can be called the stimulator, 
the motivator for the tourist. Attractions not only provide the things 
to se and do but also lure the tourist. 

Tourist spots of Uttarkashi and Chamoli districts of Garhwal 


CC-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


aes 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


34 Tourism in Garhwal Himalaya 


are replete with marvellous beauty and packed with the grandeur of 
nature. Divinity manifests itself in the lap, of the Himalaya. The 
holiest of the holy shrines of Hindus—Badri, Kedar, Gangotri, 
Yamunotri and Hemkund-Lokpal are located here. This land of 
sages, religion and cult is rich in natural assets with a variety of 
landscapes, the lush green valleys, majestic snow-covered peaks, 
soft pastures, gushing rivers, bubbling streams, tranquil tarns, ma- | 
jestic waterfalls, natural geysers, gaping gorges and salubrious cli- 
mate. This divine wildemess is adventurer’s challenge, the trailer’s 
delight, artist's supereminent inspiration, photographer's paradise, 
nature's superlative and a sanctuary of variegated fauna. "Is there 
any region of the Himalaya or even of the world, to excel this 
region in beauty and grandeur? Where are these to be found; such 
narrow and precipitous valleys and gorges, such serene vistas of 
Alps, forest, snow field and peaks."' 

To the Indian mind Himalaya and holiness are inseparable. Our 
scriptures recite rapturously that in the silent communion with the 
beauty and solitude of nature man gets rejuvenated with the feeling 


` of supreme spirituality. East and west, all agree that there is some- 


' thing in the wilderness that lures and enlivens mankind. Though 
much has been said about the tourist attractions of this region by 
great explorers and mountaineers, but still it is not properly pub- 
licised. Even today wandering in Garhwal is like exploring a new 
continent. 

The tourist attractions of this region can be divided into natu- 
ral, religious, social and other attractions. 


2.2 NATURAL ATTRACTIONS 


Himalaya and its fascinating scenery are the great assets of the 
region. Really so outstanding and unique are they that the entire 
tourist industry of the region can bank on them. Figure 2 depicts all 
the major natural attractions of the area. 


2.2.1 Landscape and Peaks 

An attractive landscape is the asset on which mountain tourism 
can bank. Jafari highly emphasised the importance of the back- 
ground tourist element **. . . it may be stated that they are ће foun-

dation and raison d’etre of the tourism industry. They are the rea-

ties are the very basis of tourism.”? 

The ‘entire area of Uttarkashi and Chamoli districts is moun- 
tainous. The rugged landmass is thoroughly and artistically punctu- 
ated with nature’s superlatives. ‘‘Mountains anywhere in the world, 
with their pronounced sculpturing carry greater aesthetic appeal 
than low reliefed land forms.”” This is true in respect of Garhwal 
as well. The natural surroundings here steal the heart of passers-by 
and give them unending delight. The landscape here is most en- 
chanting. “In scenery and climate Garhwal is comparable to Swit-

The study area, which rises from Rudraprayag (610 m) in Cha- 
moli district and from Nagun in Uttarkashi district culminates at 
Nanda Devi (7817 m), covering an area of 17,141 sq kms. Moun- 

- tain system can also be described according to the aesthetic value 
of the natural landscape. 

The elevation profile of Garhwal region begins with the foot- 
"ills (Rishikesh in district Dehradun and Kotdwara in district 
Pauri) the Siwaliks meeting the dun (600 m), on the south dun 
gives way to the first ridges of lesser Himalaya, which ultimately 
lead to the towering peaks of perpetual snow (greater Himalaya, 
the core region of Himalaya) and forms Indo-Tibetan border on the 
extreme north. Central axis, which forms the part of greater Hima- 
laya, passes through Yamunotri, Harsil, Dabrani, south of Kedar- 
nath and Nanda Devi. The scenery changes its hue and colours at 
each and every place. The lesser Himalayan zone is dissected by 
various narrow, wide and precipitous glens. The main rivers drain- 
ing the region are the Tons, Yamuna, Alaknanda and Bhagirathi. 
“It is a massive mountainous tract separated by the ‘central Hima- 
layan Thrust' and the “Main Boundary Fault', from the north and 
south respectively, with an approximate width of 75 kms. The re- 
gion is a tangeled mass of series of ridges and spurs of various 


СС-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 
Elements of Adventure Tourism 37 


height divided by deep valleys. The average relief of ridges in this 
zone ranges between 1500 m and 2700 m, and of valley bottoms 
between 500 and 1200 m.”” 

Greater Himalaya with a mean relief average between 4800 
and 6000 m has varying width of 75 kms in the east and 30 kms in 
the west and covers 28.5 percent area of the region. This zone is 
composed of crystalline and metamorphic rocks of granite, gneisses 
and schists with unfossiliferous sedimentary deposits. The Tethys 
zone consists of grey calcarious Stones, containing kinds of fossils, 
unfossiliferous Niti lime stone and dark blue shades of lower trias. 
This uninhabited region of Garhwal is Studded with hard rocks, 
boulders, mountains, snow-covered peaks, rivers, valleys, pastures 
and glaciers. It has most difficult physiography to approach, but 
most enchanting landscape to enjoy and gape. This greater Hima- 
layan chain bifurcates at many places to throw-off other ranges, 
The first is called Zaskar range, which runs a little south of the 
watershed in Garhwal and has high peaks like Kamet (7756 m), 
Abi Gamini (7376 m), Rataban (6168 m) and Trisul (7120 пп). 
Before 1962 the Indo-Tibetan trade was а common feature of the 
region. The second tributary range of greater Himalaya is 
Dhauladhar near Banderpunch, which forms part of the boundary 
between Garhwal and Himachal Pradesh. : 

Greater Himalaya has sheer ргесірісе dropping of 2000-2500 
m below into the deep and narrow gorges that horrify the viewer. 
This part of the country is difficult, but so fascinating that it always 
inspires one for the second visit. Meads has rightly said, “ . . . the 
mystery is aweful, the thrill is some times a shudder.” This diffi- 
cult but unique scenery is an attractive challenge for adventure- 
loving tourists. The highly rugged topography of this zone, sculp- 
tured by past gigantic glaciers and mighty rivers presents one of the 
most pious and unforgettable scenery. “The mountains in Garhwal 
region are more immediate and impressive than in any other region 
of the Himalaya and it is easy to understand why the range is 
Considered to be the abode of the Gods; 

It would be unfair to go ahead by making a general statement 
about higher Himalaya and its touristic potentials, the actual attrac- 
tions are the towering snow-covered peaks that make the Scenery 
challenging and beautiful to adventurers. The chains of high moun-

tain peaks are divided into different mountain groups by the mighty 
rivers of Garhwal. The high peaks of Banderpunch (6302 m), 
Kalanag (6387 m), Swaragarohini (6252 m), and Yamunotri lie 
between the Tons and Bhagirathi rivers, Matri (6721 m) Chirbas 
(6525 m), Trimukh Parbat (6422 m) between Jadh Ganga and the 
Bhagirathi rivers. 

Another cluster of peaks lies in between Bhagirathi and Sar-

area and its peaks were most adored by the Gods and even today 
pose a great challenge for the mountaineers. Famous among this 
cluster of peaks are Chaukhamba (I-7140, П-7070, Ш-6970, IV- 
6850 m) situated on the west of Badrinath temple, due east of

on the other side of Kirti glacier are Shivling (6540 m) and 
Bhrigupanth (6770 m). Gangotri glacier, which starts from Chau- 
khamba and ends at Gaumukh is almost encircled by the snow- 
covered peaks. At north-west of Chaukhamba are Satopanth (7070 
m), Vasuki Parbat (6790 m), Bhagirathi group and Chandra Parbat 
(6739 m). 

North of Chandra is Manda Parbat (6794 m), Sri Kailash (6932

Jogin touch Khatling glacier in Tehri district. Various peaks and 
glaciers of Gangotri region are shown in Fig. 3.

known as inner Nanda Devi sanctuary. This horse shoe-shaped for-

Nanda Devi (7817 m), Nanda Devi east (7430 m), Nanda Kot

One can enjoy the beauty of upper Garhwal while trekking or 
climbing the mountains, but for the other easy going category of 
tourists (who are less inclined to participate in adventure), there are 
few vantage points.to enjoy the scenery. For the view of Nanda 
Devi and its surroundings, the vantage points are Debrugheta, 
Kuari pass, Chandershila and Auli near Joshimath in Chamoli dis- 
trict, for Kedarnath group of peaks Durital and Kedarnath temple 
are the spots. For Thalay Sagar, Kirti Stambha, Kedarnath and 
Jogin etc Panwali is the best spot. For Shivling, Bhagirathi group, 
Kirti Bamak, Gangotri group and others, Tapovan (near Gaumukh) 
is the best point. In between Panwali and Trijuginarayan, Kinkona 
Khal (11,800 ft) is the highest pass. 


2.2.2 Glaciers 

Apart from the snowy peaks the glacial topography of upper 
Garhwal is punctuated with rocky ridges, thick meadows, emerald 
dells, hanging glaciers, water falls, torrents, rivers and tars formed 
by lateral and terminal moraines. As a trekker goes higher and 
higher skirting range after range along a steep trek, the scenery 
becomes more and more enchanting and awe-inspiring, though 
weather becomes cool and bracing. Longstaff, the famous moun- 
taineer moved by the irresistible panoramic charm of the area, says, 
“I still believe that Garhwal is the most beautiful country of all 
high Asia. Neither the primitive immensity of the Karakoram, the 
aloof domination of Mount Everest, the softer Caucasion beauties 
of the Hindukush nor any of the many other regions of Himachal 
can compare with Garhwal."* 

There are numerous glaciers in the Uttarkashi and Chamoli 
districts of Garhwal and their spread is stupendous. Garhwal is one 
of the most glaciated regions and here the glaciers’ maze is most 
amazing. Even present land form is an evidence of ice-sculpture re- 


CC-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai апа eGangotri 


Elements of Adventure Tourism 41 


sulted from age-old gigantic glaciers. Valleys between 2000 and 
3000 m show the glacial feature wherever not blot out by fluvial 
action. In the vast glacial stretch of Garhwal (extreme left) on the 
west is Banderpunch and deep down (extreme right) on the south 
east is Nanda Devi. In between there are many glaciers, but promi- 
nent among them is Gangotri glacier. In the lower crest zone, Ban- 
derpunch glacier originates from Batiakhund Bamak (6285 m) 
peak. It disgorges Tons river after 9 kms run. On the right is the 
snout of one of the longest glaciers of India, Gangotri glacier, 
which descends steep down from the height of 7138 m from the 
north-west slopes of Chaukhamba peaks. This is 25 kms long and 2 
kms wide and later the fall becomes shallow. It is fed by a system 
of tributary glaciers known as Mainadi (5 kms long), Swechhanda 
(7 kms), Chaturangi (16 kms) and Kirti Bamak etc. 

Bhrigupanth, Meru and Raktavarna were also important tribu- 
taries to Gangotri glacier in an early period, although these have 
developed separate snouts and no longer join it. On the south valley 
wall of the Bhagirathi river, about 4 kms downstream of the present 
snout of Gangotri glacier is Bhrigupanth glacier. Another glacier, 
Meru is located on the south flank but upstream of the present 
Gangotri snout. Raktavama glacier is located on the north bank of 
Gangotri glacier. Chaturangi, the east-west running valley glacier is 
the longest tributary of Gangotri glacier. Kirti glacier is located on 
the south bank of Gangotri glacier. 

Bhagirathi kharak descends from 6984 m peak and ends up at 
Vasudhara (17 kms). Satopanth glacier Starts rolling from (6864 m) 
and reaches Vasudhara. Panpatia glacier descends from (6974 m) 
and covers the distance of 2.5 kms and picks its tributaries Vidun 
and Bagni glaciers and spreads for 12 kms to the north of Arwa 
Pahar (5873 m). 

In central Garhwal, west Kamet glacier rolls down from south 
face of Mukut Parbat (7242 m), Abi Gamin Cum and Kamet north 
face and ends up at Khati. Subsidiary Mukut west ridge from south- 
West face of Kamet. The tributaries are Uttari and Dakshini Chami- 
rao and Khagyan: glaciers. The east Kamet glacier starts from east 
face of Kamet arid Abi Gamin (7355 m) and stops at Dobal (12 
kms), embracing on the way Raykana, Uttari Raykana and Ganesh 
glaciers. Bonkud glacier emerging from Guptakhal (5807 m) flows

to Mulya Bamak (15 kms), sweeping ten big and small—Devban,

Nanda Devi sanctuary (Rishi valley) is a beautiful amphithea- 
ter of snow covered peaks and small glaciers of their names. Well 
known among them are North Rishi, North Nanda Devi, South 
Rishi, South Nanda Devi, Changabang, Trisul Ramani and Bether- 
toli glaciers. 


2.2.3 Water System 

The water system of the region consists of rivers, streams, tarns 
and torrents. The major rivers Bhagirathi, Alaknanda, Yamuna and 
Tons have their origin in this region and all other streams and rivu- 
lets empty themselves in them. Unfortunately this water resource 
couldn't be utilised for the development of the region. Rivers are 
running deep down into the gorges from Where it couldn’t be util- 
ised for irrigation or any other purpose, resulting in low-yielding 
dry farming and manpower drainage to plains. These water re- 
source potentials can be utilised for irrigation, power generation 
and water sports. Water sports like rafting has been introduced in 
some rivers of Garhwal successfully. 


Rivers 

Most of the rivers of Garhwal have got the tendency of flowing 
parallel to mountains in deep gorges and do not descend from the 
highest peaks. Generally they descend from their own glaciers and 
on the way they collect water from the great Himalayan ranges. 
The river system of Garhwal can be divided into two— 


1. The Ganga system 
2. The Yamuna system 


1. Ganga System 
In 1807 N.S. Webb of the East India Company was deputed to 
explore the source of Ganga, but he could not succeed. In 1808 
Caption Raper was sent to achieve this mission, but it was only 
after 1857 that the British could achieve the mission. Bhagirathi, 
one of the tributaries of Ganga, originates from the snout of Gan- 
„gotri glacier, whereas Alaknanda, the other and main tributary 


СС-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


Elements of Adventure Tourism 43 


emerges from the Bhagirathi Khark and Satopanth glaciers, east of 
Chaukhamba peaks. After flowing in opposite directions from their 
snouts they meet at Deoprayag and is known as Ganga. The follow- 
ing rivers come under Ganga system: 


a) Alaknanda 

Trekking to the source of Alaknanda is an arduous journey to a 
fairy land that unveils itself gradually and is known as the abode of 
Kuber (the Yakshas and Gandharvas of Hindu mythology). A des- 
cent of 3 kms from here leads towards a pleasant surprise of dense 
forests of silver birch forest of Lakshmivan. Four kms from here, is 
famous Vasudhara falls (122 m), set in the area of rocky heights 
with a backdrop of snowy peaks and glaciers that present a unique 
phenomenon of nature. Tumbling waters of Vasudhara falls caught 
by the cold winds diffuse in fine showers and soft puffs. Close to 
Vasudhara are Chaukhamba, Nilkanth, Satopanth, Balkum and 
many unnamed peaks. Nine kms ahead of Lakshmivan is a place 
known as Chakratirtha, where Nar and Narayan mountains meet 
and form a lovely circular valley of Alpine flowers and meandering 
brooks. Further four kms ahead is a triangular lake, Satopanth, at an 
altitude of 4402 m. It is believed that Brahma, Vishnu and Shiva— 
the creator, preserver and the destroyer of the universe—are medi- 
tating at the three comers of this lake. Majestically standing, Swar- 
garohini (the stairway to heaven) is a mythological mountain. In 
Puranic literature it is said that Pandavas ascended this mountain 
with seven giant steps and went to heaven. 


b) Sarasvati 

Sarasvati emerging from the waters of Deotal and Ragastal 
(from the crest of the Mana pass), passes through the most desolate 
valley covered with moranic debris of Ratakona, Gierchakona, 
Khati and meets Arwa river coming from Arwatal, at Ghastoli. 
Both the rivers are fed by great Zaskar range. Later the con- 
fluence of the waters of Sarasvati and Alaknanda takes place at 
Keshava Prayag (Mana). The valley of Sarasvati is bounded on 
the west by the snowy chain separating it from the head waters 
of Jhanavi, a feeder of the Bhagirathi. On either side, it receives 
numerous torrents rising from the glaciers. From October to May, 
the water of Sarasvati remains crystal clear with blue hue in

44 Tourism іп Garhwal Himalaya 


contrast to the colourless water of Alaknanda. Sarasvati is the 
Goddess of learning. It is said that by the blessings of Sarasvati, 
Rishi Ved Vyas could compose Mahabharata at Mana. Even the 
ancient Indo-Tibetan trade route was along the bank of Sarasvati 
via Mana pass (5453 m), approaching Tholingmath. From Mana, 
Alakananda descends down to Badrinath (a valley approximately 
4.5 kms long and 1.5 kms broad). Kishir Ganga, Lakshman Ganga 
(Bhundhar Ganga) joint Alaknanda enroute where one finds the 
most beautiful Himalayan scenery with well-wooded forest and 
open valleys. 


с) Dhauli Ganga 

Dhauli, the principal tributary of Alaknanda emanates from the 
eastem glaciers of Kamet, Mana and Abi Gamin. It passes through 
Kanajari Sisang and Kunglang, an uninhabited rocky solitude inter- 
rupted by the falling boulders, grow] of avalanches and the noise of 
roaring torrents, collects water of Ganesh Ganga (that emerges 
from Ganesh Parbat) and Amrit Ganga (from the northern slopes of 
Rataban and other peaks). Later Dhauli meets Girthi Ganga from 
Kungribingrila. The tributaries of Girthi Ganga are Youggad (from 
Barahoti) and Kiogad (from Lapthal). 

Dhauli has most tortuous course among the rivers of Garhwal. 
Every turn in the river reveals new beauties and grandeurs. Smythe, 
while walking in Dhauli valley found the deep gorges and noisy 
torrents most interesting. Something like walking in the whispering 
gallery of Gods.” Later Dhauli collects water of Raikananadi and 
passes through Gothing and reaches Niti. 

The stoney field of Niti gives a rugged scenery. At several 
places the boulders form natural bridges over Dhauliganga. Niti, 
Bampa and Gamsali are the summer shelters of the nomadic 
Bhotias. Western Dhauli valley is known as Niti valley. 

Later Dhauli flows down and meets Alaknanda at Vishnu- 
prayag near Joshimath and becomes Alaknanda on the way to 
Nandprayag. Alaknanda collects water from Patal Ganga, Birahi 
Ganga, Dudh Ganga, Garur Ganga and at Nandprayag meets Nan- 
dakini which comes from its sources Sila-Samudra glacier and 
Trisul massif. Later at Karanprayag, Alaknanda collects waters of 
Pinder and flows down towards Rudraprayag where it meets Man- 
dakini.

| Elements of Adventure Tourism 45 


d) Rishi Ganga 
| Rishi Ganga, one of the major tributaries of Dhauli Ganga, 
| emerges from the south Nanda Devi glacier and south Rishi glacier. 
| Very soon it is joined by а tributary emerging from Changabang 
| glacier. Rishi Ganga flows into deep gorges and moves along its 
path, collecting water from various torrents from both sides and 
ultimately it joints Dhauli Ganga at Reni Village. 


e) Mandakini 

Mandakini is a major river of Kedar valley. It originates from a 
small glacier, Chaurabari, which is little down the Chaurabarital 
(Gandhi Sarovar). The snout of the glacier has no sign of change 
since its inception. From Rudraprayag to Chaurabari glacier, the 
whole valley presents most unforgettable scenery. The entire Man- 
dakini valley is full of lush green thick forests, clouds, bubbling 
streams and rivulets. Upper Kedarnath valley is most enchanting 
amid beautiful Alpine flowers and these flowers can be seen from 
August to mid September. 

The mighty Alaknanda from its inception to its confluence with 
Bhagirathi at Deoprayag, flows wide, narrow, deep and fast from 
place to place. At Koteshwar, one km ahead of Rudraprayag it 
becomes so narrow that a barking deer is said to have crossed it in 
a jump, therefore the place is known as Kakarfali (barking deer’s 
leap). Most of the rivers and the torrents emerging from the gla- 
ciers have wide open and beautiful valleys, deep ravines and 
gorges. 


f) Bhagirathi 

The most important tributary of Ganga is Bhagirathi. Emerging 
from the mighty Gangotri glacier, this holy river is known as 
Ganga at Gangotri. Mythology holds that hundreds of years ago 
Ganga came all the way from the heaven to the earth. It was the 
time when Gangotri glacier spread upto the Gangotri temple. But 
due to geophysical and other changes, the glacier melted and the 
snout receded to Gaumukh. It is said that several years of intensive 
meditation of king Bhagirath made Ganga to descend from the 
heaven to the earth. Deogad is the first rivulet emerging from De- 
орай Bamak to join Bhagirathi. Later at Gangotri, Kedar Ganga 


CC-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


НАН 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


46 Tourism in Garhwal Himalaya 


originating from Kedar Bamak flows into Bhagirathi. Various small 
revulets like Ghoragad, Nilapanigad and Lambigad etc join to- 
gether and form Managad, which passes through Naga, a small 
place from where starts a track which goes upto Dasumdu. Jadang 
is only 3 kms from here and Jelukhaga pass is 26 kms away. One 
small rivulet, Chorgad emerging from Chorgad Bamak joins Man- 
agad at Dhumku and becomes Jadh Ganga or Jahnvi, the largest 
feeder of Bhagirathi, emptying itself at Bhairoghati. Jadh Ganga 
valley is a restricted area where no one can go without prior per- 
mission. First explorers to the source of Jahnvi were Mr. J.B. Fraser 
(1815) and Messers Hodgson & Herbert (1817). But the credit goes 
to Mr. Kinney for the great trigonometrical survey which estab- 
lished a firm proof of origin and course of Jadh Ganga. At Harsil, 
Jalandrigad, emerging from Lamkhaga Bamak meets Bhagirathi. 
Varun and Ashi are the tributaries of Bhagirathi. Small township of 
Uttarkashi is situated on the banks of Bhagirathi. Due to Varun and 
Ashi rivers, the town is religiously as important for Hindus as Var- 
anasi. Before Tehri many streams and rivulets join the main stream. 
At Tehri, Bhilangana, which emerges from Sri Kantha peak above 
Khatling glacier, meets Bhagirathi and later at Deoprayag, young 
Bhagirathi meets mature Alaknanda and is called Ganga, which 
flows down to Rishikesh, Hardwar and so on. Due to its maturity, 
at Allahabad Ganga maintains its identity as Ganga, whereas 
Yamuna gets submerged into it. 


2. Yamuna System 

The second important river of Garhwal, Yamuna emerges from 
Sapta Rishi kund which is on the south-western slopes er- 
punch peak at an altitude of 3287 m. The principal soukce is 500 ft 
north-west above the hot spring of Yamunotri, where the face of 
the mountain makes a very steep rise and is entirely cased in snow 
and ice. Though there is no great difference of elevation from 
Yamunotri temple to the source of Yamuna, but the trek becomes 
difficult and interesting by the closed mountain set up and dark 
rocky cliffs. Captain Skinner rightly says, “I have beheld all the 
celebrated scenery of Europe, which poets and painters have 
immortalised, and of which all the tourists in the world are ena- 
moured; but I have seen it surpassed in these unfrequented and 


СС-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


Elements of Adventure Tourism 47 
almost unknown region.”'” After 158 years of his visit, the region 
is still the same, an unfrequented fairy land. He further says, “Ве- 
hind me, to the north-west, were the snows of Banderpunch and 
Dootie whence the Jamuna flows; thence, toward the east rose the 
high peaks which mark the source of the holy river, the Ganga—the 
Rudru Himaleh, like a white cloud in the horizon—Kedarnath and 
Badrinath, those mightly objects of Hindoo superstition mixing 
with the skies; So far out-topping other heights that I had almost 
considered them illusory, I began to doubt, as I gazed on them, 
whether there was any interval between Heaven and earth."!! 
| The following rivers are from the Yamuna system: 


a) Tons 

Its tributaries Rupin, Supin, Har-ki-Doon-gad and Ruinsara- 
gad feed westem Uttarkashi district. Obra-gad, a tributary to Supin 
originates from Devkir glacier, meets Supin and flows down until it 
meets Tons. Devkirdhar above Devkir glacier works as boundary 
wall between Uttarkashi and Himachal Pradesh. Har-ki-Doon-gad 
collects water from Maninda-gad, which originates from the ice 
walls down below Borasu ghati, joins Ruinsara-gad, which comes 
from Ruinsara tal. This joint river ultimately joins Tons at Daunsu 
and flows down with the water of Supin for hugging sister rivulet 
Rupin at Naitwar. Later Tons joins Yamuna at Kalsi. Figure 4 
shows main rivers and their tributaries of Uttarkashi and Chamoli 
districts. 


Lakes and Tarns 

Upper Garhwal Himalaya is famous for its tranquil tarns, 
which are generally found around 3000 m above the sea level. 
Most of these tarns are formed by the landslide and heaps of 
debris, partly blocking the rivers or streams. Though some are 
fed by the underground sources. Some time they are short lived. 
Mostly glacial lakes are formed by plugging of valleys by mor- 
aines deposited by ancient giant glaciers. Mostly they are sweet 
water lakes but few are of brine water too. Unfortunately, some 
are dried up lake areas today. The largest lake of Uttarakhand, 
Gohna in Chamoli district is the latest example of such happenings. 
This lake came into existence near Gohna village on 6th August,

` 1893 with a huge landslide blocking a river called Birahi Ganga. In 
1930 the forest department released some trout fishes in the lake 
and very soon it became the anglers’ paradise. But the cloud burst 
of 22nd July, 1970 made the lake overflow and break the wall of 
one side, resulting in a devastating flood. Suddenly on 26th July, 
1970 the lake disappeared. But the dried lake tells the sad tale of 
Gohna lake. 

Most of the Himalayan tarns here are associated with one or 
the other mythological gods, goddesses or saints. Some of the tarns 
are very beautiful and easily accessible. Trekking to these tarns is a 
sublime experience. 


a) Dodital 

One of the lovelist lakes of Garhwal, Dodital is located at an 
altitude of 3024 m above sea level with an area of 1 sq km. In 1948 
Mr. Jack Gibson (Head Master of Doon School) took the school 
boys upto the lake. Gradually it became one of the most popular 
lakes among the tourists. 

This lake can be approached from Gangori (5 kms from Uttar- 
kashi, on way to Gangotri), a small village with a motorable road. 
Seven kms from here, there is a small village Kalyani. The trek 
begins from here for Agoda village (5 kms from Kalyani). From 
Agoda one enters a dense mixed forest of oak, rhododendron, blue 
pine, maple, spruce and fir etc. This 16 kms trek is highly fascinat- 
ing. In the name of the sage Dodi, who meditated here thousands of 
years ago, it is named as Dodital. This emerald blue lake of fresh 
water is surrounded by the trees of oak and rhododendron. An un- 
named stream fills water into the lake, but a small river named Asi 
Ganga keeps emptying it. Fresh water trouts are one of the attrac- 
tions for the tourists. Further one can trek upto Bakriyakhaga pass, 
from where the view of Banderpunch, Sri Kantha, Gangotri group 
and Jaonli etc can be enjoyed. 

Wildlife around is leopard, jungle cat, leopard cat, Himalayan 
black bear, Himalayan palm civet, serow, samber, jackal, red fox, 
Porcupine, monal pheasant and Himalayan golden eagle etc. A 
forest rest house and camping ground are there for tourist accom- 
modation. ME

50 Tourism in Garhwal Himalaya 


b) Nachiketatal 

A small but beautiful lake, Nachiketatal (approximately 600 sq 
m in area), is situated at an altitude of 2453 m east of Uttarkashi. 
Chaurangikhal, 23 kms from Uttarkashi, is the road-head for the 
lake. From Chaurangikhal one has to trek 3 kms in the thick forest. 
Though it is a very small lake but very easily accessible and beauti- 
fully situated. Watching fresh water trouts is an enchanting experi- 
ence. The lake is surrounded by oak and rhododendron trees. Near 
the lake, there is a small temple of Nag Devta and every year on 
Nagpanchami day nearby villagers collect here for holy dip and 
worship. 

From the west of the lake a vast panorama of Banderpunch 
region is worth-seeing. An adventure loving tourist can trek from 
Uttarkashi to Nachiketatal which is only 16 kms. Few shops and a 
P.W.D. rest house are there but one can camp near the lake. 


c) Kedartal 

This large emerald lake at an altitude of 5000 m is born of 
the Thalaysagar snows. The trek to the lake begins from Gangoti, 
just above Dandi Kshetra. The first 4 kms trek is a difficult 
one with steep rocky cliffs on the west and fast flowing Kedar 
Ganga in the deep gorge, on the east. The trek is used by 
the shepherds mainly. After a trek of 7.5 kms one reaches Bhoj- 
kharak. The steep climb goes through the balmy pine forest. 
Bhojkharak provides a beautiful camp site, but one can go to 
Kedarkharak (4 kms from Bhojkharak) for camping. At places the 
trek is hazardous because of rockfalls. Water is available every 
where on the way but fire wood is not available. Further 5 kms is 
Kedartal. The ascent through loose tumbling rocks is laborious and 
dangerous. 

Kedar Ganga originating from Kedartal is considered Lord 
Shiva's contribution to the Bhagirathi. A ridge separates Kedartal 
from Rudragira Kharak. A ridge approximately 700 m, higher than 
Kedartal is a vantage point, from where one can get the vast view 
of Thalaysagar, Bhrigupanth and Jogin group of peaks and lush- 
green valleys down below. This is one of the most beautiful and 
unspoilt lakes of Garhwal Himalaya. `

Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


Elements of Adventure Tourism 51 


d) Sahastrutal 

Sahastru is the modification of the local, Garhwali word ‘Saha- 
syu’, which means seven. There are seven lakes on both sides of 
the watershed dividing Uttarkashi and Tehri districts. The pilgrim- 
age to Sahastrutal is an annual event for the villagers of Uttarkashi 
and Tehri districts. The fresh water lakes change their colour with 
the glances of sun. The trek to these lakes begins from Malla (25 
kms from Uttarkashi on way to Gangotri). From here the track goes 
to Shilla, Johra, Karki and then Dharmshala. It covers around 25 
kms long track. Dharmshala is the base camp for seven lakes but 
the hut is not in good condition. Just after a trek of one km comes 
Lamatal, then Dudhi, Pari, Darhsan and Gaumukhital. 

All these tals have good camp sites but Gaumukhi is better 
located. This thrilling trek comprises steep ascent and deep descent. 


e) Deoriatal 

At a distance of 5.5 kms from Mastura (road-head to the lake 
on the Gopeshwar-Ukhimath road), there is a beautiful lake situ- 
ated on the ridge of a spur at an altitude of 2399 m above the sea 
level. Mastura is 8 kms from Ukhimath by road, but the steep mule 
track which starts from Mastura passes through a tiny village Sari 
and enters the rich mixed forest of pine, oak and rhododendron. 
“The lake is approximately 200 sq m in area and the maximum 
depth is 66 feet." There are underground water sources. The 
fresh tranquil water, merrily darting fishes, thick forest around the 
lake, magnificent Chaukhamba, Kedamath and Bhartikhunta peaks 
on the north-west of the lake and their reflection in the water all 
together present irresistibly beautiful panorama for the visitors. The 
view from its banks is one of the grandest in the hills. Mr. Batten 
goes оп to add that “the Pargana of Nagpur in Garhwal would 


never be forgotten by those who have spent a day on the bank of 
Diurital."'? 


f) Hemkund-Lokpal 

Surrounded by the (Sapta Shringi) seven snow-covered peaks 
and glaciers at an altitude of 4150 m above the sea level, a beauti- 
ful lake, Hemkund-Lokpal is situated about 32 kms from Joshi- 
math. The road-head for Hemkund-Lokpal is Govindghat. This lake

commands two-fold sanctity, because of its association with Laksh- 
man, one of the heros of Ramayana and the Sikh Guru Gobind 
Singh. The trek is a steep climb but a constant delight of Alpine 
flowers and superb scenery. Lakshman Ganga emerging from the 
lake meanders amidst the Alpine meadows of colourful brahma- 
Ката], fen-kamal, hem-kamal, potentillas, Saxifraga, marsh-merry 
gold, blue poppy, fems and primula etc and joins the Pushpawati 
river at Ghangaria (a halting place). Here the joint stream is known 
as Lakshman Ganga. Every year on the occasion of Rakshabandhan 
a local religious fair is held at Lokpal and a large number of 
Marcha women come here to tie the Rakhee to Lakshmanji. Ghan- 
garia is coming up as a halting place for the tourists of Hemkund- 
(GMVN) accommodation and Gurdwara accommodation are avail- 
able here. 


g) Satopanth 

Situated at an altitude of 4402 m above sea level and 29 kms 
from Badrinath, is another glacial lake named Satopanth. It is con- 
sidered one of the most pious lakes of the Hindus. This triangular 
lake is glorified by the belief of the devout that the holy trinity of 
the Hindus—Brahma, Vishnu and Shiva reside at the three corners 
of the lake. The journey from Vasudhara falls to Satopanth is diffi- 
cult but beautiful. One must not go without guide and provisions— 
stove, tent, sleeping bag etc. 


h) Kakbhusandital 

A small oblong lake of emerald green water is situated at an 
altitude of about 5000 m, in the lap of Hati Parbat (6730 m). This 
beautiful lake is associated with the mythological character 
Kakbhusandi (the crow). The lake can be approached from two 
sides, either from Bhyunder village near Ghangaria or from Vishnu 
prayag. 

The Ghangaria trek is comparatively easy as it passes through 
thick forests, boulders, glacial moraines and slippery rocks. Shep- 
herd huts are the only place for night halts. The other approach 
from Vishnu Prayag is difficult due to steep ridges and climbs.

i) Deotal 

The holiest of the holy lake, Deotal, with a diametre of 800 т, 
is situated on the glacial moraines іп the inner line area of Chamoli 
district. It is on the ancient Indo-Tibetan trade route, which passes 
through Mana pass for Tholingmath. The walk is most treacherous 
on the morainic debris of ancient glaciers. The lake remains frozen 
most of the time in the year. | 


j) Bhaikaltal 

А small tam, with ап area of 400 sq m is situated on the top of 
a ridge at an altitude of 3135 m above the sea level. It is 13 kms 
from the village Phaldiya in Chamoli district. This lake is sur- 
rounded by the thick line of thododendron, oak, hill bamboo and 
scanty spruce trees, hence in winter it gets little sun and the water 
turns into ice. This ice-frozen area was used for ice-skating by the 
Britishers. The trek to the lake passes through a thick forest, where 
wild birds abound and cattle and shepherd huts provide an enchant- 
ing experience. These shepherd huts are the only accommodation 
in the area apart from camping ground. 


k) Brahamtal 

Crystal clear waters of Brahamtal, with an area of 700 sq m is 
fed by three beautiful bubbling mountain streams coming from 
three different sides. This beautiful lake is 3 kms ahead of Bhaikal- 
tal, amidst picturesque natural Surroundings. A solitary oak free on 
the upper side of the lake, vast stretches of Alpine meadows and 
floating brown ducks present unforgettable scenery. This continu- 
ously filling lake hasn’t got any visible outlet for water, though 
certainly it must exist in its bosom (underground outlet). A govern- 
ment sheep breeding farm is located nearby. Sheep, goats, horses, 
Ponies, cows, buffalows and Shepherds are seen around during 
summer months. Alpine flowers and rhododendron dwarfs are the 
unique decorations of this mountain scenery. 

Bhaikaltal and Brahamtal are the summer water points for wild 
animals like brown bear, wild boar and deer сіс of nearby forest. 


l) Vednikund 
A vast stretch of Alpine meadows, at an altitude of 3354 m,

decorated with colourful flowers during July-August is known as 
Vedni bugyal. Amidst such surroundings a natural source of water 
and a dry lake tells the tale of an age-old lake, which was of im- 
mense religious significance. The famous Nanda Rajjat (Bari & 
Chote) procession used to go via Vednikund with great reverence 
and rejoicing, Vedni is 10 kms from Debal (the road-head), the trek 
amid a thick forest is interesting. Now a man-made lake is coming 
up here by controlling the water of the Vedni spring. This is one of 
the best camp sites of the area. In winters it could be used for snow 
skiing. Neighbouring Auli slopes are most suitable for snow skiing. 


m) Roopkund 

Debal, the road-head for Roopkund trek is 57 kms from Kar- 
anprayag and one has to follow the path to Vednikund. Leaving 
behind Vedni the trekker has to proceed further for Bhagubasa, a 
halting place for Roopkund trek. From here the trek takes four 
hours. This small, but beautiful mysterious lake is situated below 
the Trisul massif on a mountain close to Chandanikot (5023 m) 
peak at an altitude of 4778 m above sea level. For the first time in 
1898 it was discovered that some human skeletal remains were 
lying there in and around the lake. Since then the lake is known as 
a mystery lake. This small lake is surrounded by steep rock walls 
about 25 metre high. After a walk from Vedni the sudden appear- 
ance of this small lake surprises the trekker. The mystery of these 
skeletal remains is yet to be solved. Though Prof D.N. Majumdar, 
J.B. Griffin and D. Majumdar worked hard to solve the mystery, 
but all efforts went in vain. This lake has a special significance for 
Goddess Nanda and her devotees. Little ahead is Homkundikhal 
(Rounti-Saddle), which is used as the base camp by mountaineers 
attempting Trisul south face. 


n) Vasukital 

A beautiful Himalayan tam, with an area of half sq km is 
Situated at an altitude of 4884 m. It is 6 kms from Kedarnath temple 
the track goes from the left side of the temple. Though the ap- 
proach is very difficult and unmarked but the very glimpses of the 
lake mitigates physical lethargy and rejuvenates the body, mind and 
soul. Initial trek of 4 kms is steep, then half a km is gradual, but the

last stretch is most difficult due to scattered boulders. In all, the 
route is not very clear and one has to cross two passes also. In the 
last leg of the walk one has to cover a descent of at least 1.5 kms. 
The scenery around is most enchanting. The lake is surrounded by 
small hillocks of Alpine meadows, full of colourful flowers promi- 
nent among them being brahma-kamal and neel-kamal. Most of the 
brahma kamals are brought down for offerings at Kedamath 
temple. Songanga, a tributary of Mandakini, originates from the 
lake. The camp Site is extremely beautiful and calm. It is said that 
mythological Vasuki nag (snake) used to live here. 


0) Chaurabarital 

The track to this small and beautiful fresh water lake is 4 kms 
ahead of Kedarnath temple on the right bank of river Mandakini. 
There isn't any thing like maintained track. The walk on the mo- 
raines of Chaurabari glacier, which is punctuated by pretty Alpine 
flowers, is difficult but interesting. The snout of the glacier has to 
be left behind to reach the lake, which is just on the back of a 
knife-like ridge, made by the lateral moraines of the glacier. The 
hidden quality of the lake might be responsible for giving it this 
name. However, due to the immersion of Gandhiji's ashes into the 
lake it has assumed a new name Gandhi Sarovar. Melting of snow 
on the higher region is the source of the lake water. Apart from 
these lakes both the districts are having many more small lakes and 
Springs. 


Natural Springs 

Waterfalls and springs are very common sights in Garhwal in 
general and upper Garhwal in particular. The sight of many large 
and beautiful waterfalls and springs on the road sides are enjoyable 
while travelling by road in Uttarkashi and Chamoli districts. But the 
presence of hot water springs in the snowy heights is the most 
fascinating phenomenon of nature. All the Char Dhams are blessed 
with this gift of nature. These hot springs are at or around the Char 
Dhams. 

For Gangotri, the hot water springs are on the way at Ganga- 
nani, for Yamunotri at Yamunotri, for Badrinath at Badrinath and 
for Kedarnath at Gaurikund. Prior to worship bathing is necessary
