Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


56 Tourism іп Garhwal Himalaya 


and this becomes a pleasant exercise due to these hot water springs 
in such high and cold areas. For tired trekkers and mountaineers 
these springs are a boon in disguise. Bath in sulpher water removes 
the tiredness of the body. 

At Yamunotri boiling water comes out from the pot holes of 
solid rocks (Divyashila). In Suryakund, rice or potatoes tied in a 
cloth are dipped for a few minutes for cooking and thereafter it is 
usually taken back home as "prasad". The temperature of the 
water in Suryakund is 180°C. Ganganani, once a popular halting _ 
place of age-old pilgrimage of Gangotri has now lost its religious 
importance due to the introduction of motor transport to Gangotri. 
Ganganani is 43 kms from Uttarkashi on way to Gangotri. The 
construction of small pools for bathing and swimming was luring 
the crowd back to Ganganani. But earthquake of 20th October 1991 
disturbed the source. One of the unknown hot water springs of 
Uttarkashi district is 5 kms from Dodital in the dense forest. This 
too, can be developed as an attraction for the trekkers. 

Gaurikund which is 13 kms down below Kedamath, on the 
right bank of Mandakini has two hot water springs (53°C and 
23°C). These springs are real boon for the weary travellers and pil- 
grims. For the comfort of travellers a regular bathing place has 
been constructed here. Another important hot spring is just below 
Badrinath temple. This thermal spring having a temperature of 
49°C provides hot water bath to the pilgrims and tourists before 
Badrish worship. A regular bath with tolerable hot water (mixed 
with cold water from the cold water spring) is provided round the 
clock. 

Two interesting thermal springs are located at Reni, 6 kms 
ahead of Tapovan (Joshimath) on the bank of river Dhauli. It is 
generally visited by the trekkers and mountaineers to the region. 

. All these thermal springs сап be developed as recreational and 
health resorts. > 


2.2.4 Wildlife ; 

Wilderness is a necessity said, John Muir, the sage of Sierra. 
Thoreau thought the health of civilization needed the tonic of the 
wild. Since time immemorial man got inspiration from nature in 
improving his ways of living and maintaining coordination with 


СС-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and ефапцоггі 
Elements of Adventure Tourism 57 


surroundings. Frank Smythe found that the hills had a capacity for 
welding into a harmonious whole the physical, mental and Spiritual 
qualities of man. Such are the qualities of wilderness that man 
forgets his sorrows, pains and miseries with its closeness. As one 
passes through hills, dales and mountains, the journey becomes a 
discovery of new things and self realisation. 

The Hindu sages of the ancient times believed in the healing 
touch of nature and regarded communion with nature as spiritually 
elevating. Technical advancement and modernisation are forcing 
man to think and respect nature as a cure to all kinds of ills that 
afflict man today. Hence the varied flora and fauna of the region 
are becoming one of the main attractions for the tourists. 


Flora 

As per the report of Hill Area Development Board (1987-88) 
the total area of Chamoli and Uttarkashi districts was 906 and 801 
thousand hectares, respectively. Out of this the area under forest 
cover was 527 and 710 thousand hectares. The remaining area . 


: was under cultivation. The great chunk of uncultivated area can be 
- utilised for wildlife tourism and recreation without disturbing the 


eco-system. 
On the basis of characteristics, the forest of the region is di- 
vided into four zones. 


a) The central zone 

b) The northern zone 

c) The northern bushland 
d) Alpine meadows 


a) Central Zone 

The central zone is thickly populated and cultivated, hence it is 
more or less devoid of forests and other wildlife. Wherever there is 
forest it is dominated by chir pine (Pinus longifolia syn. P. 
roxburghii). Other species which frequently occur with chir are 
amla (Emblica officinalis), sandan (Ougeinia dalbergioides), timla 
(Ficus roxburghii) and kath-mahwa (Glochidion velutinum). But in 
upper reaches of this zone, blue pine (P. wallichiana) often associ- 
ated with banj, oak and maple is found in abundance. This vegeta- 
tion is commonly found in both the districts. Moru or tilong (Quer- 


СС-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized оу, Агуа Samaj Foundation Chennai апа eGangotri 


58 Tourism іп Garhwal Himalaya 


cus dilatata) is found between 1800 and 2750 m. The kharsu 
(Quercus semecarpifolia) is found between 2200 and 3000 т, Вш- 
ans (Rhododendron arbo-reum), the queen of hills is widely distrib- 
uted in all oak forests mixed with blue pine. The red flowers of 
burans change the forest scenery from February to April. Ringal 
(hill bamboo) an undergrowth companion of banj and kharsu, 
makes the forest dense. Rich epiphytic moss, lichen and ferns decor 
the forest most. Panger (Himalayan horse chestnut), silver fir 
(Abies pindrow), spruce (Picea morinda), locally known as raga 
` and rausala are also found in this altitude. 


b) Northern Zone 

This zone is also full of varied flora. In Їасї її has the richest 
vegetation of Garhwal. Himalayan oak still dominates the scene, 
others are burans (Rhododendron arboreum), silver fir (Abies pin- 
drow), spruce (Picea smithiana), cypress (Cypressus Tarulosa) and 
blue pine etc. When oak starts disappearing Pangar (Aesculus 
indica), kabasi (Corylus jacquemontii), kanjula (Acer caesium) and 
ringal (A. jaunsarensis) take its place. Ferns, lichen and creepers 
are abundantly found here. Forest of Har-ki-doon, Tuni, Dodital, 
Belakkhal, Dingad valley, Yamunotri, Gangotri, Kedar Ganga val- 
ley, Wan, Nandprayag, Kedarnath valley, Tungnath, Rudranath, 
Ukhimath, Deoriatal, Valley of Flowers and outer Nanda Devi: 
Sanctuary etc are the finest examples of this forest. Fir and spruce 
are densely found from Rudraprayag to Pipalkoti and Ramni to 
Dibrugheta. 

Botanical Survey of India and other reports revealed that more 
than 200 species of Gymnosperms; 180 species of Angiosperms 
grow in the Nanda Devi National Park. Mr. Batten was surprised 
to see the oaks of Niti and deodars of Malari valley. Rhododendron 
dwarfs of Ruinsaratal, Dodital (Uttarkashi district), Kuari pass, 
Tungnath and Rudranath (Chamoli district) with crimson and 
mauve flowers change the scenery entirely. Marco Pallis praised 
the vegetation of Bhagirathi valley. 


c) Northern Bushland 
This is the zone where the tree line ends up with bushes and 
difficult inhospitable terrain starts along with cold climate (annual

precipitation less than 25 cm). But nature's bounty of rich flora 
attracts people to make a visit. The flora is xerophytic shrubs and 
bushes scattered thinly all over the earth surface. 

Safed Purcha (Artemisia maritima), Kala Purcha (A. scarorum) 
and Jhaura (Caragana pygmaea) occur in between 2750 and 3700 
m above sea level. There are three species of Juniper (Juniper 
pseudosabina, Juniper communis and Juniper macropoda). Juniper 
is the most affected flora by human and animal interference at this 
altitude. It is easy to ignite even when it is green. Dimar (Caragana 
pygmaea) is common found flora of this zone. Rhododendron bush 
also appears in this zone. Being aromatic it is used for making joss. 
sticks. 

The most suitable time to visit this zone is from mid June to the 
first week of September. 


d) Alpine Meadows 

Meadows of Garhwal are situated in between the bush line and 
the permanent snow line. Here the land is most inhospitable and 
rugged, but full of Himalayan grasses, herbs and floral plants. 
Scanty juniper and rhododendron bushes are found here too. 
Though altitudinally the Alpine pasture land varies from place to 
place due to physiographical conditions, but they are generally 
found in between 3500 and 4900 m above the sea level in Garhwal. 
It snows there in the month of November and it remains a frozen 
hostile waste land for six months. Later from mid April snow starts 
melting. With brief rain showers the waste land gets transformed 
into a miraculous valley of flowers. 

Garhwal is famous for its Alpine meadows. Most popular are 
Har-ki-doon, Ruinsara tal, Dayara, Belakkhal, Kush-Kalyani, 
Tapovan, Nandanvan in Uttarkashi district and Auli, Gorson Auli, 
Vedni, Bistola, Kuari pass, Madamaheshwar, Rudranath, Tungnath, 
Valley of Flowers, Hemkund-Lokpal, Nanda Devi outer sanctuary 
and Manpai in Chamoli district. These beautiful calm and quite 
places provide a unique and fine retreat, an eden of flowers to the 
visitors of scorched plains. Highly nutritious mamla grass locally 
known as buggi, grows in these high pasture lands. Hence these 
Pastures are locally known as Bugyals. These grasses bear highly 
aromatic flowers. Most common among them are Potentialla (three

60 Tourism іп Garhwal Himalaya 


colours—crimson red, yellow and orange) and blue Primula delicu- 
lata. Smythe identified nine spices of Primula in Garhwal,' 
Sexifraga, Forget-me-not, Iris, Marsh-Merry gold, Yellow Butter- 
Cups, Pink Geranium, Blue Gentian, Prime rose, Poppy, Rosace, 
Deep red Thymes, Yellow stripped Slipper Orchid, Blue Delphin- 
ium, Aster, Daise, Red Straw Berry, Sedum, Anemone, Brahm 
Kamal, Globe flower, Himalayan yellow Poppy, Polygonum 
Affine, Himalayan Lily, Himalayan Rose, Cobra Lily, Jacob’s Lad- 
der, Fritilaries and dark purple Monks hood etc. These flowers are 
densely distributed in the meadows of Garhwal. Mr. Holdsworth 
does some candid speaking when he says, “Let me confess at the 
outset that as the botanist of the Kamet expedition of 1931, I am an 
imposter. My qualifications for the post are wholly inadequate, 
and my opportunities for doing the job thoroughly were naturally 
rather meagre.””” These flowers are generally found in most of 
the pastures in Garhwal Himalaya. These flowers in glacial valleys, 
moraines, under rocks, ledges and crannies present the views 
of most beautiful rock gardens of earth. August-September is the 
most suitable time to visit these Alpine pasture lands. End of Sep- 
tember is the seeding time, the frost and snow later burn all the 
flora of this heavenly region and ends the floral life. These pastures 
are the real Himalayan holiday resorts with lots of things to explore 
and enjoy. 


Fauna 

Northern Garhwal is rich in varied fauna. Here the fauna 
adapts itself to enjoy the ecological factors like cold, aridity, snow 
cover and humidity with the increase in altitude. The fine adapta- 
bility is clearly visible in their body cover (hair/feather and colour) 
that provides resistance to insolation and radiation. The forest of 
pine, oak, blue pine, rhododendron, Cypress, fir, spruce, birch, juni- 
per, ringal апа’ the Alpine meadows provide an ideal habitat for 
wild animals and singing birds. 


Animals 

The most commonly found animals are langur, red monkey, 
brown bear, black bear, common fox, leopard, snow leopard, bark- 
ing dear, sambar, musk dear, serow, porcupine, tahr etc. Butterflies

Elements of Adventure Tourism 61 


of different colours and sizes and beetles are also found in this 
area. 


Avifauna 

The higher region of Garhwal is full of colourful singing avi- 
fauna. Most commonly found are—Himalayan whistling thrush, 
Himalayan golden crest, western variegated laughing thrush, monal 
and koklas pheasant, partridge, Himalayan golden eagle, Hima- 
layan bearded vulture and snow partridge etc. 


Fish 

Apart from rich mammal and avifauna, higher Himalayan wa- 
ter bodies of Garhwal are rich in aquatic life also. The river and 
streams abound in fishes. Most common are mahaseer (Indian 
salmon), Indian trout and goonch (fresh water shark), kalabans, 
pharkata and chilwa. Alaknanda, Mandakini, Pinder, Bhagirathi 
and Yamuna are the ideal habitat for fresh water fish but rivers and 
lakes of upper Garhwal abound in the Himalayan trout. 

In 1910 the rulers of Garhwal made fish hatcheries, one in 
Kalyani (Uttarkashi district) and the other at Tilwari (Chamoli dis- 
trict) by procuring a consignment of eyed ova of brown trout 
(Salmo trutta fario) from Kashmir. In 1951 these hatcheries were 
transferred to Animal Husbandry Department of U.P., but the con- 
dition of these hatcheries started deteriorating and the stock of fish 
went down. Now there is a separate Directorate of Fisheries and 
both the hatcheries come under the Assistant Director of Fisheries, 
Uttarkashi. In Talwari the original stock of trout perished which 
was replaced by the seed of rainbow trout (Salmotrutta gairdner). 

For flora and fauna some areas of these two districts of 
Garhwal are very famous. 


2.2.5 National Parks 

Parks are becoming very popular now a days, when life has 
become very much mechanical and lethargic in the cities. Park 
resources—flora and fauna are very much in demand list of the 
tourists. Parks are part of large tourism recreation conservation 
System. Tourism development also is part of a large tourism recrea- 


tion conservation System. It depends as much on the identification

and protection of natural and cultural resources as it does upon the 
building facilities. According to Indian Board of Wildlife, “A na- 


. tional park is an area dedicated by state for all time to conserve 


natural and historical objects of national significance and wildlife 
and where provision is made for enjoyment of the same by the 
public."'* 

There are two national parks in Chamoli district. Valley of 
flowers and Nanda Devi are world famous for their beauty. Figure 
5 shows the national parks and sanctuaries of the study area. 


a) Govind National Park 

In 1955 Upper Tons valley was declared as Govind Pashu 
Vihar (sanctuary), after the name of Pandit Govind Ballabh Pant. 
Later in 1990, it was notified as national park, comprising the 
whole of Supin range in the Tons forest division. The area of 953 
54 kms is a thick forest of pine, blue pine, oak, rhododendron, | 


.deodar, fir, spruce, cypress, rhododendron dwarf, birch and Alpine 


herbs and shrubs. The inhabiting animals are—common fox, langur, 
wild cat, red monkey, Himalayan black bear, brown bear, barking 
deer, sambar, musk deer, Indian marten, Jeopard, serow, thar, mar- 
mot and snow leopard. I still remember the small beautiful cub of 
snow leopard that we caught on the trek to Ruinsaratal (on the right 
side of Har-ki-Doon valley) in 1982 during an outing with adven- 
ture Course trainees of NIM, Uttarkashi. It was so small that we fed 
it milk with bottle, but later we left it near its den. Snow leopard is 
declared as an endangered species, and a plan to protect the snow 
leopard is reportedly on the anvil. 

Game Birds: Most of the birds found here are rare and pretty. 
Among the game birds monal pheasant, westerned horned phea- 
Sant, cheer pheasant, peura partridge, khalij pheasant, chukor 
and black partridge are the main attractions. At higher altitudes 
тей billed and yellow billed chough, snow pigeon, Himalayan 
nutcracker (specially in blue pine forest) аге the main attractions 
for bird watchers. But at moderate heights red-billed babbler, 
blue magpie, whistling thrush, red-billed scarlet, minivet, verditer 
flycatcher and grey-winged black bird (common in oak forest, per- 


ү the finest of all the Himalayan song birds) are commonly 
ound.

Luxuriant Har-ki-doon valley has а perfect backdrop of snowy 
mountain peaks and dense forest of pine, deodar and other conifer- 
ous that meet the vast stretches of meadows. It is said that Bhog 
Datt, a great warrior of Mahabharata who fought against the Pan- 
davas, resided here. It is therefore, that Duryodhana, the eldest of 
the Kaurava is worshipped in Tons valley. The best time to visit is 
from June to September. The altitude variation in the park is from 
1400 to 6100 m. From May to August one can see the wild flora of 
the park in full bloom. Marshall described the wilderness as, 
“Timeless dimensionless, all encompassing dynamic uncluttered 
by the artist’s conception and a form of beauty that gratifies all the 
senses.”" But with the arrival of winter (cold, frost and wind) most 
of the flora burns and all the pastures get tanned. A cloudless sky 
gives an opportunity to enjoy the wide scenery of white wealth of 
the nature. Figure 6 depicts the Govind National Park. 


b) Valley of Flowers National Park 

Bhyunder valley widely known as the Valley of Flowers in 
eastern Garhwal, was first discovered by the famous mountaineer 
Mr. Frank Smythe, the leader of successful Kamet expedition, in 
1931. He was so much fascinated with this bio-climatically rich 
valley that he later returned and collected many species of flowers. 
He has recorded his thrill in his book ‘The Valley of Flowers’. This 
8 kms long and 2 kms wide valley is full of colourful flowers of 
different sizes, shapes and aroma. The surroundings are unique. On 
the north of this valley stand cliffs 2000 m high and on the north- 
east is the beautiful mountain Rataban, that catches the attention of 
the visitors and gives perfection to the scenery. Smythe rightly . 
Says, “.... no photographer can do justice to the grandeur and 
ethereal beauty of such scene.” The wide stretches of northern 
Slopes are decorated with silver birch punctuated with Alpine 
meadows, the southern slopes are carpeted with lush green grass 
and colourful flowers. In fact the valley lies in between Bhyunder- 
Kantha pass (5090 m) and Khuntakhal pass (4425 m), from Bami- 
nidhar (3560 m) to the base of Rataban peak (6166 m). The entire 
valley falls into the Bhyunder-Ganga catchment area and covers 
Hemkund-Lokpal, Pushpavati valley and its tributaries. 

The scenery and flora changes from Ghangaria (a halting place

enroute to Valley of Flowers). It is 5 kms from Bhyunder village. 
From here the real ascent starts with terrific natural beauty. It is a 
very beautiful valley with magnificent forest and tumbling streams. 

In 1981 the valley was declared as one of the Himalayan na- 
tional parks. The 3.5 kms trek from Ghangaria to the Valley of 
Flowers is most enchanting. The forest of silver birch merges into 
the green stretches, decorated with colourful flowers, mud, boul- 
ders, rivulets, rocky cliffs and white mountains. 

Some of the flowers of the valley are Potentilla (P. argyro- 
phylla) in red, yellow and orange colours, Primula minutissima, 
Primula nivalis, Primula stuartii, Anemone (A. obtusiloba), Gen- 
tian (Gentian stipitata), Androsace (A. rotundifolia), monk's hood 
(Aconitum heterophyllum), purple ipomea (Ipomea Purpurea), 
globe-flower (Trolluis acaulis), musk thistle (Carduus nutans), 
cream yellow Himalayan lily (Lilium oxypetalum), Himalayan rose 
(Rosa macrophylla), snowy Lindelofia (Lindelofia spectabilis), co- 
bra lily (Arisaema costatum), Jacob’s ladder (Polemonium caerul- 
eum), Himalayan yellow poppy (Meconopsis paniculata), Wal- 
lich’s blue poppy (Meconopsis wallichii), Saxifraga (S. flagellaris) 
and Iris (J. kumaonensis). The nature’s eden changes its hue and 

colour weekly, fortnightly and some times daily. The life-span of 
flowers varies from few days to months. In the month of May snow 
tends to melt and seed sprouting begins, June watches the small 
plants with buds and few flowers, July: witnesses the blossom but 
August is lucky enough to see the valley in prime youth and 
colours. So are the lucky visitors of August as they watch the 
youthful valley. From September onwards the colours and youth of 
the coloured valley starts fading and the arrival of dry autumn 
heralds the decay and decomposition. October comes with frost and 
cold and tans the valley and again it becomes an isolated, desolate 
pocket of earth. The most suitable time to visit the valley is mid 
August. Figure 7 depicts Valley of Flowers national park. 


C) Kedarnath Musk Deer Park 

Kedarnath valley is very rich in its natural wealth. The forest of 
this valley is a paradise on ѕисһ:а high altitude. Musk deer is the 
Most sought-after animal of the valley. With a view to protect this 
endangered animal this valley was declared as musk deer sanctuary

in 1972. The area of the sanctuary is 967.2 sq kms and the entire 
sanctuary lies in Kedarnath forest division. Therefore it is named as 
Kedarnath musk deer sanctuary. Kedarnath ‘shrine and other old 
temples—Trijuginarayan, Madmaheshwar, Tungnath, Rudranath 
and Ansuya Devi are also situated within the bounds of this sanctu- 
ary. The Chamoli-Ukhimath motor road skirts this sanctuary from 
the south and serves as an excellent approach road for the sanctu- 


_ ary. Figure 8 shows Kedarnath musk deer park. 


Flora: The beautiful thick forests along with road and the val- 
ley are the main attraction. Here pine grows upto 2000 m but moist 
hill sides are clad with oak. Above this, the forest mainly comprises 
deodar, fir, spruce, thuner, cypress and blue pine. Among broad 
leaved trees rhododendron, walnut, oak and horse chest-nut are the 
main species. But birch occurs after coniferous forest. Above this 
lies rhododendron dwarfs, juniper and other thorny bushes. In be- 
tween the bush line and permanent snow line lies the pasture land 
which abounds in a number of medicinal and other plants, 

Fauna: Due to varying climate and vegetation a large number 
of wild animals are found all over the sanctuary. Upto 3000 m 
above the sea level, barking deer, sambar, wild cat, panther, black 
bear, ghoral and wild boar are found. Above 3000 m Himalayan 
thar, bharal, snow leopard, musk dear, serow, ghoral and Hima- 
layan brown bear are found. 

Avifauna: Among avifauna khalij, koklas and monal pheasants, 
snow partridge are fairly common. Hunting and poaching have 
created a critical condition for the birds. 

Monal pheasant and musk deer are declared as endangered 
species. A drive from Ukhimath to Mandal is most fascinating. A 
man who has ever been to that area can never forget that dense 
mixed forest of oak, blue pine, cypress, fir, rhododendron and 


birch. One shivers even in the month of June due to cold. The 


beautiful earth slopes of Chopta can be used for snow ski in winter. 
The forest rest house and GMVN’s bungalows are best located. 


d) Nanda Devi National Park 

Nanda Devi national park is situated at 30° 16! - 30° 32' М and 
79° 49! - 80° 2' E with an area of 800 sq kms. This park is drained 
by the Rishi Ganga and is clustered between the glaciers of Gan- 


CC-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai апа eGangotri 
70 Tourism in Garhwal Himalaya 


gotri and Milam. Nanda Devi is the second highest peak of India. 
Altitude variation of the sanctuary is from 2100 to 7817 m. The 
whole area is known as Nanda Devi sanctuary, a name first be- 
stowed on it by Mr. Ruttledge of Everest fame. Eric Shipton and 
Bill Tillman took years to get into the sanctuary through Rishi 
Ganga. Finally in 1934 they got success in penetrating the sanctu- 
ary and reached at the height of 6227 m. Later in 1936 H. Tillman 
and N.E. Odell of Anglo-American expedition got success in reach- 
ing the summit. It was the cherished dream of world mountaineers. 
In 1939 at the Instance of Shipton and Tillman the 182.63 sq kms 
of Nanda Devi basin was declared as Nanda Devi Wild Life sanc- 
tuary.' The cup-shaped sanctuary is demarcated by a huge amphi- 
theatre of mountain range, offering ice walls, corniced ridges and 
almost seventy named and unnamed peaks. The sanctuary has got 
two horse shoe shapes, one outer sanctuary and the other inner 
sanctuary. The outer sanctuary is a dense mixed forest which has 
Alpine pasture lands adorned by beautiful seasonal colourful flow- 
ers. The inner sanctuary includes a number of high peaks and major 
glacier system. It consists of Ramini, Changabang and north Rishi ` 
glacier in the north and south Nand Devi glacier, south Rishi, 
Trisul, Bethartoli, Rounti and Nanda Ghunti glacier in the south. 
Each glacier system gives rise to streams and all the streams end in 
Rishi stream. Ultimately this enters the awe-some lower gorge 
guarded by overhanging cliffs, and skirts Lata peak to merge with 
the Dhauli Ganga near Reni village. Some of the well known peaks 
from west to east are Dunagiri (7066 m), Changabang (6864. m), 
Kalanka (6931 m), Nanda Devi East (7434 m) and Nanda Devi 
(7817 m). From east to west Nanda Kot (6631 m), Devasthan (6678 
m), Maiktoli (6805 m), Mrigthuni (6855 m), Trisul (7120 m) and 
Bethartoli (6352 m). The area is segmented by a series of parallel 
ridges and the most important is Devasthan-Rishikot ridge, which 
divides the Rishi gorge at the base of Nanda Devi and Separates the 
inner sanctuary from the outer sanctuary. The northern wall from 
Lata to Rishi pahar is the divide between the Nanda Devi basin and 
the Dhauli Ganga. Figure 9 pives the detailed picture of Nanda 
Devi national park.

Due to its beautiful surroundings and rich wildlife, the sanctu- 
ary was declared as national park along with the Valley of Flowers 
under the Wild Life (Protection) Act, 1972 in September 1983. 

Monsoon begins here from third week of June. Thundering 
clouds are a very common sight in the valley. Inside the park the 
snow line is well above 4500 m. Though the park receives little 
precipitation, it supports a lush green vegetation cover than the 
other valleys. 

Flora: The forest forms a very small percentage of the flora of 
the sanctuary and is restricted to the Rishi gorge. The coniferous 
trees grow in Dudh Ganga valley and Dibrugheta glade. Fir (Abies 
pindrow) is most dominant, deodar (Cedrus deodara) is found at 
Lata, but rhododendron and white and pink rose (Rosa brunonii and 
R. macrophylla) are found in admixture with fir and deodars. Birch 
(Betula utilis) is found all the way from Dibrugheta to Ramni. 
Lichen and moss covers the tree trunk. The Botanical Survey of 
India and expedition reports reveal that more than 200 species of 
plants (8 species of pteridophytes, 5 species of gymnosperms, 188 
of angiosperms) grow in the park. About 15.species e.g. Aconitum 
spp., Circeaster agrestis, Epipogum lislera sp, Meconopsis 
aculeata, Nardostachys grandiflora, Orchis latifolia, Podophyllum 
hexandrum, Saussurea obvallata are considered rare and endan- 
gered.” Between the timber line and the permanent snow line there 
аге the extensive meadows with scant but evergreen shrubs (e.g. 
Rhododendron anthopogon, Juniper and Salix species), herbs (e.g. 
Aconitum, Cyananthus and Polygonum species) and grass (e.g. 
Lantonia, Festuca and Poa species). 

From Lata Kharak to Sarsupatal the trek is full of Alpine vege- 
tation with beautiful aromatic flowers. These are generally found in ` 
Lata Kharak, Dhransi, Didrugheta, Deodi, Ramni and Sarsupatal. 
The local people believe that the first Brahm-kamal should be 
plucked for the Goddess Nanda, otherwise she will be offended and 
make journey hazardous. Most of the flowers found in Valley of 
Flowers are found here too. 

Fauna; Nanda Devi national park is rich and varied in its 
faunal wealth. Among the mammals found are langur (Presbytis 
entellus schislaceus), Himalayan black bear (Ursus arctos), yellow 
bellied weasel (Mutela kathiah), leopard (Panther pardus), snow 


CC-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai апа eGangotri 


Elements of Adventure Tourism 73 


leopard (Panther uncia), serow (Capricornis sumatraensis), 
Himalayan tahr (Hemitragus jemlahicus), bharal or blue sheep 
(Pseudois nayaur), goral (Nemorhaedus goral), musk deer (Mos- 
chus moschiferus), mouse hare (Ochatona roylei) and Indian field 
mouse (Mus boodeega). А 

Avifauna: Very rich апа уагіей birds live in this park. Lamba 
during his study in 1981-84 fpund 80 birds of 27 families. Out- 
standing among them are Himalayan golden eagle (Aguila chrysae- 
tos hodgsoni), black eagle (Ictinaetus malayaensis), Himalayan 
bearded vulture or lammergeier (Gypaetus barbatus), snow par- 
tridge (lerwa leswa), Himalayan snow cock (Tetraogallus Himalay- 
ensis), chukor partridge (Alectoris chukor), Himalayan monal (Lo- 
phophorous impejanus), koklas pheasant (Ceriornis macrolophus), 
west Himalayan snow pigeon (Columba leuconota), Indian spotted 
dove (Streptopelia chinensis suratenses), Himalayan great barbat 
(Megalaima virens marshallorum), western variegated laughing 
thrush (Trochalopteron variegatum ‘similie), Himalayan whistling 
thrush (Myiophoneus caeruleus temminckii) and western red 
headed tit (Aegithaliscus concinnus redalei).? 


22.6 Interesting Treks 


а) Kuari Pass ` 

Kuari pass, at an altitude of 4268 m above the sea level, is a 
well known trek in Chamoli district. Lord Curzon went upto the 
Kuari pass in 1905. So the trek is known as Curzon road. This pass 
can be approached by three sides—(1) Tapovan (near Joshimath)- 
Khulara and Kuari pass, (2) Joshimath-Auli-Gorson-Gailgarh- 
Kuari, and (3) Ghat-Ramni-Kuari. 

Kuari pass is one of the best vantage point of this area. It gives 
extensive panoramic views of the Himalaya of north-east and the 
vast valley of south-east. The peaks to be seen are Nilkanth, 
Kamet, Nanda Devi, Dunagiri, Bethartoli, Hati Parbat and Devas- 


` Шап etc. It is liked by nature lovers and trekkers both. 


b) Auli 
. 4 
Auli attracts tourists in summer as well as in winter. In summer 
the pasture land is most inviting with green carpet and colourful 


CC-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


74 Tourism in Garhwal Himalaya 


flowers. It is a beautiful sight for camping. This Alpine meadow is 
8 kms from Joshimath along a jeepable road and provides pano- 
ramic view of Nanda Devi, Kamet, Dunagiri and a chain of un- 
named peaks. Newly constructed ropeway from Joshimath to Auli 
is the longest in Asia. 

Winter imparts an enticing colour and charm to the place. From 
January to March the slopes are usually covered with thick snow 
about 3 m deep. The stretch is approximately 3 kms with altitude 
variation of around 500 m. It is one of the finest ski-slopes of India. 
Skiing courses of varying duration are run by Garhwal Mandal 
Vikas Nigam. Equipment, board and lodging facilities and transport 
are provided by them. 


c) Dayara Pasture 

This beautiful valley of varied flowers, is situated in Uttarka- 
shi district. It is 18 kms from Bhatwari in the thick forest. Its gently 
sloping hill sides with many small lakes lure one away from mun- 
dane worldliness. The whole valley smiles in summer with pretty 
aromatic flowers of different sizes and shapes. This area can be 
utilized and developed as a snow-ski resort. 


d) Gaumukh, Tapovan and Nandanvan 

This beautiful glacial region (Gangotri, Tapovan and Nandan- 
van) can be developed as the first glacial park of the country. This 
trek area is most suitable for adventure lovers, rock climbers, and 
mountaineers. It has got a variety of flora and fauna also. For 
reaching Tapovan or Nandanvan one has to walk from Gangotri. 


Gangoti - Chirbasa (9 kms) 
Chirbasa - Bhojbasa (5 kms) 
Bhojbasa - Gaumukh(4kms) | 
Gaumukh - Tapovan (3 kms) 
Gaumukh - Nandanvan (6 kms) or: 
Tapovan - Nandanvan (3 kms) 


From Gangotri to Bhojbasa the walk amid the forest of pine, 
blue pine, birch and juniper is easy. Heards of bharal (blue moun- 
tain goats) are often spotted on the mountain ridges. The trek is 
really enthralling. At Bhojbasa accommodation is provided by 


CC-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


Elements of Adventure Tourism 75 


GMVN, Lalbaba and few others, but beyond Gaumukh there is no 
man-made ‘shelter, except open camp sites and rock ‘caves. The 
view of Manda, Shivling, Sudarshan, Bhagirathi group of peaks are 
superb. 

From Gaumukh one can go to Tapovan or Nandanvan. For 
Tapovan one has to walk 3 kms over the Gangotri glacier. Tapovan 
is a beautiful camp site at an altitude of 4463 m above sea level. 

From Tapovan one has to walk 3 kms for reaching Nandanvan. 
The trek is difficult due to moraine walk and falling stones but the 
camp site at Nandanvan is superb. 


e) Nagini Devi 

Though there is not any marked track for Nagini Devi but a 
local guide can take the tourist up to the destination without getting 
lost. This area was known as Bailkhila Parbat in the past due to 
Rishi Bailkhila’s meditation in the region. The route to be followed 
is Chaurangikhal trek upto Sankarna dhar. From here the snow 
view is fantastic. The track passes through dense oak forest and 
other rich flora. The temple of Nagini Devi has recently been reno- | 
vated. It is dedicated to an avtar of Goddess Parvati and can be 
developed for the trekkers. 


f) Har-ki-Doon 
Har-ki-Doon is a beautiful small valley at an altitude of 3566 m 
in Tons valley of Uttarkashi district. The track starts from Netwar 
and passes through Sankri, Taluka and Osla villages. This valley 
-has a unique characteristic as Kauravas are worshipped hereVil- 
lage Osla has a Duryodhan temple. The track passes through beau- 
tiful thick virgin forest and a part of the area comes under Govind 
Pashu Vihar. The smallest trek distance is 42 kms. The scenery is 
beautiful. The views of Swargarohini, Banderpunch and Kalanag 

are fascinating. 


2.2.7 Climate 

Climate is of particular significance to tourism. Higher and 
middle Himalayan regions of Garhwal have got many pockets 
which fall in a salubrious climatic zone. In fact, tourist market of 
hills totally depends upon the weather and climate. The months of

Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


16 Tourism іп Garhwal Himalaya 


May and June attract maximum number of tourists and pilgrims to 
Badri-Kedar, Gangotri-Yamunotri and higher reaches of Garhwal 
Himalaya, but from the first week of July this number starts declin- 
ing. The increase and decrease is due to weather and climatic vari- 
ation in the region. Climate is the most single important factor 
which has a Close bearing on tourism in mountains. It plays a most 
vital.role in making an area a tourist destination. 

The parameters of climate are sunshine hours, cloud-cover, 
temperature, wind velocity, vapour pressure and precipitation. Fine 
weather with warmth and sunshine is one of the most important 
attractions of a tourist area. Good weather is particularly an impor- 
tant ingredient in holidaying. It can make or mar a holiday. It is 
doubtful that the Alpine winter resorts of France, Switzerland and 
Austria would be as popular as they are if the resort were clouded 
and sunless. All the hill stations of India (Shimla, Darjeeling, 
Nainital and Mussoorie) were developed by the Britishers because 
of their climatic assets. Upper Garhwal is varied in climate. Each 
valley basin and ridge creates its own subclimate. Location, altitude 
and wind velocity affects the climate a lot. Southern slopes are 
sunnier and receive more insolation than the northern slopes. "Of 
all the geographic influences to which man is subjected in his life 
upon the earth, climate seems to be the most potent." '? Unfortu- 
nately no new survey of hill stations has been undertaken after the 
Britishers. Therefore over-crowding is one of the biggest problems 
of our hill stations in the summer season. The need of the hour is to 
explore new hill stations or climatic resorts. 

Altitudinally Garhwal can be divided into four climatic zones. 


1. Warm (Upto - 1300 m) 
2. Temperature (1300 - 2100 m) 
3. Cold (2100 - 3030 m) 
4. Glacial (above 3400 m) 


The area under study falls in between 2100 to 7817 m. All the 
hill stations of India are located in temperature zone. In Garhwal 
Gwaldam, Joshimath, Kalimath, Uttarkashi, Barkot etc come in the 
same zone. In these places summer months are cool and pleasant 
but winter months are cold with mist and frost. The temperature 
from April to October is 15.5°C to 26°C with no cold, but from 


CC-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


Elements of Adventure Tourism 77 


October to February it is always very cold due to snow fall. Areas 
between 2200 and 3030 m elevation enjoy cold temperate climate. 
Here the summer mean temperature is 4.4°C to 10°C. The winter 
temperature is below freezing point and snow fall is frequent. 

In both the districts tourist potentials are located at an altitude 
above 3000 m and have Alpine climate. Incidently all the Char 
Dhams Badri, Kedar, Gangotri and Yamunotri and most of the 
treks are located in this zone, but higher altitude treks and snow- 
covered peaks are in glacial zone. In alpine zone during summers 
the days are cool but the nights are colder. Winter is severely cold 
spreading over a period of seven months. Here snow fall begins in 
the last week of September and lasts till the beginning of April. 
Within these months the temperature becomes precarious. During 
this period the region becomes white with snow and isolated. All 
the highlanders (Bhotias of Niti and Mana valley) come down to 
lower altitudes with their herds. Generally the region remains open 
for the tourists only for five months and the low line areas for six- 
seven months. But like Auli, many places have the potential for 
winter tourism (snow-skiing, mountaineering and trekking). 

Rainfall is another factor that affects the climate. The summer 
monsoon approaches the submountain tract around the middle of 
June and the higher reaches get it by the third week of June. There, 
monsoon showers relieve man, earth and its vegetation cover from 
scorching sun. In higher Himalaya the climate almost remains be- 
yond the influence of the periodical rains. Northwards the air and 
soil become drier and arid. At comparatively lower altitude, the 
landscape starts becoming greener and colourful with rains. But 
heavy rains result in landslides and road blockages. This creates the 
biggest problem for the pilgrims and tourists. 

However, with the showers of rains the season of trekkers be- 
gins as they go upto Har-ki-Doon, Govind national park, Ruin- 
Saratal, Dodital, Tapovan, Nandanvan, Kaljndi Khal, Valley of 
Flowers, Tungnath, Rudranath, Madmaheshwar, Kalpeshwar, 
Urgam, Kuari and Roopkund etc. xe 

As a whole, the best time for the tourist and pilgrim is from 
April to June and September to the first week of October, but for a 
trekker July to September is the most suitable time. November and 
April are the driest months of Garhwal, whereas October is the sun- 


СС-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


78 Tourism in Garhwal Himalaya 


niest month with clear sky and maximum visibility. Profit largely in 
tourism depends on good climate. Adventure tourism also depends 
upon favourable weather conditions. Most of the mountaineers who 
fail on the mountains, rightly attribute it to bad weather. In fact 
climate and weather play a vital role in deciding the tourist poten- 
tial of destination area. The climate of Garhwal is conducive to the 
development of summer resorts and winter sports. Usually the win- 
ter days are sunny, warm and pleasant and most suitable for snow- 
skiing and winter climbing. 


2.3 RELIGIOUS ATTRACTIONS 


Badri, Kedar, Gangotri and Yamunotri are the Char Dhams for 
Hindu pilgrimage. Their faith in “Divine Charm” is unquestiona- 
bly. proved by annual pilgrimage to Badrinath, Kedarnath, Gangotri 
and Yamunotri. Followers of Buddhism also are full of reverence 
for this part of the country and have selected this for certain reli- “ 
gious rites. Sikhs have their Hemkund gurdwara near Lokpal man- 
dir. It is said that Guru Govind Singh meditated here. The other 
sacred and important places of Hindu pilgrimage are other Badris 
and Kedars. But construction of roads to Char Dhams isolated these 
places from national crowd. Most of them now retain their local 
importance only. But with the advent of mountain tourism these 
places are getting priority. Every rock, mountain, river, plant and 
animal symbolises one or the other God or Goddess. Few snow- 
covered peaks are said to be the permanent abode of Gods, as 
Banderpunch of Hanuman, Shivling of Shiva, Trisul as trident of 
Shiva and Nanda Devi of Parvati. 


2.3.1 Pilgrimage 

Earlier in history a pilgrimage meant toil and pain, therefore, 
common saying was, “It is the test of tolerance not of expression". 
But today travelling for religious reason and pleasant holidaying 
` goes together. Himalaya has age-old history of culture, civilization, 
religion and pilgrimage. Old and prominent among them are Kols, 
Dravidians, Kirats, Kinnars, Nags and Khasas. These tribes came 
here and intermixed with the local people and became part of 
Hindu culture and religion. We can safely deduce from the Purans 


CC-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


Elements of Adventure Tourism 79 


that the Aryans had little knowledge of Himalaya but later Vedic 
literature shows that Ganga and Yamuna and their sources were 
treated as most sacred. Since then, the area became the integral part 
of Hindu religion and pilgrimage. During Brahmanical period 
source of Ganga and Yamuna gathered, more religious importance. 
Around 700 B.C. (the period of Taittiriya Aranyaka) a new school 
of Hindu philosophy named, ‘path of knowledge’ came into exis- 
tence. For meditation they looked for remote, quiet, pure and pleas- 
ant natural surroundings and landed up in Garhwal. They found the 
river banks and mountain tops most suitable for meditation and 
self-realisation. Most probably this was the period when the banks 
of Ganga, Yamuna, Mandakini, Nandakini, Bhagirathi and 
Alaknanda were selected for meditation and prayers. So the sages 
were the first pilgrims to the region, who later on composed highly 
philosophical and religious scriptures. Ved Vyas composed and 
compiled the Mahabharata and Purans on the banks of river Sar- 
asvati near Mana village. Gargacharya, the great astronomer 
trained his pupil in the science of astronomy here. The Grammarian 
Varuchi visited Garhwal Himalaya and by propitiating Mahadeva 
obtained material for Paniniya-grammer. Even in medieval period 
(1000 A.D. to 1815 A.D.) a fairly large number of people took 
pilgrimage to Badri-Kedar. Garhwal contains no level land to suit 
agricultural pursuits. Nature fashioned the land in a way that offers 
attraction to men inclined to lead a spiritual life and live in a Spiri- 
tually charged atmosphere. 

Since ages the inner urge to travel to Garhwal remained differ- 
ent to different people. Some got attracted by peace and tranquility 
of the region, some are here for meditation and, by and large, a 
number of pilgrims come to pay homage to their ancestors. Later 
Brahminical rituals got revived and became commercialised as 
Priesthood. This gave way to the establishment of Tirth Yatra of 
Panda system. This Panda system exists even today. Yatra to Hima- 
laya is treated as most sacred in Hindu religion: In Skand Puran, it 
Is said that he who thinks of Himachal is greater than he who 
performs all worship in Kashi. Himalaya, Ganga and the religious 
atmosphere of the region have not only influenced the Hindus but 
the impression has gone deep down into the European mind too. It 
IS Said that here the Gods lived and saints and sages meditated.

Therefore Shiva said to Parvati іп Kedar-Khand of Skand Puran, 
“There is no place on this earth which can compare to this holy 
Тапа”. It is said that Lord Krishna himself meditated on Gandh- 
madan Parbat in Badrinath. 

Pilgrimage to Garhwal is still there. For the pilgrimage of 
Garhwal, apart from four major attractions (Char Dhams) there are 
Sapt Badri, Panch Kedar and confluences of holy rivers. All these 
are depicted in Fig. 10. 


a) Yamunotri 
Yamunotri is situated at an altitude of 3292 m above sea level. 
It is 226 kms from Rishikesh on the left bank of river Yamuna. 
River Yamuna emerges from Kalindi Parbat in the Banderpunch 
range, hence known as Kalindi too. At Yamunotri there is a temple 
of river Yamuna, the daughter of Surya, and is always associated 
with Lord Krishna. The temple is surrounded by trees. Its location 
on an avalanch-prone area is the reason of its reconstruction after 
every few years. But it is co-related with one of the saying locally 
' popular, which says that due to the fickle mindedness of the river 
` the temple is reconstructed after every few years. Close to the 
temple there are hot water springs and the water gushes out of the 
rock cavities at boiling point. ў 
The trek to Yamunotri is most fascinating. Wayside views of 
Kala Nag and Banderpunch are exotic. The first European to visit 
this place was Mr. Fraser (1815-18 18), but Capt. Skinner went upto 
the source of Yamuna. He was deeply impressed by the flora, 
_ fauna, civilization and religion of the region. The pandas of Yamu- 
notri come from the neighbouring village Kharsali (Yamunotri is 6 


kms from here) and the entire administration of the temple is in 
their hands. 


b) Gangotri 

An expanding settlement of Gangotri is situated at an average 
altitude of 3140 m above the sea level amidst giant pine, oak and 
deodar trees, at the confluence of Bhagirathi and Kedar Ganga. The 
20 feet high temple of Ganga was constrücted by a. Nepali general 
in the 19th century and the pandas of Mukhwa were appointed as 
Pujaris by him. Mythology says that sage Bhagirath meditated here 


СС-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


82 Tourism іп Garhwal Himalaya 


for bringing Ganga down on earth. Geological studies say that ages 
ago the entire stretch of land from Gangotri to Gaumukh was cov- 
ered with glacier and Ganga emerged here. But slowly the melting 
of glacier resulted in receding the snout back to Gaumukh, around 
19 kms north-east from Gangotri. 

Gangotri, once most suitable for meditation due to its sublime 
atmosphere, quietitude and natural surroundings, is becoming 
crowded day by day with many upcoming buildings and visitors. 
Age-old arduous pilgrimage is changing due to a motorable road 
and unplanned haphazard growth of the area. Therefore this area 
needs special attention. The mighty bear-peaked cliffs, snow-cov- 
ered mountains, roaring rivers and their confluences present most 
dreaded but clam scenery. Fraser narrated his experiences in these 
words, “. . . . it is not so simple, to many surely not very possible to 
convey an adequate idea of the stern and rugged majesty of some 
scenes, to paint their lovely desertness or to describe their unde- 
finable sensation of reverence and dread that steals over the mind 
while contemplating the death-like ghastly calm that is shed over 
them.'** Near the temple, there is a wide rock, known as Bhagirath 
Shila. Very short distance from here Bhagirathi falls into Surya- 
kund and later into Gauri-kund. The fall of Bhagirathi at these two 
points is worth seeing. 

The icon of Ganga is taken down to Mukhwa in winters and 
only few sadhus and watchmen remain there. The snow-covered 
peaks present unforgettable scenery. In the words of Marco Pallis, 
"Peaks rose in every side in wildest confusion; not in Sikkim have 
І seen any thing like the mountain scenery round Gangotri . . . . but 
it is useless to try to describe the grandeur of the scene; there are 
perfections about which the only eloquence is silence.””” Beauty of 
the area is praised by every visitor to the region. Skinner says, “I 
can never retain my enthusiasm, when I write of the grandeur of 
mountain scenery; but to retum to the beauties of the Gangas.'? 

But the unplanned expansion of pilgrimage and tourism is 
going to create catastrophe in near future. Therefore it is essential 
to systematize the crowd and buildings in and around Gangotri. 


c) Gaumukh Ж 
The snout of Gangotri glacier from where Bhagirathi emerges 


CC-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


Elements of Adventure Tourism 83 


is known as Gaumukh. This 25 kms long and 4 kms wide glacier is 
spread at an altitude of 4000 m above sea level. The glacier has its 
origin on the western slopes of Satopanth group of peaks. It is also 
connected with the glaciers along the northern slopes of Kedarnath 
peak. Therefore, it is said that three great places of pilgrimages 
Gangotri, Badrinath and Kedarnath are all within a few kms of 


horizontal distance from each other. 


It is said that long time back the snout of glacier was quite 
down below. Mr. Auden of the Geological Survey of India stated 
that the glacier must have receded by 2400 ft during the last cen- 
tury alone and in the earlier times it might have descended to at 
least Gangotri and may be even as far down as Jangla.?” Now it is 
19 kms north-east of Gangotri. This track to Gaumukh passes 
through a beautiful forest of blue pine (Chirbasa) and birch (Bho- 
jbasa) trees. This track was constructed in 1967-68, though before 
this also people visited Gaumukh. Many smaller bamaks join Gan- 
gotri glacier. In all, the glacier is punctuated with beautiful snow- 
covered peaks and presents most enchanting scenery. Six kms up- 
stream from Gaumukh are broad glacial terraces of Tapovan and 
Nandanvan. Lateral moraines are formed there above the glacier. 
Huge boulders, pebbles roll down casually. Gaumukh is approxi- 
mately 40 m high ice wall. The snout is changing shape frequently. 
From Gaumukh to Gangotri temple the Bhagirathi valley is wide 
and U-shaped with a surface area of 200 sq kms. Gangotri glacier 
is a complex glacier system, containing more than 20 cubic kms of 
ice. This ice may be more than a thousand years old.” This region 
can be a pilgrimage for tourists, adventurers and scientists. 


d) Sapt Badris 
Among seven Badris, Badrinath is the most famous shrine of 


. Lord Vishnu. Others are Bhavishbadri.17 kms from Joshimath 


towards Malari, Yogbadri at Pandukeshwar, Dhyan Badri at 
Urgam, Narsing Badri at Joshimath, Bridh Badri (about 7 kms short 
of Joshimath on the main Rishikesh-Badrinath motor route) at Ani- 
math and Adibadri (17 kms from Karanprayag on the Gwaldam 
route). There are remains of 16 small temples at Adi Badri. 

Before the advent of vehicular traffic these Badris were visited 
by the devout Hindus. Metalled road upto Badrinath diverted the

pilgrims towards Badri Vishal, leaving others isolated and by- 
passed. 


e) Badrinath 

It is located in the renowned Badrikashrama of Nar and Nar- 
ayan Rishis, at an altitude of 3122 m above sea level. From later 
Vedic records Badrinath is considered to hold supreme place in 
Hindu pilgrimage. It is learnt that as early as several centuries 
before the Christian era the shrine of Badrinath was celebrated as 
seat of leaming and as the abode of holy men. Fifty feet high 
temple of Badrinath is situated in the heart of the township with 
roaring Alaknanda below, milky Nilkanth behind and Mana valley 
on the north side. The idol of Lord Badrinath is made of black 
stone (Shaligram). The opening day is normally found during the 
last week of April or the first week of May. The Taptakund near 
the temple would have been the cause of establishing this sanctum 
sanctorum on this height. These thermal springs give thick smoke 
or steam of a strong sulphurous smell which cures skin diseases. In 
consonance with the tradition founded by Adi Shankaracharya, 
even today the priest of the temple comes from south India 
(Kerala). Brahma-kapal is situated at the right bank of Alaknanda 
to the north of Badrinath temple. In Hindu religion after death, the 
ceremonies are performed here for the peace of the departed souls 
(ancestors). 


f) Panch Kedar 

There are five famous shrines of Lord Shiva in Garhwal. These 
are Kedamath, Tungnath, Rudranath, Madmaheshwar and Kalpesh- 
war. In old tradition of pilgrimage, all the Kedars were visited by 
the pilgrims, but the introduction of motor transport in Garhwal 
popularised the pilgrimage to Kedarnath leaving the other four 
Kedars isolated. The latest trend in tourism (adventure tourism) is 
diverting the crowd towards these isolated natural and age-old pil- 
grimage centres. 


g) Kedarnath 


It is one of the twelve Jyotir Lings of Lord Shiva. Scriptures 
say that this is the place where Lord Shiva took refuge while flee- 


СС-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


\ 


Elements of Adventure Tourism 85 


ing from the Pandavas in the form of a bull. Hard pressed as he 

was, He dived into the ground leaving his hinder parts on the sur- 

face, for adoration afterwards. It is believed that Pandavas are the 
uted founders of the five temples of Lord Shiva. 

This holy temple is built оп a ridge, with grey stones down 
below the Kedarnath peak at an altitude of 3583 m. Just behind the 
temple approximately 1.5 kms away, is a small but stable snout of 
Chaurabare glacier from where river Mandakini emerges. The 
temple opens either in the last week of April or the first week of 
May, and the propitious day for the reopening of the temple for 
devotees is astrologically calculated on the day of Mahashivratri by 
the priests of the temple of Ukhimath. Symbolic images are brought 
down to Ukhimath in winters for worship. The valley of Kedarnath 
is most fascinating. The Alpine flowers of different hue and colours 
steal the heart of visitors. 

The other Kedars are the places where four other portions of 
fleeing Lord Shiva are worshipped in the following order. The arms 
at Tungnath, the face at Rudranath, the belly at Madmaheshwar and 
the hair and head at Kalpeshwar. 


h) Tungnath . 

Tungnath is situated at an altitude of 3680 m above sea level 
and 5 kms away from Chopta in picturesque surroundings of Al- 
pine meadows. This Swayambhu Lingam is the highest Hindu 
shrine in Garhwal. The Alpine flowers of Tungnath valley deserve 
highest appreciation from the mortals. Mokumath is the winter seat 
of Tungnath. 


i) Rudranath 

This natural rock temple of Lord Shiva is situated at an altitude 
of 3559 m above sea level amidst thick forest of rhododendron 
dwarfs and Alpine pastures. Here Shiva is worshipped as Nilkanth 
Mahadev. Glittering snow-covered peaks of Trisul, Nanda Devi 
and Devasthan present a picturesque view. In winters the symbolic 
Image of Lord Shiva comes down to Gopeshwar. Scattered idols of 
Gods апа Godesses are the result of the avalanches and their fury 
around in olden times. Every year a fair is held here on “Ѕгамап 
Pumima” (July/August) and is largely attended by the local people.

The extensive pasture all around is a place where one can 
forget ownself. Besides the main temple, there are many small 
temples. : 


j) Kalpeshwar 

Kalpeshwar temple is situated amidst the thick forests of 
Urgam valley. The entrance to the rock temple is through a rock 
cave. It is 24 kms from Joshimath (14 kms upto Helang is motor- 
able and the remaining 10 kms makes a fascinating trek). 


k) Madmaheshwar 
This Shiva temple is situated 29 kms north-east of Guptakashi 
at an altitude of 3497 m. The trek begins from Guptakashi and 
passes via Kalimath in most beautiful surroundings, leaving moun- 
tain peaks like Chaukhamba, Nilkanth and Kedarnath in fore- 
. ground. The entire trek is rich in natural wealth with monal pheas- 
ants and kasturi deer. 


1) Panch Prayags . 

Since olden days Panch Prayags of Garhwal have been of great 
religious importance and were very frequently visited by the pil- 
grims. But introduction of motor transport made these prayags iso- 
lated. Though hundreds of rivers and streams meet Alaknanda but 
the following five confluences are of great religious importance for 
Hindus, though today they are of local importance only. Most of 
the Garhwalis assemble there for bathing on Makar Sankaranti, 
Uttarayani, Basant Panchmi and Ram Naumi.

2.4.1 Important Towns | 
Арап from these places, some townships are also of great tour- 

istic importance. Some of the major towns are described below. 


` а) Uttarkashi 


Situated on the right bank of Bhagirathi and surrounded by 
Varuna and Asi rivers at an altitude of 1558 m, this small township 
formed a part of Tehri district till 1962. Later separate district was 
formed and Uttarkashi became the headquarters of Uttarkashi 
district. It abounds in natural beauty and temples. Among them 


.the temple of Lord Vishwanath is the most famous and important. 


The other important temples are of Parashuram, Kali, Annapurna, 
Ekadash-Rudra and Kuteti Devi. It is believed that in the second 
millennium of Kaliyug, Kashi will get submerged into the waters 
and Uttarkashi will replace it. In Skand Puran it is named as Varun- 
avrata. 

Vishwanath temple has got a magnificent Trisul, 26 ft high 


` with a base of 8/9” and a top of 1’ in circumference. The ancient 


name of Uttarkashi is Barahat. On the occasion of Makasankaranti 
a week-long fair is held here. In olden days it was a religio-cultural 
fair, but today it is just a trade fair. 

The Nehru Institute of Mountaineering (NIM) is also one of the 
attractions of Uttarkashi. In fact it is the school where mountain 
lovers get training and confidence for adventure sports. Every year 
a large number of young men and women take training of moun- 
taineering here. This institute was started on 14th November 1965, 
after the great mountain lover Jawaharlal Nehru. 


b) Bhairoghati 

Bhairoghati is situated at the confluences of Jadh Ganga 
(Jahnvi) and Bhagirathi at an altitude of 2579 m above sea level. 
Here the tumultuous Bhagirathi flows deep down into the narrow 
gorge and creates the disastrous music of Bhairo. Before June 


‚ 1985, access to Gangotri was a tough job as опе had to walk from 


i aues 


Lanka to Bhairoghati, 3 kms amidst a thick deodar forest and roar- 
ing Bhagirathi. However, the thrill is gone in the wake of motorable 
access to Gangotri. Mr. Hodgson found it most aweful looking 
place “. . . . never seen any thing in the hills to be compared with

с) Gupta Kashi 

178 kms from Rishikesh on the Kedamath route at an altitude 
of 1479 m above sea level is a scenic and legendry town of Gup- 
takashi. Here the landscape is rewarding and incredible. According 
to the legends, after killing Kauravas in the battle of Mahabharata 
at Kurukshetra, Pandavas wanted emancipation from the curse 
of killing kinsmen and went to Kashi to propitiate Lord Shiva. 
Shiva unwilling for darshan went to Himalaya, but Pandavas fol- 
lowed him in cognito. Therefore the place is known as Guptakashi. 
There are two ancient temples one of Chandersekhar Mahadev 
dedicated to Vishwanath and the other is Ardh-Narishwar. In front 
of Chandersekhar Маһайеу is a small pond known as Manikarnika- 
kund. 


d) Kalimath 

The famous Sidhpith of north, Kalimath is situated on the bank 
of Kali river. It can be approached from Ukhimath as well as from 
Guptakashi. There are a number of temples dedicated to Shiva and 
Shakti. These temple attract large crowds every year on Maha Ash- 
tami fair (September/October). 


e) Gopeshwar 

A small township of pleasing climate is situated at an altitude 
of 1308 m. It is the headquarter of Chamoli district and most of the 
district offices are located here. The main attraction is the massive 
temple of Shiva. It has got a huge trident standing in the courtyard 
bearing some inscription of 6th or 7th century. Several broken idols 
Scattered around the temple explain the theory of cluster of temples 
in ancient times. The residents of Gopeshwar and the neighbouring 
villages are ecologically very aware. They are the initiators of 
Chipko movement.

2.4.2 Tribal Villages 
Uttarkashi and Chamoli districts are situated on the Indo- 
Tibetan international boundary. Extreme northern villages of both 
the districts are basically tribal. Their way of life itself attracts the 
attention of the tourists. Few villages are getting exposure or are 
getting a feel of the modern city life as reflected in the conduct of 
the tourists. Some of the major tribal villages are described below. 


a) Netwar, Osla and Taluka 

The western part of Uttarkashi is known as Fateh Parbat and is 
separated from Himachal Pradesh by the Tons river. Here in the 
backdrop of snowy mountain peaks and dense forest human life is 
very much varied. In Tons valley, the villages—Netwar, Taluka and 
Osla are maintaining their identity as the descendents of the Kaura- 
vas. Even today, they worship Duryodhana. Netwar is 69 kms from 
Navgaon by motorable road. From Netwar jeepable road ap- 
proaches Sankari, which is 12 kms away. From here a trek of 11 
kms takes one to Taluka and another 11 kms to Osla. The women 
of the village wear costumes which resemble with the costumes of 
the Himachali women. 

The temple of Osla is intricately carved wooden structure, 
where Kauravas are honoured. The trek to Har-ki-Doon and Ruin- 
sara tal passes through these villages, but the life of these villages is 
least affected by either material prosperity or the culture of tourists. 
Natural beauty, culture, costume, tradition and typical way of life 
of the region are the attractions for the tourists. Polyandry, the most 
controversial characteristic of the region, has disappeared fully to- 
day. 


b) Kharsali 

Kharsali, a small village, is 6 kms before Yamunotri on the 
Hanumanchatti-Yamunotri route. It is the native village of Yamu- 
notri Pandas. This thickly populated village has a unique Shani 
Devta temple. Idols are kept on the fourth storey of the temple. 
Access to this is through a staircase made out of canoe-shaped logs. 
During winters a Bhog murti of Yamuna is kept in this temple. Old 
Shani temple reveals Buddhist influence, as manifested by its leo- 
Pard-perched top and stupa-like formation on the left. The view of 


СС-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai апа eGangotri 


90 Tourism іп Garhwal Himalaya 


Yamunotri peaks is superb from here. Though most of the men аге 
working in plains, this sleepy town even today is far away from the 
development activities of the world. 


с) Harsil 

This beautiful small town of Harsil is situated on the conflu- 
ence of Jalandari gad and Bhagirathi river. The scenery is unparal- 
leled with its great sentinels—Matri and Kailash mountains. On the 
right side of the town there is beautiful peak of Shrikantha behind 
which lies Kedarnath and to the rear is Banderpunch. On the other 
side is Yamunotri, all around snow-capped peaks. It might be the 
beauty of the place which attracted a Britisher, named George 
Wilson, who settled here over one hundred years ago and married a 
local girl. Even today his house, known as Wilson hut, constructed 
in 1864, tells the tale of Wilson's love for the area. He was the first 
person who introduced apples to this region and transported timber 
through rivers. Adjacent to Harsil is a small Buddhist village, 
Bagori. 


d) Nilang and Jadhang 

These were the villages adjacent to Tibetan border and the 
passes to Tibet were named after them. Nilang valley is known as 
Chongsa by the Huniyas (Tibetans). The two passes of Nilang are 
Thagala (to the west) and Tsang-Chokla (to the east). There was а 
third pass from Raithal, but it was seldom followed. After the 1962 
Indo-China war, all these passes were closed and trade abandoned. 
This ruined the nomadic traders and their trade. These villages are 
naked, barren and rocky and surrounded by mountain ranges of 
perpetual snow line, where except buggi grass nothing grows. Long 
before the outbreak of war, the nomads (Jads) frequented the area 
and lived in the villages. Now the villages are deserted and unin- 
habited. Due to its sensitive location no tourist is allowed to visit 
the area, but fortunate are those shepherds who are allowed with 


their herds of goats and sheep. Some of these highlanders are 
known as Nitwal. 


e) Mana 
On the left bank of Vishnu Ganga and 3 kms north of Badri- 


CC-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


Elements of Adventure Tourism 91 


nath, there is a village named Mana, at an altitude of 3200 m. This 
isolated border village of Chamoli district has got beautiful sur- 
roundings. The villagers are closely associated with the activities of 
the Badrinath temple and annual worship of Mata Murti. These 
people also lost their traditional trade with Tibet. Here the villagers 
are known as Marchhas. From Mana village to Mana pass the Sar- 
asvati valley is most rugged with glacial moraine, debris, boulders 
and cold climate. Mana pass (5651 m) was known as Chirbitla or 
Dangrila in Tibetan language. Closure of trade with Tibet forced 
the highlanders to migrate down to the plains for livelihood. There 
is a rock cave near Mana, known as Vyas Gupha where Ved Vyas 
is said to have composed the Mahabharata. 

Dhauli Ganga valley too has Bhotia villages. Tapovan is the 
first pasture land of Dhauli valley and last village of Khadwals. The 
valley becomes narrow and the altitude rises rapidly. In utter isola- 
tion and solitude the Dhauli flows with great noise. Largest number 
of Bhotias live in this valley. Kosa is the last village of Tolchas. 
Twelve kms ahead of the village is Kosa Bamak. Further on the left 
bank of Dhauli, Malari is the first village of Marchhas. 


f) Malari, Bampa and Gamsali 

Here the narrow V-shaped valley changes into U-shaped wide 
valleys. The valleys are covered with the forest of Kharsu (Quercus 
semicarpifolia), rose (wild), yew, horse chestnut, alder, poplar and 
ilm. Narrow gorges and swift flowing Dhauli present most wild but 
frightening scenery. Beautiful Marchha hamlets in such surround- 
ings multiply the beauty many a times. Villages of Malari, Bampa 
and Gamsali are summer houses of nomads. Deodar, birch and fir 
trees are scattered all around these villages. Bhotias drink a special 
tea prepared from fir (Raga) tree bark. 


g) Niti 

Niti is the last village of Indo-Tibetan border of Dhauli valley 
at an altitude of 5038 m, on the left bank of Dhauli river. The crop 
is buck wheat and barley, but villagers pay little attention to agri- 
culture. Before 1962 this was the safest and easiest way to enter 
Tibet (via Niti pass). Moorcroft, Webb and Batten visited Niti pass. 
Beyond Niti no settlement exists. Ninety nine percent of the Dhauli 


СС-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


92 Tourism in Garhwal Himalaya 


valley is barren and the river flows deep down into the gorges. 
Hence the water cannot be utilised by the villagers for any thing. 
Villages are very much scattered and lie deep down into the narrow 
valleys. Due to altitude, extreme climate and abandonment of 
Tibetan trade, these Bhotias and Jads started cultivating jhangora, 
millet, amaranthus and kauni. 


2.4.3 People 

The highlanders of Garhwal are known as ‘Bhotia’. Though 
the term Bhotia is used for an ethnic group of Sikkim, Darjeeling 
and Bhutan, but the Bhotias of Garhwal have absolutely no connec- 
tion with the eastern Himalayan Bhotias. Garhwal Bhotias are 
Hindus, a mixture with Tibetan culture due to long.trade contact 
with them. 

The Bhotias of Niti and Mana are called Marchhas and Jads. 
Ganga valley Bhotias are known as Jads. Bhotias have stout, well 
built figures. They are fair complexioned but much tanned hair and 
beards curled, small chinky eyes, high cheek bone of the Mango- 
lian touch. They love drinking and are born musicians.?? They are 
very tough, honest and born mountaineers. Whosoever visited the 
Himalaya admired them for their simplicity, friendliness, honesty 
and hardy qualities. Smythe appreciated their mountaineering 
ability and adaptability for altitude, "Bhotias of Garhwal think 
nothing of spending a night out in bad weather at 15000 ft or more 
under conditions which would make a European quail.”! 

Their women folk is fair in complexion and beautiful. They 
love wearing chunky silver and ‘gold jewellery. Captain Skinner 
was very much impressed with their beauty that he couldn't resist 
himself from saying, “. . . . for had I been a Turk, I might have 
made at least two additions to my harem.”?? Women spin the wool 
and make woollen clothes (pathu, baku, thulma, pankhis, chutkas, 
dan and asanas). They make baskets and mats from cane and 
wooden vessels from single block of wood. 


2.4.4 Fairs and Festivals ? 

The life of highlanders of Garhwal is full Of fairs and festivals. 
They celebrate each and every event of their life very enthusiasti- 
cally. Most of the fairs and festivals have got religious, cultural, 


CC-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


Elements of Adventure Tourism 93 


economic and environmental importance connected with them. 
These fairs and festivals are celebrated even in the villages and 
dizzy heights of Hemkund-Lokpal, Roopkund, Kedartal, Vasukital 
and Deoriatal. Most of the fairs fall in the months of Chait (March), 
Bhadon (August/September), Kartic (October/November) and 
Magh (January). gh 
TN 

a) New Year's Celebrations 

In Garhwal the new year's day falls on the first of Chait 
(March) hence the celebrations start with this day and get more 
festive colours due to the coincidence of spring during the same 
month. Chait Nauratri also falls in this month and Nau Durgas are 
worshipped. 

Nandasthami (in August-September) is celebrated as a fair at 
many places. Asoj Nauratri (September/October) (9 days) are de- 
voted to the worship of Shakti. This also is celebrated at many 
places, but the celebrations of Kalimath are famous. 

Magh Mela (January) is celebrated with great festivity. In the 
beginning it was a socio-cultural fair of Jads and Tibetans, but after 
1962 this became more as a trade fair than socio-cultural festival. 
Bathing in the Ganga at Uttarkashi or at any of the five Prayags on 
Makar or Uttarayani Sankrant is considered very auspicious. The 
fair lasts for seven days. The women are colourfully dressed and 
bedecked with chunky ornaments. 

On the Basant Panchami (Magh) day people wear yellow 
colour dresses in honour of spring and celebrate the day with sing- 
ing and dancing. 


b) Gauchar Mela 

Gauchar Mela had also been started as a religio-cultural fair, 
but today it is a trade fair. This starts from 14th November and lasts 
for a week. Women-folk wear colourful dresses and jewellery. 
Woollen products (carpets, blankets, shawls, chugta and pankhi), 
ringal (cane) and wooden artifacts are worth buying at the fair. 
Malari fair of Niti valley (15th August) was a worth seeing fair 
in olden days. Today Gauchar and Malari fairs are organised and 
Controlled by the government agencies. These are more associated 
with commerce, trade and industry of the region. Dancing is a 


СС-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


94 Tourism in Garhwal Himalaya 


common thing in the fairs. Most common dances are Pandav, Man- 
nan, Thariya, Chanchari, Bagdwal and Mukhara etc. Lata village in 
Chamoli district witnesses the dancing spree from the next day of 
Besakhi which falls in the month of April. 


c) Nanda Raj-jat Yatra 

On the auspicious day of Nandashtami (August/September) the 
villagers of Nauti, Simli and Gair gather at Nanda temple to give 
ceremonious send-off to Goddess Nanda Devi. It is said that 
Nanda, one of the forms of Shakti, was born in Garhwal and got 
married to Shiva. Later she was sent by her brethren for Shiva’s 
abode. This is repeated іп the Nanda Raj-jat Yatra. =. >gay 

There are two Raj-jat yatras; one is known as small Raj-jat, 
which is performed after every second year and which ends at 
Vednikund. The other is known as Bari Raj-jat, which is performed 
after every twelfth year and is joined by thousands of Garhwalis. 
On this occasion the Goddess accompanied by her attendant Lord 
Latu is carried into the snow, beyond Homkundi in Chamoli dis- 
trict. According to local people’s belief, every twelfth year a four 
homed ram is born in Pargana Chandpur, who leads the procession 
until it is lost in the snows of Nanda Ghunti. Ladies are not allowed 
to join the procession. 


2.5 OTHER INTERESTING SPOTS 


a) Maneri Bhali Project 

Maneri Bhali project is one of the hydro-electric projects on 
Bhagirathi river, conceived in two phases. First phase was commis- 
sioned in 1984 while work on second phase is in progress. 

In the first phase a 39 m high and 127 m long concrete gravity 
dam near Мапегі, on the river Bhagirathi is constructed. From here 
the water is diverted along an 8.6 kms long tunnel towards Uttar- 
kashi and 90 MW of electricity is produced. 

The first phase gravity dam and nearby area can be developed 
as water ski resort. 

The second phase is considerably complex and ambitious. 
There will be a lake (water reservoir) roughly the size of Nainital 
lake which can be developed as water resort area. Later a tunnel of

| 16 kms will take the water to Dharasu where a power station ca-

b) Wilson Cottage al Harsil 

Wilson, the British adventurer, constructed an impressive cot- 
tage in 1864 near the present-day military encampment in Harsil. 
He introduced apple growing in the region. He is credited with the 
construction of old forest rest houses at Harsil, Bhatwari and Dhar- 
asu. He also built a 350 ft long suspension bridge over the Jad 
Ganga river. He was very adventurous man. His cottage tells the 
tale of fine Garhwal architecture. Efforts are on to convert the 
Wilson hut into a local museum. Even today one can find the pho- 
tographs of Wilson and his wife, Gulabi there. 


c) Purola Excavations 

| Purola, а small town ОЁ Uttarkashi district, has come up ге- 
cently in lime light due to its archaeological treasure—a massive 
brick structure of а flying Garuda bird, measuring 28 Х 18 пп, 
dating to a period between 2nd century BC to the 2nd century A.D. 
This township is 19 kms from Naugaon (on the way to Mussoorie- 

! Har-ki-Doon road). Арап from Сагида many more things have 
come out in the archaeological excavation of Purola conducted by 
Garhwal University. 


d) Tilari Shahid Smarak 

This historical memorial is situated in Tilari, 2.5 kms from 
Barkot. Even today Uttarkashi people remember the martyrs of 
"Tilari Капа`. This event, which took place on 30th May, 1930 was 
instrumental in igniting the fires of revolt against the Raja's mis- 
‘ tule. It eventually led to the merger of the state with the Indian 
Union in 1949, 

This Tilari Kand had its roots in the forest settlement of 1927- 
28, which re-drew forest boundaries, encroached on the old and 
established cattle pastures and caused great resentment among the 
people. Agitated local people set fire to a part of the forest on May 
20, 1930. The district forest officer arrested the leaders, but agi- 
tated local people freed them while proceeding towards Tehri.

Then the army surrounded the protesters and opened fire killing

2.6 CONCLUSION 


A chain of mountains, jumble of snow-covered peaks, glaciers, 
bubbling rivers, tranquil tarns, dense forests, countless vertical and 
horizontal trails that bring the tourist in contact with glades and 
gleens, forests and meadows, hills and waterfalls, flora and fauna, 
culture and customs, folk and folklore, all this manifest in Garhwal. 
No hill side, no village, no valley, no mountain, no river that's 
without some sacred memory or which is not associated with some 
God or Goddess, sage, saint, hero or heroine of mythology. A rare 
combination of pilgrim’s paradise and tourists treat, such is 
Garhwal, the elevated world far beyond the imagination of man. 11 
is upto the visitor to discover the arena bristling with possibilities 
more exciting and more exhilarating. 

Natural beauty is scattered in Uttarkashi and Chamoli districts 
of Garhwal. Partially the resource potentials are identified and 
publicised but still many areas like Kush-Kalyani, Dingad valley, 
hot water spring near Dodital, Ruinsaratal and the small lake and 
campsite area near Dharali village in Uttarkashi district and Urgam 
valley, Manpai, Aali, Bistola and Kuari pastures need special atten- 
tion. Some of the restricted areas like Jad Ganga valley, Upper 
Mana valley, Kalindi Khal and Nanda Devi can prove to be suit- 
able places for adventure tourism. 

Rivers and lakes of the region are full of water sports potential. 
Though few private enthusiasts are exploring the possibilities by 
introducing rafting and canoeing in Ganga and Tons rivers. U.P. 
Government has constructed rest houses, maintained camp area and 
angling facilities, but Deoriatal, Bhaikaltal, Vasukital in Chamoli 
district and Ruinsaratal and Nachiketa tal in Uttarkashi district are 
desperately in need of such efforts. Wildlife has been publicised as 
a resource to tourism but no serious and action-oriented Steps have 
been taken so far for wildlife tourism. 

There is no dearth of religious attractions but lack of infrastruc- 
ture is hampering the crowd towards this region. Socially and cul- 
turally the region is very rich but certain socio-cultural attractions 


CC-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


Elements of Adventure Tourism 97 


of the region (fairs, festivals, customs and tradition etc) are dying. 
such things should be revived to attract the tourists. 


To make tourism round the year affair winter avenues have to 


be explored.
