Existing Infrastructure 


3.1 INTRODUCTION 


A combination of facilities, in addition to the natural beauty 
and unique exotic spectacles are of great help in increasing the 
attraction of an area. The availability and quality of facilities help 
in promoting tourist influx. Hence development of tourist facilities 
like transport system, accommodation, food and lodging, sports, en- 
tertainment activities, travel agency, tour operator, art and craft, 
base camp facilities, adventure tourism equipment and the souvenir 
industry provide a sound base known as infrastructure for tourist 
industry. In fact, it is the prerequisite for the development of tour- 
ism. It is an industry where apart from commodities and services, 
the people are greatly in movement. Therefore, the mode of move- 
ment and place of movement are very important. If, for instance, 
a destination is very rich in tourism attraction but lacks infrastruc- - 
tural facilities, tourists will not feel impelled to visit the place. 
| “Attractions and amenities are the very basis of tourism, unless 
these are present the tourist will not be motivated to go to a particu- 
lar place"! 

Pilgrimage, general tourism and adventure tourism, all the 
three are going together in Garhwal region. Due to the political 
turmoil of Kashmir and Darjeeling the adventure tourists are being 
i attracted towards Uttarkashi and Chamoli districts. Hence infras- 
tructural facilities are required. Most of the existing infrastructure 
had been constructed for the sake of pilgrimage and general tour- 
| ism. Adventure tourism has been introduced recently, but except

for a few things (GMVN’s Mountaineering Division, few travel 
agencies, few forest bungalows and GMVN’s tourist bungalows) 
nothing special has come up in the name of adventure tourism in- 
frastructure. No doubt that the existing infrastructure of the region 
has been utilised by the adventure tourists too. Presently general in- 
frastructure in the region is existing upto the road-heads, whereas

road-head to the mountain base, wildemess areas, river banks and

3.2 TRAVEL, TRANSPORT AND ROADS 


Prior to the inception of motor roads Garhwal Himalaya was 
traversed horizontally and vertically by the indigenous people. 
Bhotia and Jads used to do trade with Tibet by the yaks and ponies. 
This travel was out of necessity and not for the sake of fun or 
frolic. All the ancient pilgrimage and travels to the region were 
performed’ on foot due to the religiousness and lack of transport 
facilities. Great spiritual solace was experienced in braving the 
hardships and accomplishing the journey. The meaning of pilgrim- 
age was to renounce the material world and sought nature’s balmy 
effect on the troubled souls. Present day pilgrimage is absolutely

changed. Today even pilgrims ask for comfortable transport, ac- 
commodation, food and medical facilities. 

Though transport can be classified into three—surface, air and 
water. But Uttarkashi and Chamoli districts are blessed with sur- 
face transport only. Therefore, construction and maintenance of 
roads in the region are most important for the regional develop- 
ment. Even state government felt, “In a nut shell, the development 
of hills entirely depends on road communication.”” The surface 
transport is being discussed in detail. 


Road Transport 

Till 1825 there were no facilities of transport and communica- 
tion in Garhwal. Though construction of cart roads started in 1800 
and only the pilgrims used to traverse this region on foot. “The 
well-to-do employed coolies to carry their luggage and them- 
selves.” Complete pilgrimage to Badri-Kedar, Gangotri- Y amu- 
notri was arduous and time consuming. Those days worst kind of 
bridle paths were in existence, which were termed as by the cour- 
tesy road.* The man who took first initiative to construct the roads 
was G.W. Trail, the Commissioner (1827-1835) of Kumaon and 
Garhwal. Prior to him mule and foot tracks were popular here and 
travel was a tardy and risky job. “A 500 kms length of pilgrim road 
between Haridwar-Badrinath, Rudraprayag-Kedarnath, Ukhimath- 
Chamoli and Karanprayag-Lobha was constructed at his ini- 
tiative.'^ Road to Rishikesh-Kirtinagar was completed in 1931 and 
Haridwar to Karanprayag in 1947, but the network of roads and 
link roads was formed after independence and particularly after the 
1962. Chinese war. The Chinese assault stirred us up from slumber 
and reminded us of our long negligence of roads in Garhwal re- 
gion. We resorted to quick speedy mechanized construction of 
roads. The mechanized roads brought landslides, soil erosion and 
floods. This ultimately forced the planners and policy makers to 
think in terms of conserving the fragile eco-system of the region. 
Though construction of roads made this region accessible, it de- 
stroyed the traditional form of pilgrimage to which were allied pad- 
yatra, sadavarta, chatti and employment for the local people. Even 
today the condition of roads is not good, most of the roads are fair 
weather roads, which get obstructed during the rainy season. The 


СС-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai апа eGangotri 


Existing Infrastructure 103 


State of Uttar Pradesh is backward in respect of roads as compared 
to other states. The position is particularly bad in the hill region, 
where the roads are of great importance not only to open the re- 
mote areas for tourists but also for the improvement of economic 
condition of the local people. “For want of rail-communication, 
without having a network of roads in the hill areas, economic, in- 
dustrial and social development is not possible there . . . Flow of 
tourists has also been retarded due to lack of all-weather roads." 
Better road transport, the only system of communication, is an 
indispensable infrastructural necessity for opening up new areas of 
development in Garhwal. 

At present comparatively good roads are in existence and more 
link roads are under construction, but even today there is lack of 
free flow of tourist traffic on these roads. Table 3.1 shows the 
length of roads in the study area. District Uttarkashi has 826 kms 
metalled road and 694 kms unmetalled road. In Chamoli district the 
length of metalled road is 1078 kms and unmetalled 911 kms. Table 
3.2 shows the length of metalled road/100 sq kms area and/lakh of 
population. These roads were under UPPWD (Uttar Pradesh Public

Works Department) till 31 March, 1979 though funds were given

two roads Rishikesh-Uttarkashi-Harsil road and Rishikesh-Badri- 
пай road. Rishikesh-Uttarkashi-Harsil road is 222.40 kms long and 
is a State Highway No. 53. The second part of the road is Harsil- 
Bhairoghati-Nelong road, with a length of 38.12 kms. In between 
Lanka and Bhairoghati there is a 90 m long bridge of steel girders. 
This was constructed in 1985 by UPPWD. Before 1985 the journey 
of Gangotri, specially from Lanka to Bhairogathi was very arduous, 
but now the bridge gives comfortable journey and attracts more 
tourists and pilgrims to the area. As the bridge ensures easy acces- 
sibility more and more people are visiting Gangotri every year. 
From Bhairoghati the road bifurcates—one for Nelong and the other 
for Gangotri. Bhairoghati-Gangotri road is under UPPWD, but 
Bhairoghati-Nelong road comes under DGBR. Beyond Gangotri 
the foot trek is maintained by UPPWD. 

The other road Rishikesh-Joshimath-Badrinath (297 kms) and 
Joshimath-Malari-Jumma (86 kms) comes -under DGBR. The re- 
maining link roads and main roads are under UPPWD.

The roads to Yamunotri and Kedarnath are under UPPWD. 
Though all the Char Dhams are connected with roads, which by 
and large, are fair-weather roads. Small showers of rain create soil 
erosion and landslides which hamper the traffic very frequently 
from hours to days. 

Ecologically, Himalaya is very fragile, therefore, construction 
of roads is a delicate issue. Though modem adventure tourist is 
lured by the bridle roads than the metalled roads, because the bridle 


CC-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri

roads have not been robbed of their natural surrounding, where 
man can walk with his own shadow. However, this can not be 
denied that roads are essential for the development of the region. 
During different Plan periods high priority has been given to the 
construction of roads, but it can be said that road development 
couldn’t reach to the desired point in Garhwal. 


Transport Agencies 

At present there are many transport companies working in 
Garhwal. The oldest transport company GMOU (Garhwal Motor 
Owners Union) came into existence with 16 buses of 18 seats each. 
Later, one more Garhwal Motor Transport (GMT) was founded in 
the then Tehri Garhwal. In 1976 Garhwal Mandal Vikas Nigam 
(GMVN) came into existence with a fleet of deluxe coaches given 
by U.P. Hill Development Corporation. But the oldest and the larg- 
est is GMOU with very fast service and in 1954-55 it had 172 
buses. During 1973-74 the total trade of the company was Rs. 1 
crore, which became Rs. 5.60 crores in 1981 and during 1987-88 it 
got gross ticket booking of Rs. 5,63,47,863. In 1989 there were 301 
buses in its fleet with 142 employees, excluding drivers and con- 
ductors (office records of GMOU, Kotdwara). 

Garhwal was ceremoniously opened for foreign tourism in 
1974 with a network of roads and buses. This was the formal be- 
ginning of a new era of tourism in Garhwal, though package tours 
to Badri-Kedar, Gangotri- Yamunotri were formally started іп 1972 
by Uttar Pradesh Hill Development Corporation. These package 
tours gave wide publicity to the tourist destinations of Garhwal, not 
only in India but abroad too. Ever since, the number of coaches has 
constantly been increasing. In the year 1981 its fleet strength went 
up to 22 and the Nigam conducted 220 package tours taking 5,733 
tourists to various destinations in Garhwal.” Package tours attracted 
a large number of tourists to the region. 

Apart from these, there are buses run by U.P. Roadways, Tehri 
Garhwal Motor Owners Union Ltd. (TGMOU) and Roop Kund 
Travels. During pilgrim and tourist season additional buses are put 
on the yatra route to provide frequent buses to the tourists and 
pilgrims. At present there are 90 deluxe coaches and 20 cabs in the 
fleet of GMVN.

3.3 ACCOMMODATION 


Accommodation is one of the most essential tourist facility. Its 
importance can be measured by the fact that it takes away the 
largest chip of the tourist's budget. Accommodation includes all the 
facilities used for the sojourn of a tourist, such as hotels, motels, 
travellers lodges, dharamshalas, ashrams, camps and inns. The 
provision of adequate availability of accommodation is one of the 
basic prerequisite of tourism development and the expansion of 
tourism automatically gears up the development of accommoda- 
tion. The United Nations' Conference on International Travel and 
Tourism held in Rome in 1963 acknowledged the essential impor- 
tance of means of accommodation, both traditional (hotels and mo- 
tels) and supplementary (camps, youth hostels, tourist villages etc) 
as an incentive to international tourism. According to the develop- 
ment of Tourism Act (Britain) 1969, accommodation means, 
"Hotel or other establishment at which sleeping accommodation is 
provided by way of trade and business." All the hotels, motels, 
youth hostels, camping sites, circuit houses, traveller's lodges, for- 
est lodges, and self-catering holiday villages and holiday camps are 
the different types of accommodation provided for the tourists. 

Tourism and accommodation are interdependent, without ac- 
commodation nature's beautiful attractions blush unseen without 
tourists and without proper accommodation the talk of tourism as 
an industry is an exercise in futility. "Accommodation is one of the 
more visible and tangible manifestations of tourism. Facilities may 
form a part of the attractions of a destination . . . . they may 
complement the attractions, a well appointed hotel may enhance the 
attractiveness of а resort"? In India the concept of shelter for 
travellers and pilgrims is not new, the historical records of vihars, 
dharamshalas, sarais, musafirkhanas and chattis prove the point. 

“A country may possess within its national confines the maxi- 
mum number of attraction of most diverse kind, but without good 
satisfactory hotels it is not possible to develop its tourist traffic.” 

In Garhwal there wasn't commercial hospitality system in age- 
old pilgrimage. It was only pilgrimage and the crowd was religious- 
minded. The objective of the arduous journey (pilgrimage) was to 
reach the temples for salvation and not for the sake of fun and 


CC-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


Existing Infrastructure 107 


frolic. But the modem circumstances and fast city life have im- 
posed the necessity of visiting country side for rest and recreation. 
Today’s domestic traveller is not only tourist but pilgrim too. He 
comes not only for Kedarnath, but for the trek of Vasukital and 
Chaurabarital too, similarly the pilgrim of Gangotri is a trekker of 
Gaumukh, Tapovan and Nandavan too. An Indian traveller to 
Garhwal is neither purely a tourist nor a pilgrim, but the symbiosis 
of the two exist in him. Though such tourists are not interested in 
luxurious accommodation, but their requirement is a clean and 
comfortable bed, good food and catering service etc. 

However, in Uttarkashi and Chamoli districts considerably 
good accommodation for tourists and pilgrims has come up in pub- 
lic and private sectors, though limited to some key points. A wide 
range of accommodation types to suit the requirements of different 
classes of tourists has come up. GMVN is doing fairly well in this 
direction whereas private sector entrepreneurs are also coming for- 
ward after initial hesitation. Other than GMVN, many other gov- 
emment departments have their rest-houses and dak bungalows at 
various tourist centres of Garhwal, foremost among them are forest 
rest houses and PWD bungalows. 

At points like Rishikesh, Srinagar, Uttarkashi, Joshimath, Bad- 
rinath, Gaurikund, Kedarnath, Gangotri, Hanuman Chatti and Janki 
Chatti quite a lot has been done for the accommodation. The reason 
being that a pilgrim or a tourist who comes here has to take his first 
night halt at Rishikesh before pushing off next day either for Uttar- 
kashi or Joshimath (Uttarkashi for Gangotri, Gaumukh, and 
Joshimath for Kedarnath, Badrinath, Valley of Flowers and Hem- 
kund-Lokpal), according to their destinations. This has resulted in 
the agglomeration of accommodation at these places. Table 3.3 
Shows the accommodation type available: in study area. GMVN 
constructed accommodation at most of the tourist places in 
Garhwal but the progress in this matter has not come upto the 
expectations. The capacity of GMVN accommodation at various 
places has been shown in Table 3.4. 

Apart from GMVN's accommodation there are hotels (run 
by private owners at various centres), dharamshalas, chattis, 
gurudwaras and paying-guest accommodation. Hotels have got 
proper beds but in dharamshalas, chattis and gurudwaras sleeping

enthusiasm in private entrepreneurs in the region. In Japan in the 
year 1961, only 27.7 percent of the capital was owned privately in 
hotels whereas 72.3 percent was paid by the Japanese govern- 
ment”. 

Seasonality, high risks of investment and higher proportion of 
fixed assets in hotels are few causes of the hesitant attitude of the

tourism is very significant. Besides destination and destinational

Existing way-side amenities are of poor standard. There are 3 
petrol pumps in Uttarkashi district and 5 in Chamoli district. These 
pumps cater to the requirements of regular automobiles as well as 
the seasonal flood of tourist/pilgrim automobiles. There should be 
more petrol pumps in this area. One petrol pump at Uttarkashi 
proper is not sufficient for the seasonal crowd, occasional unavaila- 
bility of petrol at Uttarkashi petrol pump creates chaos. The people 
are sometimes stranded there due to non-availability of petrol 
which disturbs the accommodation, food and other facilities (de- 
mand/supply equilibrium). Though most of the places have got 


СС-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 
-L 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai апа eGangotri 


Existing Infrastructure 115 


service centres but they are not generally located on the way. 
People have to look for such centres which spoils the enjoyment of 
journey and creates the problem of wastage of time, too. Therefore, 
either petrol pumps should have these service centres or they 
should be located somewhere on the way for the convenience -of 
the tourists. Not a single restaurant or eating house is there for 
| exclusive use of the tourists/pilgrims. All the restaurants are located 
| іп the heart of the towns every where іп Uttarkashi апа Chamoli 
districts. 

The entire region lacks toilet facilities on the road-side. Gunny 
| sack toilets, that dot the yatra route are a big blot on the tourism 
infrastructure of Garhwal. Sulabh Sauchalayas should be con- 
structed at least after every 50 kms from Rishikesh onwards. The 
conditions of gunny toilets are generally unhygienic. Such toilets 
are prone to epidemics and cause annoyance to the users. It is very 
necessary to improve toilet facilities of road-sides. 


3.5 ENTERTAINMENT AND RECREATION 


In Garhwal and specially in Uttarkashi and Chamoli districts 
| there is nothing to provide for recreation and entertainment. No one 
| can think of glittering night life in such serenity, but centres of 
| yoga, meditation, jungle safari (for watching varied flora and 
| fauna), rock-climbing, skiing, rafting, canoeing, hang-gliding, 
camping etc can be developed. Such centres can serve the needs of 
tourists Of varied interests. The inclination of tourist towards ad- 
venture activities necessitates the development of above activities 
for recreation and fun. Large capital investment is not required for 
developing these recreational facilities. 

Dwindling local fairs and festivals have to be revived for the 
entertainment of the tourists. Magh mela of Uttarkashi, trade fair of 
1 Gauchar, Bari Raj-Jat-Yatra of Roopkund and Choti Raj-Jat-Yatra 
of Vedni Kund have to be revived. 


3.6 TRAVEL AGENCIES AND TOURIST GUDES 


( Іп tourism travel agents, who offer travel packages for various 
attractions of the area and bring tourists to these places, play a vital 
role. Travel agents assist individuals or groups wishing to take

wips. Prepairing itineraries, arranging transport, accommodation

tourist and the destination. He does the publicity job also. This 
business of middleman began in July 1841 with the pioneering 
work of Thomas Cook in Britain. Travel agents are known as the 
wholesalers of travel and tourism trade.

These travel agencies are generally providing facilities for ad- 
venture tourists, who are coming here for trekking, mountain- 
climbing, rock-climbing, river rafting and canoeing etc. The facili- 
ties provided by these travel agencies are—porters, cooks, HAP 
(High Altitude Porter), guides, accommodation arrangement and 
transport etc. These agencies have equipment stores and they give 
these on hire to the tourists. Initially these agencies got work from 
Delhi-based travel agencies, but now with their better services and 
improved contacts they are getting direct booking from the tourists 
(Indian and foreigners). 

These travel agencies can enlarge the spectrum of their ser- 
vices to the customers and generate more opportunities of employ- 
ment and income. For the development of tourist industry, healthy 
travel agencies are essential. In fact with a sound network of travel 
agencies proper flow of tourists can be maintained. Just to encour- 
age tourism in private sector as a means of employment the govern- 
ment started a special programme of loaning up to Rs. 50,000 with 
20 percent subsidy. Further increase in the amount of loan is under 
consideration.'? қ | 

The tourist guide is one of the most important figures in accel- 
erating the tourist traffic. He is the one who comes in close contact 
of the tourists. He satisfies each and every query of the tourist

Effective publicity is the basic means of increasing tourist іп- 
flow. If this is badly planned or inadequately financed, it would not 
only hamper the development, but also reduce the inflow of 
tourists.'* Publicity, therefore, is an essential effort to encourage 
tourism in the region, and it can be done variously: 


1. Audio or visual means 
2. Literature 
3. Returning tourists. 


For publicity GMVN and government have done fairly well. 
View cards, calendars, books, brochers and maps are being brought 
out. Advertisements in newspapers and maganizes are being 
inserted. The electronic media which has a wider and effective ap- 
peal has also been pressed into service for publicity purpose. Being 
the most impressive component of publicity it has to be tapped 
, more vigorously. 


3.8 SHOPPING 


Shopping is one of the important activities of the tourist in and 
around the destination. Every tourist wants to take some thing with 
him as a souvenir. Apart from the money which a souvenir brings 
in exchange to the local people, it gives invaluable publicity to the 

| region. The woollen goods (pankhis, chutka, thulma and carpets) of 
Bhotia and Jads are worth buying from Garhwal. Other traditional 
wares аге bamboo items, wooden toys and replicas of old temples, 
but these things are languishing in dark comers due to lack of 
show-rooms on the Yatra route. This neglected aspect of tourism in 
E Garhwal region results in loss both to the tourist and the craftsmen 


of the region. The government show-rooms should display and sell 
these artefacts.

In both the districts the villages are having the problem of 
drinking water. Either the villages are without drinking water sup- 
ply or wherever it is provided, it is full of problems. The percentage 
of villages devoid of proper drinking water supply upto (1986 Sep) 
was 69.88 іп Chamoli and 92.07 in Uttarkashi districts." Though 
all the major towns are having drinking water facility, but there is 
scarcity of water during summers and the water (hard water) is not 
properly treated. Foreigners are particularly very panicky of drink- 
ing this water (100 foreigners were interviewed out of them 45 
were not drinking water here, 40 were drinking with purifying pills 
and 15 were taking as it was).

not electrified fully. Major towns are electrified but the supply is by 
and large erratic. The percentage of electrified villages in total

General hygiene and sanitation in public places in and around

Department, Uttarkashi to cope with the needs of thousands of pil-

ings and the banks of Bhagirathi stink very much every moming. 
All the Char Dhams which are claimed to have been developed as 
pilgrim and tourist centres are having this problem.

Presently radio communication, which is very important for 
mountain tourism specially at the time of casualty, search and res- 
cue, is non-existent in the region. 

Insurance companies also play important role in the develop- 
ment of tourism. Insurance company branches are there in the main 
towns of the region. Insurance of porters, high altitude porters and 
guide etc creates confidence among the local people, which is nec- 
essary in mountain tourism of the region. 


3.10 ADVENTURE TOURISM INFRASTRUCTURE 


For Himalayan tourism and specially for tekking and moun- 
taineering some special infrastructural facilities are required apart 
from the normal infrastructure. Mountaineering and trekking activi- 
ties start from the road-head, therefore infrastructural requirements 
also begin from the road-head. They are as following: 

Camp areas, camping kit, equipment and clothing, food, re- 
gional informations, maps of the area, radio communication and 
search and rescue service. 

The sorry state of the region is that apart from scanty equip- 
ment stores most of the things are non-existent in the region. Worth 
mentioning is the introduction of Vayudoot flight for aerial viewing 
of Garhwal. 

Spectrum of infrastructural facilities for pilgrimage and general 
tourism are coming up gradually but for mountain tourism suitable 
Steps and directions are desperately required. Talking about moun- 
tain tourism without proper infrastructure will be a myth. If un- 


organised mountain tourism (without infrastructure) is allowed to 


80, certainly it will create catastrophe. 

It can be concluded that fair-weather roads, shortage of buses 
and road-side amenities are the biggest problems of travel and 
transport. Urgently required air transport is utterly lacking. Accom- 
modation is scarce and not up to the standard. Publicity requires 
great care and effort. Radio communication is no where on the 
Scenario of adventure tourism infrastructure and to be more spe- 
cific there is nothing worth mentioning for the development of 
adventure tourism infrastructure in the region.

Economics of Mountaineering 
ya and Trekking 


4.1 INTRODUCTION 


Of all the development activities, mountaineering and trekking 
have the potential to usher іп an all-round development іп Garhwal 
region. As we know that tourism involves expenditure and те- 
sources use to achieve multiple ends, retums become important. 
Retums can be economic, social, cultural, political, psychological 
and ecological. To achieve them, ends and means count a lot. The 
retums, positive or negative, which are not related to the objectives 
can be deemed as unintended consequences. But even today, in 
| India, tourism has been largely conceived of іп economic terms. 

| Therefore, the economics of mountaineering and їтеККїпр is very 
important, when we talk about mountain tourism. 
Mountaineering and trekking reguires lots of money from the 
pockets of tourists, the government and private operators, Adven- 
ture tourism has to go to such areas which are difficult in approach. 
Tourist attractions in Garhwal are not only located in remote cor- 
ners but are difficult in approach. Simultaneously clothing, equip- 
ment and food for such activities are varied and costly. Hence 
acquiring such things is difficult and expensive for the domestic 
tourist if not for the foreign tourist. On the other hand tour opera- 
per tors also face great difficulties in arranging such things. Firstly 

because, they are expensive, secondly they are scarcely available in 
India. Economics is the game of demand and supply. Simplest thing 
to earn profit is to increase supply when demand is increasing, but 


CC-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 
EE. 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


124 Tourism іп Garhwal Himalaya 


in adventure tourism demand for mountains, treks and infrastruc- 
ture is great in Garhwal, whereas the supply of goods and services 
‘to cater to the tourist demand is meagre. Supply side i.e. transport, 
roads, accommodation and road-side amenities are either very 
weak or at its initial stage. However, they are coming up, though at 
a slower speed. 


4.2 Tourism: A Source оғ Economic DEVELOPMENT 


It is well known that Garhwal, due to its difficult topography, 
deficient mineral resources and fragile eco-system, is not suitable 
for any manufacturing industry. Hence its mountains, rivers, for- 
ests, glaciers, peaks, pastures and tarns can be the only source of 
economic development (through mountain tourism) of this under- 
developed region. Davis gave four reasons for the encouragement 
of tourist industry in developing countries. 


1. “In many developing countries, the demand prospects for 
international tourism are considerably brighter than they are 
for traditional exports. 

2. Tourism is relatively labour-intensive and compares fa- 
vourably with other economic activity as a generator of 
employment. 

3. Tourism can speed up the development process because the 
gestation period of most tourist investment projects is rela- 
tively short. 

4. Tourism can often generate economic growth in countries 
or regions that however much they offer to tourists, have 
little or nothing in the way of resources to offer to anyone 
else. The sector can thus help to instigate disequilibrium.” 


Most of these are existing in developing countries of the world. 
Therefore tourism development might be defined specially as the 
provision or enhancement of facilities and services to meet the 
need of the tourists. If tourism is to become successful and a self- 
perpetuating industry, it needs to be planned and managed as a 
renewable resource industry, based on local capacities and commu- 
nity decision-making. Right decision at right time is most essential. 
Right decision about the funds (government, big businessmen and 


СС-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai апа eGangotri 


Economics of Mountaineering & Trekking 125 


banks) is of foremost importance. Finally, tourism development 
must combine planning (existing potentials, goals, infrastructure, 
facilities and development strategy) with marketing and manage- 
ment (day to day, season-wise etc). All these things are of para- 
mount importance in such sensitive business. Planning is concemed 
with anticipating and regulating change in a system, to promote 
orderly development, so as to increase the social, economic and en- 
vironmental benefits of the development process. 

To-date most tourism plans have been oriented towards per- 
sonal profit and careless economic growth. Therefore, very soon 
tourist destinations started facing environmental problems. Primary 
concem of tourism business was economic gains. The existence 
of tourism potential, however, need not necessarily lead to a flour- 
ishing tourist industry. Therefore the objective of every potential 
holding area is not only to maintain it but to provide it to the tourist 
with care. Therefore ‘what’ (potentials), ‘how’ (planning) and to 
| ‘whom’ (tourist) are to be decided first before we embark оп 
launching tourism in the area as an industry and later on they are 
the factors on which rests the entire dynamics of tourist economy. 

The potential of this industry is spread every where on earth 
from easy plains, rivers, forests, sea-shores, sky to mountain tops.. 
There is also a branch of economy that avoids central places апа: 
the agglomeration of industry. This is tourism. “Tourism is drawn 
to the periphery of settlement districts as it searches for a position 
on the highest mountains in the most lonely woods, along the re- 
motest Беасһев.”? Tourism industry, hence deals with identifica- 
tion of potentials, planning for their betterment, developmental 
Strategy, marketing and management, without one the other re- 
mains useless. 

Without the intervention of the tourism industry, the tourists 
would necessarily tend to neglect the tourism assets, however valu- 
able these may be. In other words, the tourism industry is a vital 
link between the tourism assets and the tourism market. Since the 
tourism industry provides the vital tourism growth, but inefficient 
and inadequate tourism industry retards such growth, whereas a 
@% Strong and efficient tourism sector promotes and accelerates the 

| growth of the region. The need of leisure and pilgrim tourist differs 
| from those of business tourists. And even within each of- these 
| 


| CC-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai апа eGangotri 


126 Tourism in Garhwal Himalaya 


market segments, the requirements vary in accordance with the 
income pattern, as well as sex, age and other characteristics of the 
tourists therein. While the tourism industry is, thus an essential pre- 
requisite for the growth of tourism, its importance, however, is 
essentially dependent on a country or a region. Bermuda, situated 
hundreds of miles from a land mass, was once small agricultural 
producer; but with the growth of population, tourism was an obvi- 
ous choice to improve the island’s economy. Now it is the major 
industry of the country. 

Tourism and leisure industry in the world has an interesting 
history. Prior to the industrial revolution in Europe, the aristocracy 
had a virtual monopoly of leisure and tourism. Gradually as time 
went on some workers did manage to arrange short holidays largely 
limited to Christmas and Easter. Holidaying for masses throughout 
the Victorian era was almost entirely confined to day excursions. 
Till 1900 holiday was taken for granted as a luxury and until 1960 
working week was about 45 hours, but later it was reduced to 40 
hours a week. In USA, hundreds of firms now handle the week-end 
holidays. Reduction in working hours, increased disposable іп- 
come, fast transport system and monotonous character of city life 
not only accelerated tourism but diverted it towards remote country 
side and nature. Weekly leisure time gave rise to the demand for 
essentially local leisure facilities, but the annual leisure pattern in 
the developed countries is of a different significance when it comes 
to determining how and where the disposable free time is spent. 
“Four weeks paid holiday of 1980 has popularised holidaying 
abroad. Tourism, as we know it today, is a relatively new phenome- 
non, which generates new demands on land for use and enjoyment 
оп а scale not envisaged until recently." ? 

The struggle for the country side, remote areas close to nature, 
in fact are becoming every where popular and more complex. The 
tourist, the farmer,.the sportsman and the resident himself all claim 
title to its use. Each has his own activity for which he can make a 
good case. Each, if given the chance, would exclude one or all the 
others. Hence the system of taking decisions must match the new 
complexity of tourist industry and the pressure from others. Recon- 
ciling development and conservation thus depends upon a skillful 
sorting out of goals and values, both public and private.

Hence it is very clear that the tourist demand and local uses can 
seldom clash, but can be checked by wise management and plan- 
ning. It can reduce the conflict and ensure the full use and enjoy- 
ment of scarce resources in economic, social and aesthetic terms. 
To date, most tourism goals and planning have been oriented to- 
wards business interests and economic growth. The prime motive 
| for tourism development and planning has been commercial and 

economic gain. Тһе mere existence of tourism potential, however, 
need not necessarily lead to a flourishing tourist industry. It can be 
disastrous too. Therefore the planning, policy framing, administra- 
tion and careful implementation is most essential. 


| 
| 4.3 Key PHASES оғ TOURISM PLANNING 


Іп planning tourism to ап area, опе must not forget the aesthe- 

tics of the region which determine the rate of development of tour- 

| ism. Іп fact, it is ехігетеіу important that the natural beauty of 

| potentially popular destinations be preserved and protected by sen- 
sible planning. Key tourism planning phases are: 


. Assessment of tourist demand and supply 
. Establishing objectives 

. Territorial planning 

. Basic infrastructure 

. Financial planning 

Human resource planning 
Administrative structure 

. Marketing and promotion 

. Monitoring progress 

10. Time factor 


Prior to the establishment of tourism industry, if we look upon 
all above segments of planning, there will be no chance for haphaz- 
ard development of the region or imbalance in the multiple те- 
source use system. So the first thing is: what are the assets that can 
be developed into destination? what is the number of tourists, 
whether it is increasing or decreasing, what should be the objec- 

| Uve? and to achieve these objectives what should be the planning? 
How infrastructure should be developed, how finances can be 


( CC-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


128 Tourism іп Garhwal Himalaya 


allocated, and what will be the priority in investment, how local 
human resources can be utilised, what will be the administrative set 
up? How marketing will be looked after with an idea of promotion 
with specific time period? If this all is framed well in time and 
chalked out properly there is no question of failure or dark future of 
tourism. The planning system must also be flexible in time and in 
scale. There is no absolute or all-time best use of resources. The 
pattern of demand changes over the years, therefore, flexibility in 
tourism planning is must. This flexibility should be adopted in dif- 
ferent spheres of tourism planning. 

If tourist arrivals to a particular destination keep on increasing, 
the planners have to think about augmenting the facilities for in- 
creasing number of tourists, but this increase can be adjusted upto a 
certain extent i.e., upto the carrying capacity of the region. The 
planners have to think about the ways and means of diversifying 
the crowd to other destinations rather than concentration of tourists 
at a particular place. 

Beginning of tourism in Garhwal had been made earlier in 
1972 but GMVN ushered mountain tourism with the establishment 
of Mountaineering Division in 1976. U.P. Government made pri- 
mary investment with the construction and maintenance of accom- 
modation and other basic amenities. This encouraged the private 
entrepreneurs for secondary investments in the region as hoteliers, 
travel agents, transporters and guides etc. But the private tourism 

. operators have never revealed their income, expenditure, profit and 
loss details, in spite of a firm assurance given to them that their 
information will be kept confidential and will be used only for the 
purpose of study. Therefore, this study is based on the data col- 
lected from GMVN, U.P. Tourism and Indian Mountaineering 
Foundation, New Delhi. 


4.4 TOURIST INFLUX IN THE REGION 


The influx of tourists/pilgrims to Uttarkashi and Chamoli dis- 
tricts of Garhwal is increasing. Earlier the crowd was purely of 
pilgrims but today whosoever is visiting Garhwal, is having reli- 
gious sentiments as well as adventure pursuit. In fact inclination for 
adventure is increasing day by day. Tourist arrivals to a few desti- 
nations has been shown in Table 4.1.

The coefficient of variation for Badrinath, Kedarnath, Gan- 
gotri, Yamunotri and Valley of Flowers has been calculated as 
following: 19.28, 13.69, 29.26, 25.37 and 62.76 percent respec- 
tively. This shows that highest (62.76%) variation in tourist/pilgrim 
arrivals is in the Valley of Flowers, minimum is in Badrinath and 
Kedamath. 

In Valley of Flowers camping is prohibited. Badrinath and Ke- 
damath have got variation of less than 20 percent. Both the places 
have good and plenty of accommodation and other facilities in 
comparison to other tourist destinations (Gangotri and Y amunotri). 
Therefore the variation is minimum and tourist arrivals is maxi- 
mum. This can increase with additional facilities and publicity. 

Long term trend has been calculated. The data has been taken 
from Table 4.2 and trend line for Badrinath (Fig. 13), Kedarnath 
(Fig. 14), Gangotri (Fig. 15), Yamunotri (Fig. 16) and the Valley of 
Flowers (Fig. 17) has been drawn. In general increasing long term 
trend has been found for all the five tourist centres of Uttarkashi 
and Chamoli districts of Garhwal. 


4.5 INCOME AND EXPENDITURE OF MOUNTAINEERING DIVISION OF 
GMVN 


Table 4.8 shows the income and expenditure of Mountaineer- 
ing division of GMVN. Though the number of tours decreased 
from 1986-87 to 1987-88, but the net profit increased a lot. Net 
profit of 1986-87 (deducting rent, salary and other expenditure 
from total income) was only Rs. 7245.20, whereas in 1987-88 it 
increased many fold, and became Rs. 74,897.60. In 1988-89 num- 
ber of tours increased from 14 to 22, and net profit went up to Rs. 
1,63,291.10. Similarly total tours undertaken іп 1989-90 went up to 
34, and net profit went up to 2,53,604.30. That is quite a large 
increase in income. Number of mountain tourists handled by 
GMVN is also increasing every year. In 1988-89 the number of 
tourists was 740, and this shot upto 1582 in 1989-90. Figure 18 
shows the growth of mountain tourism in Uttarkashi and Chamoli 
districts. 

Number of tourists, tours, income and employment show a 
healthy trend of mountain tourism in Garhwal Himalaya. Though 
the number of tourists entertained by GMVN is very small part of

on hire for earning money and providing facilities to the adventure 
tourists.

worked as a catalyst which encouraged mountain tourism in 
Garhwal. This type of investment (purchase of equipment and con- 
struction of accommodation) contributed a lot to the promotion of 
mountain tourism. A trekker or mountaineer who reaches Rishikesh

Mountaineering Division and Yatra office (the two depart- 
ments of GMVN), deal with tourists of the region. Mountaineering 
Division was established in 1976 with 7 employees and officers. 
Similarly in Yatra office, in the year 1986, there were 53 employ- 
ees and officers, today the number is 95. Though permanent 
employment in Mountaineering Division and Yatra office couldn't 
increase very much, but seasonal, temporary employment increases 
with increasing number of tourists. It is well known that tourism 
projects generate employment, but its size couldn’t be established 
and developed here easily due to haphazard tourism growth and 
lack of data base. 


4.6 MOUNTAINEERING INSTITUTE AND ITS IMPACT ON MOUNTAIN 
Tourism DEVELOPMENT 


The institution which is imparting training in mountaineering 
as well as promoting it in Garhwal, is the Nehru Institute of Moun- 
taineering, Uttarkashi. Every year hundreds of youth come here for 
getting training in mountaineering. NIM was founded in 1965 and 
till date it has imparted training to thousands of people. Table 4.11 
shows the number of trainees and the courses conducted since 
1981. 

The fluctuation in the total number of trainees is not due to 
change of popularity of mountaineering among youths but it de- 
pends on the finance received from Central Govemment and U.P. 
Govemment for running the courses. Training fees for basic course 
is Rs. 650 per trainee, adyance course Rs. 650 per trainee and for 
adventure course Rs. 350 per trainee. But the Institute spends ap- 
proximately Rs. 5000 per trainee. It involves а big sum of money, 
manpower and activity on the mountains. It can be safely inferred 
that mountaineering courses are not only popularising this adven- 
ture sport in the region but simultaneously accelerating the physical 
and economic activities in the region. Instructors, doctors, adminis- 
trators, porters, washermen and cooks etc get jobs directly. This 
activity creates demand for climbing equipments, clothing, food 
and tentage. So indirectly the suppliers of these things get demand

| to project the employment generation capacity of mountain tourism 
in Garhwal. Infrastructural facilities and mountains of Garhwal are 
attracting increasing number of expeditions to Garhwal. 


4.7 MOUNTAINEERING EXPEDITIONS TO INDIA & GARHWAL HIMALAYA 


Mountaineering got momentum in India with the ascent of 
Everest by Edmund Hillary and Tenzing in 1953. Though Nanda 

Devi region was a preparatory ground for foreigners from the 

early 20th century but commercial adventure tourism entered in 

Garhwal Himalaya very late (in the decade of seventies of 20th 

century). Gradually the number of mountaineering expeditions to 


the region is increasing. Tables 4.13 and 4.14 explains the condi- 
tion.

desired destination of mountain climbers and adventure lovers. 
Though Garhwal is far behind in facilities (infrastructure) but the 
challenging peaks of Garhwal are highly attractive for Indian as 
well as foreign climbers. Figure 20 shows the foreign and Indian 
mountaineering expeditions in India. 

These expeditions spent a lot of money on food, equipment,

porterage, transport, medicine and organisation. Though much of 
the money is spent away from Garhwal in purchase of food, equip- 
ment, clothing, medicine but part of the money comes to Garhwal 
for arranging porterage, guide, cooking staff, transport and accom- 
modation etc. By providing good food, tinned juices and fruits, 
good clothes and equipment on hire in Garhwal itself it will attract 
more money from the expeditions. 


Тавів 4.15 


Summary of expenditure of Nanda Devi expedition 1981 


S.No. Head Expenditure in Rs. 

1. Equipment 1,70,415 

2: Food 40,323

2,82,838 


Source: IMF, New Delhi. 


Table 4.15 shows the estimated expenditure of the expedition 
though actual expenditure was more than this. A single expedition 
of Nanda Devi brought more than Rs. 42,750 in the regional eco- 
nomy as porters payment. Much more money can be attracted by 
providing other things to the expeditions in the region. 

Expeditions generally go with plenty of tinned fruits and juices. 
Garhwal has got good fruit belts and mountain tourism can acceler- 
ate horticulture and fruit preservation industry in the region. It will 
be convenient for the mountaineers and trekkers to buy the fruits 
and juices here rather than to carry them from far away and this 
will generate economic activities, employment opportunities and 
income in Garhwal region. This will be cheaper for the trekkers 
and mountaineers to buy their fruit requirement in Garhwal. 

Mountain tourism itself is one of the economic activities and it 
can accelerate many more activities in the region. There are differ- 
ent (minor to major technically difficult and easy) types of peaks, 
so the expenditure varies from one expedition to other. Even expe- 
ditions are also of different types like traditional, Alpine style or 


СС-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


Economics of Mountaineering & Trekking 151 


solo. Though the Alpine style has been introduced here recently but 
solo climbing hasn't approached Garhwal yet. So the expenditure 
of these expeditions (different styles) varies a lot. The one thing 
that is common to all kinds of expeditions is that a lot of money has 
got to be spent. 

This money accelerates many economic activities in the re- 
gional economy. Hence it can be said that money spent by a tourist 
circulates through the economy and stimulates it, as it changes 
hands and is spent a number of times. It creates a chain reaction in 
the economy and the constant tum over of the tourist expenditures 
is known as the multiplier effect. Though at present the concept of 
tourism multiplier is not very clear in Garhwal but in coming years 
when this adventure tourism is properly established, there will be 
no problem in calculating multiplier. 

Expenditure of tourists on transport, accommodation, food, en- 
tertainment, goods and services becomes the income of the regional 
population, but it never remains forever with them. In fact, it moves 
very fast to other hands. For example the expenditure of tourist on 
accommodation never gets stagnated with the hotel manager or 
owner but he also spends it for paying the bills of telephone, house- 
keepers, servants, other personnel and goods and services. Hence, 
it can be said that the expenditure of tourist becomes income 
for local people, but a little later it becomes their expenditure 
and income for another group of people in the society. This way 
transfer of money goes on till the leakage—payments for imports, 
foreign investments, hoarding and other diversions that take 
money out of the region. Thus, this unbroken series of conversion 
of visitors’ money constitutes the multiplier effect. The leakage 
may be because of savings, imports (clothes, equipment, food, and 
other infrastructural things), investment abroad and travel outside 
the region. The greater the leakage, the smaller will be the multi- 
plier factor. 

The multiplier effect of tourist expenditure varies from econ- 
omy to economy. Leakage of money from the economy discour- 
ages the multiplication of income. In affluent countries which are 
more self reliant and less in need of foreign imports, multiplier will 
be higher. “Even for those economies” Peter says, “where there is 
a high import and leakage content, it is hard to visualise a multi- 


CC-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


152 Tourism in Garhwal Himalaya 


plier of less than two.” It shows that tourism expenditure has got 
tremendous capacity to accelerate money velocity and activities in 
the economy. 


4.8 REGIONAL TRAVEL AGENCIES 


This mountain tourism activity has induced some enterprising 
young men to become travel agents in Garhwal. At present there 
are seven travel agencies in Uttarkashi and many more are coming 
up. Prior to the closure of Nanda Devi sanctuary, many travel agen- 
cies mushroomed at Joshimath. But the closure of Nanda Devi 
national park in 1982 gave way for the closure of these agencies. 
This closure created unemployment and frustration among the local 
people. Mountain tourism was about to take off in Chamoli district, 
when there was sudden closure to adventure activities in the region. 

Table 4.16 shows the income generated by mountain tourism in 
travel agency business in Uttarkashi. 


TABLE 4.16 


Mountaineering/Trekking expeditions conducted by travel agencies in 
Uttarkashi and income accrued (1989) 


S.No. Name of Agency No.ofIndian  No.offoreign Income Rs. 
expeditions expeditions 

1. Mountain support 20 12 79,395 

2. Mountain guide 11 7 1,93,300 


Source: Mountaineering Support, Uttarkashi. 
Mountaineering Guide, Uttarkashi. 


Mountain tourism is a seasonal activity in Garhwal. The season 
is of five-six months. Within this span of time travel agencies are 
earning quite a large sum of money. These agencies at present 
provide porters, guides, cooks, equipments, clothes, tents and make 
arrangements of transport and accommodation. ' 

Due to the closure of Nanda Devi region for mountaineering 
and trekking, Gaumukh is attracting large crowds of adventure 
tourist every year and truly speaking it has become the focal point 
of adventure activities.

Economics of Mountaineering & Trekking 153 
4.9 SAMPLE STUDY 


A sample study of Gangotri (a religio-tourist resort) has been 
conducted in 1990. 

Gangotri is situated in between 31° N lat 7895! long, at the con- 
fluence of Bhagirathi and Kedar Ganga. This small seasonal town- 
ship of Gangotri was declared as a revenue village by the U.P. 
Government in 1960. Construction of a giant iron bridge over Jadh 
Ganga river at Bhairoghati in 1985 resulted in larger number of 
tourists and pilgrims to Gangotri. 


Physical Setting, Climate and Flora & Fauna 

The altitude variation is between 9000 ft and 26000 ft. Slopes 
towards river are more than 50 degree, therefore more prone to 
landslides, erosion and instability. Climate of the region is greatly 
influenced by the height. This micro-climatic zone is situated in 
between cold and Alpine zone. Most prominent among the trees are 
deodar, blue pine, fir, spruce and birch. Among the animals black 
bear, brown bear, wild boar, blue sheep, musk deer, langoor and 
barking deer are found here. Common avifauna is pigeon, pheasant 
(chir, koklas, khaliz and monal) and chough etc. 


Population and Settlement Pattern 
The population of Gangotri township is increasing rapidly. 
Even female population is also coming up now. See Table 4.17.

According to 1981 Census there were 51 families and 49 
houses. These houses were constructed with stone, timber and slate, 
but now these have been replaced by bricks, cement and tin. Bar- 
ring exceptions, residential standard is not good in general. In 1990 
the number of houses increased to 70. Haphazard building con- 
struction created the problem of poor water supply, sanitation, 
drainage, sewer and garbage disposal. 


Infrastructure 

An integrated package of infrastructural facilities is the pre- 
requisite for an area’s development in general and tourism in par- 
ticular. 


Communication and Transport 

Road is the only means of communication to Gangotri, Bus 
stop and parking area is very congested (only 900 sq m). Condition 
of vehicular traffic is shown in Table 4.19.

Drainage & Sewage 

| There is no hygienic system of drains and sewer and every 
| drain ultimately gets submerged into Bhagirathi river. Health 
[ Department Uttarkashi every year constructs three pakka and seven 
temporary gunny bag toilets on the banks of river Bhagirathi. Ten 
Sweepers get temporary employment for such large floating popu- 
lation every year. 


Electricity 


. There is а 15 KW generator installed by UPSEB, but due to 
higher altitude, it generates only 10 KW. Five KW for the temple

Ехрепайиге Pattern 

Regarding expenditure it is found that 62% respondents never

Economics of Mountaineering £ Trekking 173 


of trekkers to this region is increasing rapidly. A marked tendency 
to regard Garhwal as tourist destination is becoming apparent. 

Home tourists are mostly religio-adventure tourists. They want 
to touch the feet of temple deity, but simultaneously want to enjoy 
nature and adventure. In the months of May-June there is a crowd 
of pure pilgrims but from August onwards till October every one is 
a pilgrim as well as a tourist. For instance іп Gangotri region every 
one wants to go to Gaumukh. During Dushera the crowd comes 
from West Bengal mostly. The main attractions are Ganga, Gan- 
gotri temple, Gaumukh and mountain scenery. 

In General the main attraction for tourists in Garhwal during 
summers are Badri-Kedar, Gangotri-Yamunotri but in autumn it is 
Gaumukh, Tapovan, Tungnath, Rudranath, Kathling glacier, Dodi- 
tal, Kedartal, Vasukital, Sahastrutal etc. 

This religio-adventure tourism has given a momentum to 
Garhwals’ economy (employment generation, increase in economic 
activity, income and development of entrepreneurship) and eco- 
logical awareness. These are some of the positive signs which indi- 
cate that tourism as an industry has come of age in the Garhwal 
region. 

It can be said that the region experienced the warmth of activi- 
ties due to tourism development. This is going to increase with 
further development of tourism. In fact regional infrastructure is 
not able to cope with increasing demand of tourist industry. In the 
months of May and June at Gangotri, it becomes difficult to get 
accommodation on rent. Many people can be found sleeping under 
the open sky. This shows that the supply force of tourist industry is 
weaker then the demand force in the region, or it can be said that 
there is enough room for the expansion of supply force (infrastruc- 
ture) of this industry. 

Though it is widely accepted that tourism has been the key 
factor in the growth and development of Char-Dhams and théir 
surrounding treks and trails. But in this process of development 
better jobs have gone to outsiders and low jobs to the local people. 
Disequilibrium between the tourist demand and supply had fanned 
inflationary trends. But few economic advantages of tourism devel- 
Opment are clearly visible, though difficult to measure exactly yet. 

Apart from religious pilgrimage, adventure tourism too is 


СС-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


174 Tourism іп Garhwal Himalaya 


approaching Himalayan heights with leaps and bounds. Tourists 
are enjoying typical Garhwal Himalayan environment and hos- 
pitality. Unique mountain scenery, snout of Gangotri glacier and 
snow-covered peaks will remain challenge for many in coming 
years too. But there is urgent need of more infrastructure building, 
maintenance of existing facilities and conservation of ecology in 
the region. Then only friendly and sustainable tourism development 
in Garhwal is possible.

Impact of Mountaineering 
and Trekking 


5.1 INTRODUCTION 


Tourism is the most desirable and essential activity оҒ modem 
man, and the cause of tourism varies from person to person and 
place to place. Since it. promotes the spirit of adventure and un- 
doubtedly has a great educative value, it involves a huge amount of 
capital as well as other factors of production to achieve different 
ends. Returns therefore, become important, more so because tour- 
ism is one of the invisible items of international trade. The expendi- 
ture the tourists incur affects the economy, society, culture and 
environment very much. When we talk about Himalaya and border 
districts of Garhwal, the issue becomes more sensitive as well as 
vital for growth and development. 

The primary expenditure involved in accommodation, trans- 
port, food, souvenir and recreational services has a direct bearing 
on different spheres of tourist destinations. Tourism has led to a 
greater mobility of the population, and has become an industry and 
accelerated growth of many towns of distinctive features and 
brightened the horizon for millions. The main objectives of devel- 
opmental policy are—increase of national revenue, increase in em- 
ployment situation, income generating activities, increase in pro- 
ductivity, greater balance between various sectors of production, 
increase in foreign. exchange earning, greater development of the 
region and finally of the country. 

Basically, keeping all these things in view a developmental 


СС-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai апа eGangotri 


176 Tourism in Garhwal Himalaya 


activity is launched and the moment such an activity gets acceler- 
ated the positive and negative impacts can be deemed as intended 
or unintended consequence. Tourism, declared as an industry, also 
exercises positive and negative effects. 

Tourism can have economic, social, cultural, ecological and 
demographic impact. 


5.2 Есомоміс IMPACT 


In recent years there has been a growing realisation of the 
potential of tourism in most countries. In certain countries tourism 
x` the single largest foreign exchange earner. The income from 
tourism has tended to increase at a higher rate than merchandise 
export. Tourism is greatest employment generator too. It injects the 
external purchasing power into the host economy and accelerates 
economic activities. Since tourism deals with people rather than 
commodities, it is difficult to present accurate figures of its contri- 
bution towards the national income. But, the identification and 
recognition of the potential tourism resources is growing rapidly in 
most of the countries. It is much more pervasive in its economic 
impact. It diffuses the benefits of foreign exchange earnings into 
the remote corners of the country and works as stimulus to every 
sector of economy. It can bring benefits other than the more 
obvious and direct economic benefits of contributing to the balance 
of payments, increasing national income and providing employ- 
ment opportunities, although quantification is difficult. There are 
very considerable social and economic benefits in the contacts 
made through visitors who develop new tastes and become inter- 
ested in new activities. One of the fastest expanding sectors of the 
economies of most industrialised countries is leisure industry. 
"Tourism represents a significant part, but only a part of this much 
wider concept.” 

The impact of tourism on the host country's economy is quite 
massive. Now a days, developed and developing, capitalist and 
socialist all countries of the world are trying to exploit their tourism 
resources for the economic development and growth. The Head of 
the Foreign Tourism Board of Russia, Ankudinov said, *Electron- 
ics, plastic, computer and many other new industries are expanding 


СС-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


— GERENS 


È 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


Impact of Mountaineering & Trekking 177 


at a much faster rate than the old classical industries. But there is 
one industry for which rate of growth can well compete with any of 
the new comers. That industry is tourism.” 

World tourism expenditure comprises about 5% of the total 
world consumer expenditure, and it will increase in future. The 
importance of tourism was formally acknowledged when the Ist 


_ United Nations General Assembly designated 1967 as the Interna- 


tional Tourism Year with a unanimous resolution recognizing that 
tourism is a basic and most desirable human activity deserving the 
praise and encouragement from all people and all governments. 

Tourism gives an opportunity to the local people to use the 
amenities specially made for tourists. “Tourist is frequently a 
source of amenities for the resident population of the tourist desti- 
nation. Tourism by its nature, tends to distribute development away 
from the industrial centres towards those regions in a country 
which have not been developed.'? 

Expenditure done for tourism development and incurred by the 
tourists accelerate diverse economic activities and leave their im- 
pact on many areas of economy. “Tourism has become the largest 
single item in the world's foreign trade, currently of the order of 
£ 8000 million a year . . . hence the economic value of tourism is 
substantial." The full impact of tourism on the economy is not just 
through expenditure on the front line tourist trades but it embraces 
all those other industries outside the direct tourism sector. The trad- 
ers directly involved in tourism, in 'order to^meet the demands of 
the tourists, purchase from other traders and industries those goods . 
and services e.g. foodstuff, furnishings, camping, sporting equip- 
ments and facilities, which are demanded by tourists. Hence many 
people get involved in fulfilling the demands of tourists. In this 
process developing countries are also forging ahead. A channel of 
financial flow has started from richer countries to the developing 
countries, raising the latter's export earnings and rate of economic 
growth. : ; 

Similar are the experiences of Garhwal. Age-old pilgrimage 
is intermingling with recently introduced adventure tourism. For 
better analysis and easy understanding economic impact is visual- 
ised in terms of employment, income, regional development and 
growth.

‘5.2.1 Tourism and Employment 

Tourism is a labour-oriented industry and it provides many 
times more employment than provided by any other manufacturing 
industry. Invested capital in adventure tourism in tum gives em- 
ployment in hundreds of different ways in hotels, restaurants, 
boarding houses, recreation centres, porter agencies, travel agen- 
cies, gift shops, transport companies and other utility centres. In 


fact adventure tourism provides a wide range of employment from ` 


skilled to unskilled such as front-house personnel, chamber-maids, 
porters, kitchen-staff, guides and travel agents etc. It can play a 
crucial role in combating unemployment. This employment genera- 
tion capacity of adventure tourism increases in marginal areas, 
which are relatively isolated and economically underdeveloped. 

Adventure tourism is in search of rural or country side destina- 
tion. Unemployment is acute problem of developing countries in 
general and backward and isolated regions in particular. It is an 
indisputable fact that no technology on earth can render human 
factor less dispensable than in tourism. It is felt that in areas where 
resources and market manufacturing enterprises are less, tourism is 
the only alternative left for increasing employment and promoting 
development. Tourism can be the main component of a regional 
policy aimed at achieving an equitable balance between major 
industrial areas and other non-industrial areas of the country. 

In Garhwal, adventure tourism will activate many more indus- 
tries resulting in creation of many job opportunities. Increasing 
tourist inflow not only accelerates with transport, accommodation 
and amenities sectors but also increases the demand for secondary 
industrial products like woollen clothes, wooden show pieces, food 
and recreational components etc. World Tourism Organisation esti- 
mates that, “tourism generates directly and indirectly 74 million 
jobs in the world.” According to Prof. Risch, "tourist industry in 
Switzerland provided employment to 1,40,000 persons, of which 
about 70,000 were employed directly by the hotel industry. This 
shows that tourism alone provides employment to the 50% of the 
total working роршайоп.’% 

In Garhwal, particularly in Chamoli and Uttarkashi pilgrimage 
and adventure tourism are going side by side, and giving employ- 
ment to many people in hotels, restaurants, rest-houses, inns, trans- 


CC-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


Impact of Mountaineering & Trekking 179 


port, travel agencies, amenities sector, souvenir industry and іп 
industries providing equipment, furniture, accessories used by 
catering establishments. Apart from these electricity, gas supplies, 
sanitation, water supplies, vegetables, fruits, dairy product etc also 
provide employment to the people. But, in general domestic tourists 
will, by and large, generate more favourable relative employment 
than foreign tourists. Tourism industry provides employment in 
bulk. A 200 rooms hotel costing 3 crores generally needs a staff of 
not less than 400. Most of the industrial enterprises costing the 
same amount have much less employment potentials.’ 

The number of permanent employees and officers employed in 
Yatra office of GMVN was 58 in the year 1986, 72 in 1988 and it 
went up to 95 in the year 1990, whereas the number of temporary 
employees varies with the tourist influx during summer. Mountain- 
eering Division of GMVN started functioning in 1976. Today it has 
8 permanent employees apart from large number of its casual em- 
ployees in summers. Both the offices, Yatra and Mountaineering 
Division appoint employees on daily wages during the tourist sea- 
son to cope with the heavy inflow of tourists and pilgrims. In 
Garhwal, GMVN is the foremost organisation that caters to the 
tourists and pilgrims with highest repute. Apart from GMVN, em- 
ployment opportunities are created in other hotels, souvenir shops, 
rest-houses dak bungalows, travel agencies, transport system and 
road-side amenities etc. In Uttarkashi alone, seven travel agencies 
have come up within a very short period. Probably the most out- 
standing benefit of tourism in developing countries with backward- 
ness and high unemployment is its employment potential. But the 
negative impacts like menial jobs for local people, seasonal unem- 
ployment, inflationary trends in prices and uneven economic devel- 
opment etc are surfacing in the regional development scenario. 


5.2.2 Tourism and Income è 

Tourism is very important as it provides a source of income to 
the host country. In developing region it becomes difficult to meas- 
ure the income generated from the expenditure of domestic and 
international tourists, but it is very clear that arrival of foreign tour- 
ists chanalises the flow of foreign exchange in the area. In the case 
of some countries notably Spain, Portugal, Switzerland, Austria, 


CC-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and'eGangotri 


180 Tourism іп Garhwal Himalaya 


Yugoslavia, Greece, Bermuda and Bahamas the invisible earnings 
from tourism are of a major significance and have a very strong 
and positive effect on the balance of payment. Thus, tourism is a 
very useful means of earning the much needed foreign exchange. 
In fact it is an invisible export, which offsets a loss on the visible 
trading account and is of critical importance in the overall financial 
reckoning. Most developing countries from time to time have seri- 
ous problems of balance of payments that forced the countries to 
focus on tourism. "Tourism is a very useful means of eaming 
much needed foreign currency; indeed, it is almost without rival as 
a source, as such countries as Spain, Eire, Mexico and Panama 
have found.” Advanced transport technology and increased dis- 
posable income is spreading tourism from earth to sky. This tour- 
ism expansion is increasing the income of countries. It is undeni- 
able that for certain developing countries, tourism is a factor of 
economic development for correcting an adverse balance of pay- 
ments situation, but it is equally true that developed countries with 
higher income, standard of living also get advantage in their for- 
eign exchange reserves from it. 

Garhwal too is doing well in income generation from tourism. 
Apart from the inns enroute, today good hotels and tourist bunga- 
lows of GMVN, Forest Department and PWD rest-houses have 
' come up and are providing good facilities. Today the tourists/pil- 
grims want to make their stay comfortable, so they prefer good ac- 
commodation in comparison to cheaper inns. They prefer confort- 
able journey in deluxe coaches, cars and cabs. Therefore the trans- 
porters, hotel owners, shopkeepers are earning quite a large sum 
from tourists. Income of Yatra office and that of the Mountaineer- 
ing Division of GMVN is increasing every year, though it is sea- 
sonal even today. Tables 4.1 to 4.3 and 5.1 explain the fact. Sum- 
mer is the time when tourists/pilgrims come to Garhwal. Winter 
tourism is yet to find feet though Auli ski-resort got off to a good 
start. Seasonality is quite clear from Table 5.1. 

5.2.3 Tourism and Multiplier Effect 

Money spent by tourists affect the economy of a region in a 
profound way. The expenditure made by these tourists becomes the 
income of others and this process keeps on repeating till it leaks 


СС-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai апа eGangotri 


Impact of Mountaineering & Trekking 181 


out. When a tourist pays his hotel bills the money goes to the hotel. 
Hotel owner or officials will use the money to pay various debts 
incurred or make outlays on behalf of the tourists—electricity, tele- 
phone, personnel and other goods and services. The recipients in 
tum use the money in this way that may be spent several times, 
spreading into different sectors of the economy. Each time this 
will give rise to fresh income until ‘leakage’ (payment for imports, 
foreign investment, hoarding and other diversions take money out 
of circulation). This unbroken series of conversion of tourist money 
constitutes the multiplier effect. Such leakages are low in deve- 
loped countries but high in developing countries.

First hand expenditure of tourists accelerates many times the 
economic activities and income. The greater the number of hands 
through which such money passes, the greater the beneficial effect 
on the gross national product and the national accounts. In fact 
multiplier is an income conceptor. It can be said that multiplier may 
be defined as the effect of total expenditure of the tourist on the 
income and employment of the region and country as a whole. 
Tourist expenditure not only supports the tourist industry but it 
provides base for other industry to come up. Tourism expenditure 


СС-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai апа eGangotri 


182 Tourism in Garhwal Himalaya 


multiplies economic activities and income many times before it 
diffuses or leaks out. 


Estimation of Multiplier 

The “multiplier effect” of the expenditure made by tourists 
varies from economy to economy. Let the sub-division of tourist 
expenditure and its estimate be as follows: 


Division Estimate 
Accommodation (including camping) x% 
Food (including high altitude food) у% 
Transport (including carry animals 

and porterage) 2% 
Shopping 5% 
Recreation p% 
Others 4% 


Тһе money paid for accommodation will be divided towards 
wages and salaries, administrative ezpenses, commission to travel 
agents, hiring of tentage for camping, interest оп investments, ге- 
pair and maintenance, return to investors, depreciation and insur- 
ance etc. The beneficiaries will spend some of this profit or earning 
on local product and services. This trend will continue till the 
money leaks out of the economy. The money spent on other divi- 
Sions will follow the same process. It has been estimated that the 
money after fourth or fifth transactions does not move again to 
change the hands, specially in developing countries.

5.2.4 Tourism and Economic Development 

Tourism like any other industry generates diverse activities in 
various fields and modifies the area in which it operates. The raw 
materials of the industry are the natural objects, old townships, 
monuments and temples, the non-renewable resources. Around 
such things, if infrastructure, shopping centres, recreation centres 
and public utilities are provided a particular area can become a 
high priority destination of tourists. It is certain that this will be 
utilised by the local people also, at least partly if not fully and will 
be a plus point for the development of the region. Tourist expendi- 
ture in marginal and isolated areas is of great and special signifi- 
cance. In fact, this will be the best solution to the problem of 
unemployment and underdeveloped characters of the economy. 
The world body of travel and tourism (United Nations Conference 
of International Travel and Tourism, Rome 1963) experienced the 
fact way back in the decade of sixties that tourism is not only 
important as a source of foreign exchange earner, but also as a 
factor in the location of industry and in the development of less 
developed regions. Even in some cases the development of tourism 
may be the only means of promoting the economic advancement of 
less developed areas, lacking in other resources. Underdeveloped 
areas with high scenic value are the high potential tourism areas. 
These areas, if developed for tourism, can bring a lot of prosperity 
to the local people and area. 

It is more accurate to call tourism as a group of industries 
rather than an industry. The direct impact of tourism is clearly 
visible on hotels, restaurants, transport, travel agents and tour op- 
erators etc. In fact tourism accelerates most of the commodity and 
service sector of the economy. But there are few others who get 
influenced indirectly by tourist arrivals and tourism development. 
They are the agriculturists, builders, horticulturists, souvenir manu- 
facturers, public utility owners etc. 

In Garhwal, adventure tourism and pilgrimage are not only

providing. employment in primary sector directly, but also provid- 
ing employment and development in secondary and tertiary sector. 
The process of revival started in many dying industries of yester 
years like wood carving, woollen cloth manufacturing and handi- 
craft in the region due to pilgrims/tourists. Road network is improv- 
ing, fleets of buses and cabs are increasing, travel agencies are 
mushrooming and horticulture is becoming profitable. According to 
Mr. Chib, "one of the less known and therefore less recognised 
benefits ОҒ tourism is that it helps to redress regional imbalances.” 

In Garhwal, the hardy and stout local people were not able to 
make much out of their physical strength due to lack of opportuni- 
ties of work, resulting in manpower drainage to the plains, mostly 
serving in forces, but the recently introduced mountain tourism ac- 
tivities have started providing them opportunities to work in moun- 
tain tourism as guides, travel agents, tour operators, porters, hoteli- 
ers seasonally and sparing themselves much time for use in agricul- 
ture. 

Due to the mountain tourism activities good accommodation is 
coming up, amenities are improving, good markets are developing 
at Joshimath, Uttarkashi, Rishikesh and Srinagar. Good schools, 
medical facilities, road-side amenities are coming up. This all is 
giving more opportunities of work to the local people and better 
standard of living is the natural outcome. Pilgrimage and mountain 
tourism activities are giving momentum to the stagnant economy of 
border districts of Garhwal. Time is not far away when properly 
planned adventure tourism activities will accelerate multi-dimen- 
sional economic activities in Garhwal, blocking the manpower exo- 
dus to the plains. The Swiss are among the most prosperous people 
in Europe. They have achieved their high standard of living partly 
because of their mountains, but mostly they have been able to win 
their prosperity largely because they have learned special skills and 
abilities which people who live in flatter reaches and more fertile 
lands do not need.'? Such skills are necessary for the development 
of a region. There was a time when Alps were becoming depopu- 
lated because of its mountainous structure, but gradually this diffi- 
cult terrain with the help of tourism rescued the region from eco- 
nomic disaster and depopulation. It seems that border districts of 
Uttarkashi and Chamoli (zero industry regions) are going to be res-
