cued by mountain tourism. Best thing about adventure tourism is 
that it disperses the activities to non-industrial, undeveloped and 
barren land mass. 


5.3 Socio-CuLTURAL IMPACT 


Tourism is а passport of peace. The first appearance of tourism 
in the world had a socio-cultural motivation and it stood as a unique 
vehicle for cultural exchange, which is necessary for a deeper 
understanding among the peoples of the world. It must not be re- 
garded only as a business in the world, but as a means by which 
people can get to know and understand each other and maintain 
peace. Apart from goodwill and friendship, tourism gives a chance 
for socio-cultural exchange and scientific, technological interac- 
tion. This impact becomes more prominent and clear in the case of 
mountain tourism. Because, mountains have a knack of bringing 
men together."! 

Cultural and social exchanges take’ place with visiting tourists. 
Tourism not only effects changes in the host country but in the 
guests too. Travel has always enabled man to broaden his mental 
horizons. When people travel to distant lands they gain an insight 
into the customs, traditions and ways of life of other people and are 
thus able to perceive the unity of the human society. A cultural 
exchange of habits, behaviour and ways of life among hosts and 
guests takes place in a smooth way. There is hardly any other 
activity which presents so wide a scope for the promotion of human 
understanding, so smoothly and so successfully as tourism. It de- 
velops a link of friendliness among individuals and societies. Peter 
has rightly said that the international tourism contributes maximum 
in creating poeple’s interest in the world affairs and friendly under- 
standing for foreigners, foreign behaviour and tastes. 2 

Tourism brings the socio-cultural heritage into the economic 
circuit by justifying the investments. It is an important medium of 
social and cultural development as a means of social and cultural 
education for the hosts and the guests. Development of tourism 
changes the pattern of social amenities too. Drinking water, trans- 
port, electricity etc follow pace with increasing tourists, which ulti- 
mately is used by the local people also. The tackling up of certain 


СС-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


Impact of Mountaineering & Trekking 187 


social problems, opening of schools and dispensaries and revival of 
old customs and style along the trek enroute to Everest in Solu- 
Khumbu district of Nepal is yet another example of humanising 
tourism and making it more acceptable and trauma-free for local 
people. The World Tourism Conference which was held at Manila, 
Philippines in October 1980 stated that international tourism can 
contribute to the establishment of a new international economic 
order that will help in socio-economic development and progress. 
This can be said that tourism is perhaps the most effective means of 
promoting international understanding and goodwill. 

Apart from positive impacts of tourism, it certainly creates cer- 
tain undesirable inroads into the socio-cultural life of the host coun- 
try. The drug menace spread by European hippies in developing 
countries is one of the damages done by tourism. Theft, robbery, 
criminal acts and growing selfish materialism are some of the evil 
impacts of tourism on the simple rural people of developing coun- 
tries. It is said that some times the hosts are confronted with com- 
paratively wealthy, culturally different visitors. This results in 
changed cultural values and decay of native language and customs. 
In case of certain destinations, initially cultural traditions and life 
Style attract the tourists. If these things get polluted with the inflow 
of the tourists, there will be no tourism in future. Therefore, it is 
essential to maintain regional identities along with modern facilities 
and welcome smile for the tourists. In an article in New York Times, 
Dr. Frances Collington tried to prove a correlation between the 
growth of tourism and increase in divorce in Hawaii. Tourism 
experts did not hesitate to equate mass tourism with post-barbarian 
invasions. 

But to compare the negative and positive impacts of tourism in 
Study area, one easily comes to the conclusion that the positive 
impacts of tourism on the socio-cultural life of hosts are greater 
than the negative ones. “The frequent charge that tourism contri- 
butes to a degeneration in this field appears, however, to be an ex- 
aggeration. Arts, crafts and local culture have been revitalized as a 
direct result of tourism. Transformation of traditional forms often 
accompanies the development but does not necessarily lead to 
degeneration.””'*

Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 
188 Tourism in Garhwal Himalaya 


5.4 ENVIRONMENTAL IMPACT 


From time immemorial civilisations were born and nurtured in 
wilderness. The Vedic and Puranic literature underlines the impor- 
tance of wilderness for balanced development of man and civilisa- 
tion. This was the time when people were very close to nature. 
Great love, affection and respect were the hallmarks of the people. 
Each and every object of nature was worshipped, respected and 
conserved. There is a beautiful shaloka (couplet) in Atharva Veda 
which when translated means, “whatever I take from Earth may 
that have quick growth again, purifier, may we not injure the vitals 
or thou heart.” This was pronounced more than 5000 years ago. 
Therefore, every object of nature was reverentially protected. To- 
day's closeness of man with nature is not because of spontaneous 
love and respect, but due to tiredness caused by the sickening life 
of cities. Today, large number of city dwellers are trying to get 
mental and physical solace from the isolated pockets of fragile 
natural environment. The ills of industrialisation are over-pressuris- 
ing the demand for nature. 

This is resulting in more competition to grab the wilderness by 
different groups and users.” Developmental activities of recent past 
have disturbed the balance between man and nature. 

Tourism development in mountain and remote areas also acce- 
lerated ecological problems, like garbage trails, deforestation, dis- 
turbed flora and fauna life systems, over-crowding, congestion, 
landslides and climatic changes etc. Garhwal Himalaya particularly 
the upper regions like Chirbasa, Bhojbasa and Nanda Devi sanctu- 
ary are facing utter deforestation, poaching and landslides. In- 
discriminate wood cutting for fuel is creating landslides and soil 
erosion. 

This is one side of the coin, the other side Says that tourism 
advocates conservation desperately. Without nature there will be no 
tourism, therefore tourism demands nature conservation. Man and 
nature. be viewed together. Therefore, awareness about co-exis- 
tence of man and nature was born. It is said that tourism interest 
complement conservation and recreation in many ways. Tourism 
elements—transportation, guided tours, literature etc stimulate 
interest in landscape and understandings of the environment, an 


СС-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


Impact of Mountaineering & Trekking 189 


important component of conservation. Much of the conservation 
ideology and practice fosters tourism and recreation. It reveals con- 
cern for aesthetics and underscores a direct connection between 
recreation and tourism, especially in Supporting, protecting and 
making accessible both vast and intimate areas of outstanding 
scenic beauty. 

The philosophy that all human conduct affects nature must be 
carefully judged as it has gained considerable ground in the last 
two decades. Ultimately, the General Assembly of United Nations 
on October 29th, 1982 adopted and declared World Charter for 
Nature. This was based on Human Rights Declaration, Stockholm 
Declaration (1972) and World Conservation Strategy (1980). 
World Charter for Nature is really a collective call to human kind 
in favour of nature conservation. This was the culmination of 
seven-year long effort, starting from the opening session of the 
12th General Assembly of the International Union for Conservation 
of Nature and Nature Resources (IUCN) at Kunshasa (Zaire) in 
September 1975. Mrs. Indira Gandhi, while launching the world 
conservation strategy in India in March 1980, said, “the interest 
in conservation is not a sentimental one but the rediscovery of a 
truth well known to our ancient sages. The Indian tradition teaches 
us that all forms of life—human, animal and plant—are so closely 
inter-linked that disturbance in one gives rise to imbalance in the 
other . . . Nature is beautifully balanced. Each little thing has its 
own place, its duty and utility. Any disturbance creates a chain 
reaction. Which may not be visible for some time. Taking a frag- 
mentary view of life has created global and national problems.” 

The slightest disturbance in the ecology of Himalaya is disas- 
trously repercussive, according to most of the Himalayan ecolo- 
gists. In ecological terms the Himalaya is the youngest and most 
fragile mountain systems of the earth. Seemingly fixed and eternal, 
it is changing rapidly geologically and ecologically. Eighteenth 
century never felt the impact of man on nature adversely, but the 
idea of conflict between man and his environment grew in late 19th 
century and 20th century, which never bothered the 18th century 
man at all.'^ Developing countries realised the presence of the 
ghost of growing commercial attitude towards nature very late. 
Most of the developing countries are facing ecological crises due to 


CC-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


. Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai апа eGangotri 


190 Tourism in Garhwal Himalaya 


their haphazard development programmes and not due to the over- 
use of resource potentials. 

In Garhwal Himalaya growth of pilgrimage and adventure 
tourism has created some disturbances in the ecosystem of the re- 
gion. Mainly cutting of wood for fuel and timber, blasting of moun- 
tains for the construction of roads, and concrete buildings in serene 
locations are creating ecological havoc which results in floods, 
droughts, cloud-bursts, landslides and change of climate etc. If this 
process of development and change goes on unchecked, ecological 
catastrophe in near future will be unavoidable in Garhwal and the 
plains will also not remain unaffected. 

Three decades ago the local economy was quite self-sufficient 
due to the limited requirements of highlanders. They found their 
local resources limitless, but the material advancement in low land 
plains (which lured the highlanders) and the absence of employ- 
ment alternatives other than agriculture in development plans re- 
Sulted in the stream of ecological refugees of Garhwal with eco- 
logical capital flight. “It is a brain-drain which is as bad as the 
drain of ecological capital. In this situation past concepts of plan- 
ning are futile.””” Forests were commercially cut to fulfill the 
needs and greed of few contractors. In this process people started 
migrating towards plains. Nearly 150 years ago, similar conditions 
prevailed in Switzerland, Bavaria and Austria. In the early phase of 
industrialisation these countries also lost their forests; coal came as 
the alternative energy source, later hydel energy came to their res- 
cue and today they are paradise for tourists. ‘‘In this subsequent 
century and half, three factors have mainly accounted for the pres- 
ent state of conservation and development. The first was land use 
planning . . . . The second factor in Europe was the development of 
hydro-electric power as a major alternative source of local energy 
for development, preventing the destruction of forest cover. The 
third was the development of planned and dispersed tourism. Be- 
tween these three, there seems to be a happy blend of conservation 
and development.’ 

Uttarkashi and Chamoli districts and their Char Dhams Badri- 
Kedar, Gangotri-Yamunotri were open for Hindu pilgrims from 
time immemorial, but these Dhams and other areas (apart from 
inner-line area) were thrown open for the foreign tourists in 1975. ` 


СС-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


Impact of Mountaineering & Trekking 191 


Mountaineers and trekkers of our country and abroad started com- 
ing to Garhwal for adventure. The crowd of pilgrims and adventure 
lovers is increasing year by year, but the amenities are not increas- 
ing in the same proportion. This mismanagement is leading towards 
ecological crises in Garhwal. Lack of infrastructure forces the pil- 
grims and tourists to apply pressure upon the natural resource base. 
Trampling by haphazard camping erodes natural ground cover, ex- 
posing the surface to erosion from rains. Horses and ponies also 
disturb the trek route with their hooves and make it prone to soil 
erosion. Cutting of wood for fuel is also converting the forests into 
pock-marked barren land. 

In Garhwal, if the introduction of tourism is spoiling nature and 
giving negative environmental results, certainly this is because of 
faulty planning, policies and management rather than over-use. 
Land, water, air, forest, flora and fauna everything in the nature has 
the capacity to accommodate certain number of human beings. It is 
time to. reframe our policy so that both nature and tourism could co- 
exist otherwise the imbalance could become irreversible. This is 
happening in all the developing countries. In developed countries it 
is absolutely the other way “in some cases, because of increased 
flow of tourist to particular places plant and animal life have in- 
creased in volume in order to maintain better balance between the 
flora-fauna and visitors, resulting in improvement in many game- 
forest management programmes. Increased tourism has provided 
the motivation and economic support for many, of these program- 
mes."'* Unfortunately, it is not being pursued in developing coun- 
tries, specially in Garhwal there is very little management of such 
resources, though the region is rich in natural resources. 

All the Char Dhams and prominent treks are having environ- 
mental problems due to indiscriminate forest cutting, garbage dump 
and untreated sewage etc. Among the most frequently visited and 
adversely affected areas are Lokpal-Hemkund, Valley of Flowers, 
Badrinath, Gangotri-Gaumukh and Nanda Devi sanctuary. 

For Hemkund-Lokpal and Valley of Flowers trek, Govind Ghat 
is the road-head. From here a trek to Ghangaria is approximately 5 
to 6 hours away. Mostly the Sikhs and few nature lovers go there. 
During tourist season few tea-shops come up on the way. These are 
always constructed with forest timber and for fuel forest is merci- 


CC-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


192 Tourism in Garhwal Himalaya 


lessly cut. Government opened the area for tourists and pilgrims 
without giving a thought to the basic amenities essential for human 
beings. Pilgrims, trekkers and pack animals (mule and pony) walk 
on the same track resulting in mule dung, mud, filth and rubbish on 
the footpath which is stamped by the walker knowingly or unknow- 
ingly. All the rubbish and waste water of tea shops also come on to 
the track or backyard of shops, converting the area as junkyard. Big 
boards and name plates tacked on the tree trunks by different 
groups of people are converting the natural landscape into commer- 
cial advertising hoarding. But the worst thing is the haphazardly 
coming up settlement of Ghangaria. This is spoiling the aesthetics 
of the region on 0.75 hectares of land. For five-six months at least 
110 to 120 people (tea shop owners and their servants) stay there to 
entertain the tourists and the pilgrims. Total accommodation capa- 
city of Gurudwara is approximately 2000 people, whereas GMVN 
lodge has got 50 bed accommodation. A population of 500 mule 
drivers and porters remain there every day. It is expected that the 
accommodation will be expanded for 3000 people or more within a 
year or two. 

The immediate impact is on the 2.2 hectares of land around the 
accommodation and tea shops. Encroachment of forest land is un- 
avoidable in future. Mule dung and human excreta scattered in the 
forest opposite to tourist lodge makes it a nauseating experience. 
Local tea shops and Gurudwara require minimum 400 kgs of fire- 
wood per day, which is purchased from U.P. Forest Corporation 
and transported from Govind Ghat, but the chopped heads of the 
trees are telling the untold story of inhuman mauling of nature in 
the area. Sewage disposal is going to create serious problem in near 
future. The sewage pits of Gurudwara and Tourist Lodge are in 
critical shape. Bridle path is all soaked with urine and filthy water. 

The problems of deforestation, garbage, filth and other un- 
treated waste all around is not due to the saturation of carrying 
capacity of the region, but due to haphazard planning and program- 
mes. Young has rightly said, “these evil consequences are not 
inherent in the development of tourism; they just happen when 
tourism is developed in a thoughtless and casual way.””?° 

Though the polygonum weed in the Valley of Flowers is very 
dense, but even then the beautiful Alpine flowers are quite promi- 


СС-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


Impact of Mountaineering & Trekking 193 


nent. Candy-wrappers and other litter, some times detract from the 
freshness of the wilderness. Night camping in park area is banned 
for the conservation of the flora and fauna of the park. Though park 
authorities have placed litter bins in three different places, but the 
people are not accustomed to using them. Eco-consciousness has 
yet to be developed among people. The best thing is to introduce 
teaching natural ecology and cultural ecology in school education, 
otherwise the scars of human interference on nature will be incur- 
able. 

Nanda Devi national park also faces similar problems of defor- 
estation, litter and disturbed animal life and was closed for tourists 
in 1982. This was done with a view to protecting the natural envi- 
ronment of the park region. Simply declaring an area as a prohib- 
ited one can not solve the problem. In fact such areas need action- 
oriented programmes like afforestation, demarcation of camp areas, 
strict restrictions on leaving refuse, waste and digging of hearths 
etc, rather than closing the area for nature enthusiasts. “Tourism 
need not be shut down, if the many segments of tourism develop- 
ment perform their individual roles in better collaboration and with 
great sensitivity to the negative economic, social and environmental 
consequences.””! 

Gangotri township is undergoing the similar plight of indis- 
criminate forest cutting for fuel and timber, garbage heaps and 
refuse, untreated refuse and filth of drains into river Bhagirathi, 
night soil on the banks of the river and landslides all along the track 
of Gaumukh and around Suryakund are some of the consequences 
which are distracting the attention of every passerby. At Gangotri 
average fire wood consumption is 20 quintals per day (every one 
collects the fire wood from the forests though government fire 
wood depot is there to provide the fire wood on payment). Haphaz- 
ard building construction is killing the aesthetic value of the region. 

Construction of Bhaironghati-Lanka bridge in 1985 made the 
journey to Gangotri comfortable resulting in increasing number of 
tourists and pilgrims. This increasing number of tourist/pilgrim is 
disturbing the equilibrium of demand and supply of services and 
goods. 

Track from Gangotri to Gaumukh is passing through serious 
ecological problems of dilapidated forest, landslides, and garbage

trails. Unauthorised possession of land for the construction of huts, 
ashrams and tea shops is most noticeable. Chirbasa, once famous 
for its blue pine forest has been deprived of its glory. Birches of 
Bhojbasa are all finished and the juniper bushes have disappeared 
from nearby areas, and now the attack is on far distant juniper 
bushes and birch trees. 

The township of Badrinath and its fuel and energy crises have 
converted the surroundings into barren mountains. Kedarnath is 
going to meet the same fate, if proper fuel and energy arrangements 
are not made. UNESCO sponsored conference in Munich in 
December 1975 is still relevant today and it needs to be recalled 
that man has been the maker of desert in past ages, even with his 
barren hands, and often in the name of progress. Now that he is 
multiplying fast and has more powerful technology, his capacity to 
endanger and balance between man and nature (only one earth) has 
enhanced many times. This technology and thrust for development 
is more dangerous when the Steps are taken in haste. 


5.5 IMPACT ON DEMOGRAPHY AND HUMAN SETTLEMENT 


Demographic structure and human settlement are always af- 
fected by the economic development of the area. Sometimes eco- 
nomic development works as a Catalytic agent for positive demo- 
graphic changes and sometime just reverses the process. In fact the 
quality and structure of population changes according to the re- 
gional development. Initially the efforts of the people change the 
economy and society and later on the process of progress changes 
quantity and qualities of the population. In Namche Bazar (Everest 
region, Nepal) there was a noticeable change in the quantity and 
quality of population. In 1970 there were 11.7 per cent Nepali 


Tourism development is changing the demographic structure 
and human settlement in Garhwal Himalaya too. All the Char 
Dhams and adventure tourism destinations are seasonally popu- 
lated. During summer months the abode of Gods welcomes the pil- 
grims and the recreationists. In olden times the feeling to serve the 


CC-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 
Impact of Mountaineering & Trekking 195 


visitors among the local people was so desperate that 10 сагп from 
them was a secondary objective. Though people were eaming their 
livelihood from pilgrimage, but to serve them and thus earn reli- 
gious satisfaction was the prime objective. 

However, modernisation has brought in its train many behavi- 
oural changes. Modern commercialisation is making the local 
people more conscious about money making. Now the pandas, 
shopkeepers, pony-owners and porters, every one wants to earn fast 
money. Indirectly, even cheating the visitors is not treated as sin. 
So the general feeling of serving the religious trekkers is substi- 
tuted by profit maximisation at any cost. These seasonal pilgrim 
and tourist centres are demographically changing rapidly. Accord- 
ing to 1971 Census the population of Gangotri was 9 with no fe- 
male, but this has multiplied manyfold within ten years and became 
123 in 1981 with 6 females.” It is due to migration and not due to 
higher fertility rate. 

This marked change is not prominent quantitatively at many 
places, but qualitative changes, mainly behavioural (business 
mindedness, cheating and laziness etc) are also common among the 
local people. Local people feel that they have to earn for the whole 
year within six months. Thus dishonest means of earning are also 
resorted to. 

All the pilgrim centres, trekking and mountain climbing desti- 
nations are facing the same problems. But the best thing out of this 
tourism development is that it has partially checked the manpower 
migration towards plains from Garhwal. Especially adventure tour- 
ism is very encouraging in economic development of the region. 
Due to this Himalayan tourism development the local people have 
started earning their livelihood in Garhwal itself. This has gener- 
ated a sort of self-confidence among local people, who were other- 
wise ready to migrate towards plains. College-going boys are get- 
ting employment in adventure tourism activities and gaining self- 
confidence. 

Tourism development makes deeper impressions on settlement 
pattern too. The spate of buildings are the result of tourism devel- 
opment all around the world in general and Spain, England, Den- 
mark, Sweden, France and the USA in particular. This change took 
place in the seventies and eighties of this century. Rapid growth of 


CC-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


196 Tourism іп Garhwal Himalaya 


second homes in rural areas of developed countries indicates 
change in settlement pattern due to tourism development. Rapidly 
coming up hotels and tourist houses in Garhwal are changing the 
settlement pattern of the region. The sites, situational aspects, lay- 
out and overall form is inevitably different from the indigenous 
Style. At places upcoming settlements do not even suit the local 
pattem and natural surroundings. But this is very correct that the 
influx of tourist increases and changes the settlement pattern of the 
host country. 

The sorry plight of the settlement pattern is that it is unplanned, 
„haphazard, unhygienic and unscientific. This is not the story of 
Char Dhams only, but all the road-side townships are facing the 
same problems. 


5.6 CONCLUSION 


There are certain positive and negative impacts of mountain- 
eering and trekking on economy, socio-cultural life and ecology of 
the region. These effects for the Study region are summarised here. 


Positive Economic Effects 

* The income of the local people is continuously increasing. 

* The Government is eaming hard currency and its balance 
of payment is improving. 

* Itis creating jobs for hoteliers, travel agents, guides, porters 
and HAPs. 

* Some of the snow-covered peaks, barren land-mass and 
Stretches of water bodies are being utilised for economic 
gains. 

* The economic activities of entire region are being diversi- 
fied. 

* Better multiplier is giving momentum to the economy. 

* The existing infrastructure is improving and new infrastruc- 
ture is coming up. 

* Local art, craft, souvenir, handicraft and other small and 
cottage industries are developing. 

Government is getting additional revenue through taxes. 
It is improving the standard of living of the people.

Impact of Mountaineering & Trekking 197 


It is checking the drainage of manpower towards plains. 

It is giving opportunities to local people to become entre- 
preneurs by penetrating into travel and tourism business. 

It is mobilising the funds for regional development.

inflationary trends in prices.

cooks, HAP, waiters etc. 

The tourism of the region is presently confined to summer

The over-use of scarce resources like wood is causing

the mountaineers and trekkers. 

An exchange of food in mixed expeditions has developed 
the tendency of fast food, resulting in extra time for adven-

Tourist communication systems and other modem facilities 
are also being used by the local people. 

Shy women of traditional Garhwali society are coming out 
as expert mountain guides like the members of Seven Sis-

Кеше Socio-Cultural Effects 


ж 


Westemisation and selfish tendencies аге visualizing in 
local youths. 

Culture of the region is slowly losing its beauty and tradi- 
tionality. 

It is leading towards the decay of religious practices and 
sentiments. 

The valuable antiques are being smuggled out. 

Crimes like gambling, theft and drug addiction are increas- 
ing. 

Begger menace is developing. 

Concentration of tourists in particular spots is resulting in 
scarcity of facilities and chaos in the society. 

It is changing the life style of local people and the native 
architecture of the region. Concrete brick houses аге sub- 
stituting the beautiful wooden houses. 

Inferiority complex amongst the local people is developing. 


Posi Be Ecological Effects 


Mountain tourism is developing eco-consciousness among 
local people. 

It is giving an opportunity to study one of the most inhospi- 
table but unique ecological and natural surroundings. 
Mountain tourism and conservational measures are check- 
ing the drainage of ecological wealth. 

The concept of hygiene and sanitation is developing 
amongst the local people. 

The importance of scenic landscape, flora, fauna, barren 


СС-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


Impact of Mountaineering & Trekking 199 


land-mass and other neglected natural resources of 
Himalaya is being realised. 

* General awareness about the pollution (air, water, noise, 
garbage and trash) is being developed amongst the local 
people and the government. 

* Accessibility of mountaineers and trekkers to remote and 
difficult areas makes the poacher scare from poaching. 


Negative Ecological Effects 
Destruction of forest for fuel and energy is taking place in 
the region. 

* Misuse and abuse of natural resources by local people, 
trekkers, pilgrims, mountaineers and carry animals is lead- 
ing towards degradation of the landscape. 

* Destruction of Alpine flora from trampling, haphazard 
camping and hobby collection is endangering some rare 
species. 

* Garbage trails and dirty camp sites are developing like on 
Gangotri-Gaumukh trek route and Bhojbasa camp site. 

* Habitat disturbance for wild fauna is taking place. 

* Pollution of water due to the submergence of drains into 
rivers is happening. 

* Unplanned and unscientific road construction is leading 
towards landslides and floods. 

* The disturbance of top soil of trek route by the hooves of 
ponies and mules is causing soil erosion. 


It will not be hyperbolic to say that the benefits of adventure 
tourism are greater than the harmful losses. Directly it contributes 
to the balance of payment as an earner of hard currency. It diverts 
development towards non-industrial backward regions, creates 
employment opportunities in seasonal market. This number will 
grow with the popularity of winter tourism in the region. Socio- 
cultural interaction is taking place with certain positive and 
negative impacts. Ecological gains and losses are also noticeable.

Problems Associated with 
Mountaineering and Trekking 


6.1 INTRODUCTION 


“The population of hill areas is thin and scattered, people are 
too much dependent on agriculture, and agricultural land is limited, 
the real cultivable area is 13 percent of the total reported area, 
number of small and marginal farmers is more than 87 percent. Ag- 
ricultural activities are difficult and non-profitable. Maximum 
labour is involved in agriculture. Among the total population of the 
area (1981 Census) 17.07 lakhs are labourers (35.5 percent) and 
among total labour 70 percent is involved in agricultural activities. 
Basic facilities of loan, marketing and industrial atmosphere are ' 
lacking. Due to landslides and soil erosion, apart from ecological 
and environmental disequilibrium, there are problems of higher 
cost of maintaining the assets." These are the problems and con- 
straints which the hill people of Uttar Pradesh have been experienc- 
ing for a very long time. Uttarkashi and Chamoli two districts of 
ОР. are economically worst. Both districts have been declared as 
“по industry districts' by U.P. Government. 

These conditions are most discouraging for development. Mini- 
mum facilities are essentially required for the development of any 
industry or activity in any region. The Govt. of U.P. felt tourism as 
an alternative for economic development and ecological stability. 
But for the establishment of any industry it is necessary to plan for 
it and foresee the problems on its way. Later on, problems arising 
out of developmental process should not be ignored, but carefully 


CC-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai апа eGangotri 


202 Tourism in Garhwal Himalaya 


tackled. Few problems exist when an industry gets started, but 
gradually the problems germinate with the development of the in- 
dustry. These problems are the after-effects of that particular-type 
of developmental activity. Examination of the obstacles on the way 
to the development of tourist industry, as well as the problems 
sprouting out due to tourist industry’s development must be made. 

The old theory, “more the toil, higher the religious attainment 
and salvation”, of pilgrimage no longer holds good in general. 
People demand comforts during their pilgrimage. Today even the 
devout pilgrims also evince interest in basic facilities, not to speak 
of the tourists. It can be said that lack of infrastructural facilities 
and government hesitation to accord the required priority that tour- 
ism deserves, are the most serious problems for the development of 
tourist industry. Michael Peter's opinion is worth quoting, “Тош- 
ism, which only a few years ago was the leisurely pastime of the 
idle rich, has grown at such a furious rate that many (or, indeed, 
most) host countries are hopelessly ill-equipped to receive the vast 
number of tourists wishing to visit their country.” In spite of natu- 
ral attractions poor quality of desired facilities discourages not only 
tourism but any other developmental activity. 

Various problems associated with adventure tourism develop- 
ment are briefly discussed. 


6.2 Poor TRANSPORT AND RoAD-SIDE AMENITIES 


Roads comprise the only transport system for men and material 
in Garhwal. Hill driving and difficult roads discourage the tourists 
who are short of time. Here road transport is not very congenial to 
the requirements of well-off tourists. Even today the condition of 
roads is in a very critical state. Landslides are common. Slight rain 
can hang the tourist on the way for hours without water, food or 
toilets etc. Sights of crawling bulldozers, sliding boulders and soil 
make the tourist tired and frightened. Roads are narrow and risky, 
therefore night driving is prohibited in Garhwal. This again leads to 
the loss of precious time. These difficulties of journey upset the 
tourists very much. Journey in Garhwal is time consuming, tiring 
and sickening. This is not only for the tourists but for the hardy 
local people also. 


СС-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


Problems Associated with Moutaineering and Trekking 203 


Booking of tickets is most difficult today. Арап from GMVN 
and other chartered buses, all buses of TGMO, GMO and roadways 
are over-crowded during summers. This makes the joumey more 
difficult. Sometimes passengers are twice the number of the seats, 
sights of passengers on the roof tops of the buses are not uncom- 
mon. It can be said that the road transport is in bad shape even 
today. The governmental agencies always claim that in hill areas of 
О.Р. during different plans, road development has been given maxi- 
mum priority.’ Bad roads and over-crowded buses discourage the 
tourists from visiting the region directly and indirectly. A tourist is 
directly obstructed if roads and buses are bad, but obstruction in the 
supply of goods (required by the tourist) also discourages the tour- 
ist. This tells the sad tale of bad roads and risky driving іп Garhwal. 
Apart from general transport system the facilities for transport in 
adventure tourism too are not good and sometimes they are non- 
existent. Some treks are publicised but not maintained and marked 
properly. It is easy for one to get lost in the trek. This hampers the 
solo trekking. Such treks are Kedartal, Kushkalyani, Vasukital, 
Gandhisarover etc. In some tracks distances are not marked, and 
wherever marked it is always in Hindi. This becomes a problem for 
foreigners to read and understand. 

Pony trekking facilities are existing only in few tracks like 
Yamunotri, Gaumukh, Kedamath, Valley of Flowers and Hem- 
kund-Lokpal. Lack of pony trekking facilities, in other areas is also 
problem for those who want to come close to the nature but don't 
want to exert. There are no ropeways in entire region, therefore a 
class of tourist is not attracted here. Although Vayudoot service has 
been started for aerial viewing, however air services for the region 
are yet to be introduced. Due to this a section of tourists is not 
being attracted. 

Road-side amenities, most essential for tourism development, 
are in worst shape. Toilets, drinking water, restaurants, eating 
houses, automobile repair centres, petrol pumps, first-aid centres 
and hospitals are in bad shape. Eating places are mostly un- 
hygienic. Toilets made out of hanging gunny bags present ugly 
sights. Spreading urine and stinking space around the toilets pre- 
sent a deplorable sight. Though Sulabh Sauchalaya for the hills is 
a good idea of Government of U.P., but the scheme is yet to be 


СС-0. Іп Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


204 Tourism іп Garhwal Himalaya 


implemented extensively. For adventure tourist, away from the 
road-heads, in the name of way-side amenities except Chattis, 
Dharamshalas and Dak Bungalows nothing exists. Along most 
of the treks even the tea shops are not there. А tourist who comes 
with a travel agency enjoys the trek and the facilities provided by 
the travel agency but a tourist who comes here assuming that he 
will get every thing in the trek gets stuck-up without food in the 
belly. 


6.3 LAcK ОЕ ACCOMMODATION FACILITIES 


Like good roads, accommodation too is equally important for 
the development of tourism. Unless a tourist/pilgrim is sure that 
after a whole day's tiring journey he is getting accommodation 
for proper rest, he will certainly not like to go ahead to higher 
altitudes. Though most of the religious places possess enough ac- 
commodation but adventure tourism destinations lack accommoda- 
tion at many places (especially at higher altitude treks). On 
many occasions at Gangotri, people are seen sleeping outside 
under the sky. The first problem is lack of accommodation and the 
second is bad accommodation. Poor house-keeping, dirty rooms, 
dusty floors, shabby bed sheets, broken window pans, unhooked 
curtains, dirty toilets, scarcity of running water in the toilets, un- 
attentive room boys and poor room services are very common in 
private and government accommodation. This all creates inconven- 
ience for the tourists who are paying for every thing, from good 
accommodation to good service. Back home the tourists publicise 
negative as well as positive experiences among prospective tourists 
of future. 

Presently insufficient and substandard adventure tourism ac- 
commodation exists in Har-ki-Doon. Ruinsaratal has good camp 
ground but no camping facilities, at Bhojbasa, facilities are always 
Short of requirement and Tapovan has nothing to offer in the name 
of camping facilities. Lack of accommodation is the problem of 
Tungnath and Rudranath. Kushkalyani trek has neither tourist lodge 
nor dharamshala. In this trek the only accommodation available is 
dirty and stinky huts of the villagers and their cattle. 


СС-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


ESSE 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


Problems Associated with Moutaineering and Trekking 205 
6.4 Lack оғ Funds AND AWARENESS 


Though it is widely publicised nationally and regionally that 
adventure tourism, specially Himalayan tourism will be given more 
impetus but verbal jugglery is not going to achieve the objective, 
The funds to develop this industry are lacking. Good equipments 
for mountaineering and trekking are very expensive and not easily 
available in India. Therefore it becomes difficult for local unem- 
ployed poor youth to avail the facility of partial subsidised loans of 
Government of U.P. 

Local people are not aware of region's tourism resource poten- 
tial and methods to exploit them. They often curse the rugged 
mountainous surroundings and do not understand that the same sur- 
roundings with little awareness and development can be the source 
for their livelihood and comfort. 


6.5 LACK OF INFORMATION AND TELECOMMUNICATION FACILITIES 


Even today, all the information regarding treks, trails, climbing 
equipment, camping kit, porters and other things can not be gath- 
ered at one point. Therefore appropriate and adequate information 
can not be provided to the tourists. The travel agents, accommoda- 
tion personnel and others who come in contact with the tourists are 
not properly trained to explain the region’s attractions, routes, 
country culture, history, geography and archaeology. Such under- 
developed structure of tourism is common in entire Study area. 
Some times much publicised availability of equipment turns into 
unavailability or shortage of stock position. Though it is easy and 
simple for the organisers to offer excuses but it is most painful for 
the tourists. The facilities of telephone and telegraph is very poor in 
the region. Radio communication is not yet introduced in a big 
way. Though GMVN tourist bungalows are having wireless sets for 
their use, but it is looking after a very small percent of the tourists 
visiting Garhwal. Lack of radio communication creates great risk to 
life at the time of accidents on the mountains. Messenger takes 
days to reach to the road-head and the man in danger may die 
within hours. Poor telecommunication facilities discourage the flow 
of adventure tourist to the region. Telex and fax are nowhere in the 
Scenario of the region. 


CC-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


206 Tourism in Garhwal Himalaya 
6.6 BuREAUCRATIC DELAYS 


Bureaucratic system is also one of the problems for tourism 
development. For the foreigner the delay and harassment begins 
from the airport. They are of the opinion that conditions at inter- 
national airports in India are discouraging for tourism development. 
“Post-monsoon, an Indo-Japanese party failed to repeat the Ameri- 
can route, originally interested in Nanda Devi east, from the north, 
they were so delayed by Bombay customs that they tried this in- 
stead. Every expedition (bigger or small) seems to have its tales of 
woe about Indian customs. As the country is supposedly trying to 
attract climbers and trekkers, this constant delay and harassment is 
hard to understand.” such acts of Custom Department force the 
country to lose foreign exchange without any logic and discourage 
tourist trade. Getting clearance from Ministry of Defence, Govern- 
ment of India, Ministry of Home, Government of U.P., to enter the 
inner-line area is another problem for domestic as well as foreign 
tourists. The Jadh Ganga valley, area beyond Mana village in 
Alaknanda valley and Kalindikhal trek comes under restricted area. 
Seeking permission becomes an expedition in itself and it discour- 
ages the flow of tourist. 


6.7 RESEARCH LAG AND PLANNING PROBLEMS 


Another major lacuna observed in the field of Himalaya tour- 
ism in Garhwal, in a greater degree, is the dearth of statistics and 
lack of research base that makes scientific analysis next to impos- 
sible. Prior to the introduction of Himalayan tourism in the region 
no research has been done for identifying the sensitive seismic 
zones, avalanche-prone areas and economic and ecological viabil- 
ity of tourism. No research and planning has been done for alterna- 
tive fuel, energy and garbage disposal systems. 


6.8 SEARCH AND RESCUE 
There is no official search and rescue system in the region. 


Though Himalayan tourism has been introduced and expeditions 
are allowed for Himalayan peaks, no provisions are made to look

after the accidents on the mountains. One who comes for mountain 
climbing or trekking knows that any mishap can occur in the moun- 
tain and simultaneously knows that no arrangements are there for 
search and rescue. If NIM people go and bring the casualty down 
there is no provision to airlift the casualty. The lack of search and 
rescue discourages the tourist flow of the region. 


6.9 OTHER PROBLEMS 


There are few things very essential for smooth and comfortable 
living of the host and guest both. Such things are drinking water, 
electricity, banking facilities and medical facilities. Weak links in 
the chain of services and facilities adversely affect the progress of 
otherwise healthy sectors of the industry. Tourist/pilgrim season 
begins here from the month of May and simultaneously the prob- 
lem of drinking water and electricity start together. This not only 
creates inconvenience to the guest but to the host also and due to 
this, tourist industry gets affected adversely. Bank branches in 
Garhwal are not doing foreign exchange transaction. This some 
times becomes troublesome for the foreign tourists. Hospitals at 
most of the places do not have enough facilities even for the hosts, 
therefore it becomes difficult to accommodate the needy tourists. 
Hospitals are not equipped for high-altitude sickness, etc. 

These are the general problems which are inhibiting the devel- 
opment of Garhwal in general and tourism in particular. Apart from 
hosts, pilgrims and sight-seers, there are adventure tourists (about 
whom the tourism planners, programmers and implementors talk 
too much today) for whom nothing alluring has been done. 

These tourists require few facilities explicitly, like special 
equipment, clothing, food, area maps, cooks, helpers, camp site 
facilities etc. But these are utterly lacking at certain places if not 
every where. 

Clothing for high altitude trekking and mountain climbing are 
different from the usual ones. Tourism organisers іп Garhwal even 
today are not able to provide gualitatively and guantitatively the 
clothing reguired by adventure tourists. Similarly food for higher 
altitudes is not available in the local market. This becomes an acute 
problem for foreigners who are not used to relish our traditional 


CC-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


208 Tourism in Garhwal Himalaya 


dishes. Food problem becomes more acute in case of trekkers, who 
suddenly plan for trekking in Garhwal or who come with an as- 
sumption that every thing will be available in the local market. 
Unavailability of food and clothing not only embarrasses the host 
but also spoils the trip and leisure of the tourists. 

The next impediment in the way to the development of adven- 
ture tourism in Garhwal is the non-availability of area maps as both 
the districts are on the Indo-Tibetan International Border. There- 
fore the government has declared the higher reaches of both the 
districts as restricted areas and the general tourists cannot buy these 
area maps in India, though most of the foreign tourists are in pos- 
session of detailed maps of such areas. However, these maps are 
restricted for Indians. A lot of inconvenience is felt by all Indian 
and few foreign tourists without maps. 

Declaring areas as restricted also hampers the tourist from 
touring the area. Jadh Ganga valley, with uninhabited and aban- 
doned villages can attract the solitude-loving tourists, but the area 
is prohibited for the general public (local people and tourists alike). 
Similarly villages called Bampa, Gamsali and Niti etc are closed 
for tourists for the same reasons, though we know that powerful 
satellites are giving every detail to the people thousands of kms 
away from here. Such undulating meadows, virgin forests, valleys 
and peaks are waiting for the eyes who can enjoy their beauty. 

Declaring areas of immense natural beauty as restricted areas 
for human beings in the name of eco-conservation is also one of the 
problems of tourism development in Garhwal. Nanda Devi sanctu- 
ary was declared as national park and biosphere reserve for the 
sake of ecological conservation. This stopped the mountaineering 
and trekking expeditions to this area resulting in jeopardizing the 
economy of Lata and Reni villages and it does not appear to be 
helping the conservational idea. Such closures are leading to 
poaching menace rather than conservation. Conservation must be a 
priority in the area, but it must be done in such an effective way 
that it does not harm tourism and local people. 


6.10 PRoBLEMs Due To HAPHAZARD Tourism DEVELOPMENT 


Himalayan tourism was much publicised, but not fairly and 


СС-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


Problems Associated with Moutaineering and Trekking 209 


judiciously worked out from the very day of its inception. For start- 
ing any industry or trade, these things are supposed to be well 
weighed, but the sorry state of adventure tourism in Garhwal is that 
planners and policy makers never bothered about all this before 
ecological catastrophe started showing symptoms. These problems 
are the outcome of unscientific and unfriendly tourism develop- 
ment. They are not only the result of unplanned tourism of today 
and yesterday, but are becoming problems for the future develop- 
ment of tourism in Garhwal. Mainly they are: 


1. Scarcity, resulting in price rise in commodities and service 
sector. 

Hike in land prices. 

Cultural pollution and loss of old traditions. 

Inadequate facilities (drinking water, milk, electricity and 
sewage system). 

5. Problem of housing and higher rent. 

6. Heavily crowded, narrow streets. 

7. Serious parking problem. 
8 
9 


>ш 


. Unplanned urbanisation of tourist destinations. 
. Chronic problem of weak municipal administration with 
inadeguate budgets for providing the services. 
10. Loss of regional aesthetics and ecological destruction. 
11. Creation of low jobs for local residents and better jobs for 
outsiders. 
12. Seasonal dependence etc. 


Tourism being a seasonal activity affected the general demand 
and supply pattern of the region. Due to flood of pilgrims and 
tourists in summers, demand side increases but accordingly supply 
never gets adjusted. This results in scarcity of commodities and 
services and hike in prices. This is drastically felt by the local 
people who are economically quite backward and financially un- 
sound. Handful of businessmen and entrepreneurs are taking all ad- 
vantages of pilgrimage and tourism and the majority is getting frus- 
trated and alienated from the mainstream of the economy. This is 
resulting in unbalanced economic development. Society is getting 
divided into two masses of poor and a handful of rich. This is hap- 
pening in Uttarkashi, Gangotri, Joshimath, Badrinath and Kedar- 


CC-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


210 Tourism in Garhwal Himalaya 


nath. Being seasonal the infrastructure remains idle for six months. 

To grab the land of potential tourism destinations, outside busi- 
nessmen are giving high prices, resulting in soaring high land 
prices, inflation and utter frustration among local entrepreneurs, 
who are enthusiastic for work but handicapped with their poverty. 
In particular, problems arise and costs are increased when the 
different sectors of the industry do not develop harmoniously or 
when the motives and capabilities of the different development 
agents conflict. Carried to an extreme, uncontrolled growth of tour- 
ism can irrevocably destroy the very resource base on which it was 
built. 

This haphazard tourism development is creating a conflict be- 
tween the needs of tourists and the interests of the resident popula- 
tion. The larger the influx of tourist, greater is the risk of inconven- 
ience and reduction in amenities and facilities to the local people. 
At many of the tourist destinations in Garhwal, during tourist sea- 
son there is scarcity of milk, accommodation (higher rents too), 
electricity, vegetables and unmanageable garbage heaps, inade- 
quate sewage, weak municipal administration, and inadequate 
funds, shortage of medical facilities and growing delinquency etc. 
These are some of the problems cropping up due to haphazard 
tourism development. Small and congested parking areas and con- 
crete towering buildings are the results of tourism, which are mer- 
cilessly killing mountain aesthetics. Fuel requirements, building 
timber and unplanned human (tourists and hosts) intervention in re- 
mote areas is resulting in deforestation, drying of water sources, 
shifting of glaciers, landslides, disturbed flora-fauna, and in all it is 
leading towards resourcelessness in Himalayan tourism. One of the 
greatest problems is the fact that government, leaders, economists, 
planners who influence government policy are not aware as to how 
little they know about tourism and local circumstances. 

Tourism should not involve the wanton destruction of the very 
basis of existence of the local population. In fact tourism should be 
planned in this manner so that it can help the local people in having 
more employment avenues, and income generation so that they 
(local people) could come out of age-old cobweb of poverty and 
Superstition and work for the development of tourist industry.

6.11 Conclusion 


For the development of any industry few things are essentially 
required but simultaneously few things exist іп the way that they 
become the obstacle. In Uttarkashi and Chamoli districts of 
Garhwal insufficient and substandard transport, accommodation, 
toad-side amenities, lack of funds, poor telecommunication facili- 
ties and bureaucratic delays are hampering the tourism. At the same 
time due to poor planning and haphazard development few prob- 
lems like inflation, pollution, shortage of facilities to local people, 
over-crowding, unplanned urbanisation, seasonal dependency and 
ecological disturbances are cropping up in the region.

7 


Conclusion and Recommendations 


7.1 CONCLUSION 


In the beginning of the study this was hypothesized that Hima- 
layan tourism is the only alternative for the balanced and sustain- 
able development of the region. After collecting information and 
details about the tourist attractions, infrastructure, present day tour- 
ism development and its impact, following conclusions have been 
made. 

Uttarkashi and Chamoli districts, due to the difficult topogra- 
phy, deficient mineral resources and fragile ecosystem are not suit- 
able for agriculture and heavy industries. Mining is neither eco- 
nomically sound nor ecologically viable. Commercial forestry is 
not advisable. So the only thing left with the region is Himalayan 
tourism and pilgrimage. 

This region is very rich for religious and adventure tourism. 
Apart from Panch Badris, Sapt Kedars, Gangotri, Yamunotri and 

_ the confluences of holy rivers, every town, river, rivulet, mountain 
valley and snow-covered peak is associated with one or the other 
God or Goddess. Pilgrimage has got bright prospects in the region. 
Simultaneously the scope for adventure tourism is not narrow. 
Long mountain chains, snow-covered peaks, open valleys, frothy- 
foamy rjvers, lakes, varied flora-fauna and wide pasture lands are 
the great attraction of the region. Apart from identified and pub- 
licised attractions, there are many things yet to be identified and 
publicised. Rich potentials for water-skiing, snow-skiing, hale- 
skiing, angling, wildlife watching, rafting, canoeing and rock 


СС-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


Conclusion апа Recommendations 243 


climbing etc are existing. With proper research, planning and re- 
source management these potentials can be converted into finest 
mountain tourism attractions. 

Infrastructure of the region is gradually improving, though it 
could not reach up to the required standard. Roads are improving, 
extension of roads are under process. Non-existence of air and 
water transport and ropeways are obstructing the flow of tourist in 
the region. An urgent necessity of air transport and ropeways is 
felt, not only for the transportation of tourists but also for casualty 
evacuation. Accommodation at road-sides is coming up, but the 
house-keeping is not upto the standard. Tourists prefer GMVN 
bungalows and pure pilgrims prefer dharamshalas. Road-side 
amenities are substandard. But beyond road-heads, facilities are 
poor in few places and non-existent at many places. Uttarkashi and 
Chamoli districts are attracting maximum number of mountaineer- 
ing expeditions. Therefore proper base camps are required at 
Tapovan and Nandanvan. The region lacks funds for adventure 
tourism development. Though local people are interested to explore 
regional adventure tourism resource potential, but lack of funds is 
not allowing them to materialise their plans. There is nothing in the 
name of entertainment and recreation. Travel agencies and porter 
agencies have scope to grow. For publicity Government of О.Р. has 
made efforts but much more is required. To give boost to the tourist 
industry and regional economy local handicraft, small and cottage 
industries have to be developed. There is scope for woollen cloths, 
wood carving and bamboo work. There is scarcity of drinking 
water, electricity, banking and medical facilities etc. Absolute 
absence of telecommunication (specially radio, wireless, telex and 
fax etc) is crippling the tourist industry. 

Himalayan tourism is gaining the momentum with pilgrimage. 
Economic activities are getting generated. Number of tourists/ 
pilgrims is increasing. Though there is fluctuation in tourist arrivals 
in different years, but coefficient of variation is less than thirty per- 
cent for Badri, Kedar, Gangotri, Yamunotri and increasing long 
term trend in tourist arrivals is confirmed. Increasing income of 
Yatra office and Mountaineering Division of GMVN is also a clear 
indication of growing Himalayan tourism and its positive impacts. 

Travel agencies are multiplying. Their activities are increasing. 


СС-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


274 Tourism in Garhwal Himalaya 


Local people are getting jobs. But few negative aspects of this in- 
dustry too are cropping up. These negative aspects are not the 
result of over-use of the resources but misuse and mismanagement 
of the resources, wrong tourism planning, lack of collective think- 
ing and collective action. Proper thinking, planning and action 
would provide sustainable development of man and nature in the 
region. Prior to the take-off of this industry the so-called environ- 
mentalists have started creating noise of environmental distur- 
bances and disasters. This resulted in the closure of Nanda Devi 
national park and the destruction of local economy which was 
based on adventure tourism. Simultaneously this environmentalist 
group is raising voice for the closure of Gangotri-Gaumukh region 
also. They have a misconception that ecological balance in Hima- 
layas can be maintained only by shutting out human being. Nanda 
Devi, Valley of Flowers national parks and Gangotri region have 
reached to the saturation point of their carrying capacity. But in this 
study this has been concluded that carrying capacity is the function 
more of user’s behaviour (operators and tourists) than the number. 
In fact in this region isolated tourism planning (tourism for the sake 
of tourism) has been done and this approach is creating the prob- 
lems. Strict rules, regulations and their implementation can cer- 
tainly check the vandalism on the mountains. 

Now, it seems that in the name of eco-conservation any thing 
like snatching of employment, income and development will not be 
tolerated by the local people. They are deadly against this type of 
conservation. Recent action of Uttarakhand Kranti Dal can not be 
forgotten. The activists of this party had cut all the trees which 
were hindrance to the construction of roads and bridges of Uttara- 
khand. Conservation should be done by the people and for the 
people. In the higher reaches of Uttarkashi and Chamoli districts, 
the problem is not of exhausted carrying capacity but misuse and 
mismanagement of Himalayan tourism resources. Sensible Hima- 
layan tourism planning and development can remove the evils of 
present day tourism and will lead the region towards sustainable 
development. 

Based on the study of this book following recommendations 
are proposed. 


СС-0. In Public Domain. Gurukul Kangri Collection, Haridwar 


Digitized by Arya Samaj Foundation Chennai and eGangotri 


Conclusion and Recommendations 215 


7.2 RECOMMENDATIONS 


1. 


14. 


Coordination amongst various agencies such as Forest De- 
partment, PWD, tourism, hydel and regional administration 
etc is essential for speedy and healthy development of 
adventure tourism. 


. Regional aesthetics and environmental balance should be 


insured before any developmental planning takes place. 


. Participation of local people in adventure tourism develop- 


ment should be encouraged. 

Proper information and data should be established at 
various points such as Uttarkashi, Barkot, Joshimath and 
Gopeshwar. 

Efforts must be made to ensure that adventure tourism is a 
round the year activity. 

To promote adventure tourism participation of NRI, pri- 
vate entrepreneurs and other funding agencies should be 
approached and encouraged. 


. Cottages and huts of villagers along the tracks should be 


slightly modernised to convert them into vacation houses 
or second homes. 


. Active and vigilant conservation acts and programmes 


should be identified and implemented. 

To prevent the deforestation solar and hydel power 
potential should be explored, afforestation programmes 
and supply of LPG and kerosene oil should be en- 
couraged. 


. Foreign exchange facility should be provided specifically 


at places like Dehradun, Uttarkashi and Joshimath. 


. Some of the hospitals like in Uttarkashi and Joshimath 


should be equipped for the treatment of high altitude sick- 
ness and diseases. 


. Modemised rescue operation cells should be established. 
. Road-side amenities such as petrol-pumps, automobile 


repair centres, hospitals, hotels, toilets (Sulabh Saucha- 
layas) should be provided/improved and trekking facilities 
away from road-heads should be provided. 

To sustain multiplier effect and to prevent the leakage of

income, inputs of adventure tourism should be made avail- 
able locally. 


. Permission to mountaineering expeditions should be 


granted from a single window. 


. Rope ways and chair lifts should be introduced. 
. Dried up lakes like Ghona and Dharali should be restored 


to their past glories. 


. Hot water springs of Gaurikund, Tapovan and Yamunotri 


should be converted into spas. 


. Wildlife tourism should be developed. 
. Nanda Devi region should be reopened. Jadh Ganga val- 


ley, Kalindikhal, area beyond Mana and Malari should be 
opened for adventure tourism. 

Local industries like wool work, medicinal herbs, honey, 
mushroom cultivation, fisheries and fruit preservation and 
processing should be properly organised and expanded. 
Cultivation of local cash crops such as potato, amaranthus, 
soya-bean etc and horticulture like apple, citrus fruits, wal- 
nuts etc should be encouraged and promoted. 

Effective measures should be taken to prevent soil erosion 
and landslides. 

Adventure tourism potentials and infrastructure of the re- 
gion should be рүн апа promoted through electronic 
media. 

Appropriate pidió transmitting station should be estab- 
lished to give information about weather forecasting, 
search and rescue operations. 

Negative economic, social and environmental effects of 
adventure tourism should be given special attention. 

Flora, fauna and pasture lands should be protected and 
properly used for adventure tourism. 

For the interaction of guest and host local fairs and festi- 
vals, yoga, meditation and other spiritual activities should 
be organised. 

Air transport should be re-introduced from Gaucher and 
possibilities should be explored in Uttarkashi district 
(Chinyali saur). 

The region being located in seismically sensitive zone

7.3 SCOPE FoR FUTURE Work 


1. 


Although exhaustive efforts were made to collect the tour- 
ist expenditure data and the rate of leakage but the data 
could not be obtained. An attempt must be made to collect 
the exact tourist expenditure estimates, number of transac- 
tions and leakage for the computation of multiplier factor. 


. Environmental impact of adventure tourism on the region 


and its carrying capacity should be studied. 


. A computerised data base information system should be 


prepared for the entire region. 


. The impact of adventure tourism on the socio-cultural life 


and demographic pattern of the region should be studied. 


. Cost-benefit analysis, employment multiplier and inflation- 


ary impact of adventure tourism can be studied and a 
sound tourism development model can be worked out. 


. On the basis of tourist profile studied in this work more 


broad-based study can be made for Kumaon and other hill 
regions of the country. 


. Integrated tourism model for general tourism, pilgrimage 


and adventure tourism can be developed for the overall 
development of the region.

Dr. (Miss) Harshvanti Bisht, India’s leading 
woman mountaineer, has been actively 
` associated with mountaineering and Hima- 
. layan Tourism research work for the past 
two decades. She has climbed Baby 
|Б Shivling (5660 m), Kala Мар (6387 ш), 
а Трем (6002 m), Gangotri (6672 m) and 
| Nanda Devi (7817 m) in Garhwal Hima- 
| + aya. She has participated in the Indian 
| Everest Expedition of 1984. For her excep- 
| tional mountaineering skills and outstand- 
| ing achievements, Dr. Harshvanti Bisht was 
awarded the Arjuna Award and Directorate 
“ of Higher Education, Government of О.Р. 
. Gold Medal. In mountaineering circles, she 

| is widely known as Harsha. 
| She is a Fellow of the Royal Geo- 
graphical Society, and member of several 
mountaineering and environment conserva- 
tion societies. She has many research 
papers and radio talks to her credit. She 
has worked as Principal Investigator to a 
major research project, “Growth of Hima- 
layan Tourism and Pilgrimage in Ganpotri 
Region: An Impact on Physical Resources 
and Economy," supported by Ministry of 
Environment & Forests, Deptt. of Environ- 
ment, Forests &,Wildlife, Govt. of India, 

New Delhi. 

Cürrently she is teaching Economics in 
Govt. Postgraduate College, Uttarkashi and 
actively working for the eco-conservation 
of Gangotri-Gaumukh area (source of river 
Ganga) in Garhwal Himalaya.
